Fundamentals / Mechanical engineering / Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

Apply energy, entropy and exergy balances to steam cycles, heat exchangers and enclosures, and derive how heat transfer sets operating temperatures.

Read the subject in depth

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Thermal processes and transport in engineering

    Close energy and entropy balances around real processes. Use measured states and heat flows to separate component performance from modelling assumptions.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Properties, equilibrium and phase change

    Predict the sign of a response from a potential, then track what a measured fraction or coexistence curve says about the state of the material.

    Separate sensible heating from transported or radiated energy. Test the temperature signal against a field loss measurement and a conservative heat discretisation.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Choose first a closed system, then a flowing control volume. Energy balances determine work and heat, entropy restricts their conversion, and returning to the initial state creates a cycle whose performance can be measured.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    A thermal resistance sets a steady heat rate. Adding storage makes temperatures time-dependent. Phase change and moist air then require tracking composition as well as sensible energy.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Use a reference environment to measure available work. Follow water around a steam cycle, transfer energy between two moving streams, and close the course with radiative exchange between surfaces.

Chapter 1 · State, process and the first law for a closed system

Distinguish properties from path-dependent transfers and determine heat and work for a piston-cylinder process.

From first principles

Obtain closed-system work from the motion of a boundary

Choose a fixed mass of gas enclosed by a piston and cylinder. No mass crosses this boundary. The first law is the empirical balance of energy: with heat positive into the gas and work positive out, change of internal, kinetic and potential energy equals net heat minus net work. For a piston moving normally through \(dx\) against a uniform resisting pressure \(p_{\rm ext}\), force times displacement gives

\[\delta W_b=p_{\rm ext}A\,dx=p_{\rm ext}\,dV,\]
\[\Delta(U+KE+PE)=Q-W .\]

For slow internal equilibration with tiny pressure differences, one can use gas pressure in the integral. If bulk kinetic and potential changes are negligible and no shaft or electrical work crosses the boundary, the integrated heating requirement is

\[Q_{12}=U_2-U_1+\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p_{\rm ext}\,dV .\]

The area under the specified pressure–volume path is therefore part of the heat calculation. For a rigid vessel it vanishes. For a constant-pressure piston heating an ideal gas, \(p\Delta V=mR\Delta T\) adds to the internal-energy change. The end temperatures alone determine the ideal-gas internal-energy change, while the moving boundary determines the work.

Derivation and applications

Determine the heat input when a spring loads the piston

A freely sliding piston does not necessarily hold the gas pressure constant. Attach a linear spring to a frictionless piston of area \(A\) and let its resisting force increase as the gas expands. Consider a closed mass \(m\) of ideal gas, heated slowly enough that piston force balance holds at each intermediate state. Neglect piston kinetic energy, heat leakage and changes of elevation except as already included in the constant external pressure \(p_1\). At the initial position the gas is in equilibrium at absolute pressure \(p_1\) and volume \(V_1\).

The displacement \(x\) produces a volume increment \(Ax\) and an extra resisting force \(kx\). Dividing by piston area gives the pressure as a function of the evolving volume:

\[V-V_1=Ax,\]
\[p(V)=p_1+\frac{k}{A^2}(V-V_1).\]

Boundary work is performed at this changing external pressure. Integrating the straight-line path over the swept volume \(\Delta V=V_2-V_1\) separates work against the initial pressure from work stored in the spring:

\[W_{12}=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p(V)\,dV=p_1\Delta V+\frac{k}{2A^2}(\Delta V)^2.\]

For constant specific heat \(c_v\) and specific gas constant \(R\), the first law gives the heat required, positive into the gas. State temperatures follow from the ideal-gas relation, including the final spring pressure:

\[Q_{12}=m c_v(T_2-T_1)+W_{12},\]
\[T_i=p_iV_i/(mR).\]

Only the temperature change determines the internal-energy change; the mechanical law selects the work term. Setting \(k=0\) recovers expansion against constant pressure. Abrupt heating that accelerates the piston would invalidate the assumed quasistatic pressure path.

Worked example

Calculate the heater input for an air cylinder

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Take \(m=0.0100\) kg, \(V_1=0.0100\) m³, \(p_1=100\) kPa, \(A=0.0100\) m² and \(k=1000\) N/m. Increase volume by \(0.00200\) m³. The final pressure is \(120\) kPa; the work is

\[(100+120)(0.00200)/2=0.220\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

For \(R=0.287\) and \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K), \(\Delta T=(1.440-1.000)/(0.0100\times0.287)=153\) K. The gas receives \(1.10+0.220=1.32\) kJ of heat.

Second application

Separate actuator work from stored spring energy

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

On the same stroke the piston travels \(0.200\) m. The initially unloaded incremental spring stores \(kx^2/2=20.0\) J, exactly the triangular part of the pressure–volume work. The constant load receives \(200\) J. If the spring were removed while retaining the same initial state and swept volume, the final pressure would stay at \(100\) kPa and the temperature rise would be only \(69.7\) K. The two processes end at different thermodynamic states despite having the same final volume.

Try it · Spring piston

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Decide what crosses the boundary

Draw a boundary around the gas in a piston cylinder. If no gas crosses its surface, the system is closed even though the piston moves. A rigid vessel is a special closed system with fixed volume. An insulated wall prevents heat transfer; a shaft or electrical lead can still transmit work through it. These descriptions impose different constraints and should be identified before choosing an equation.

At equilibrium a simple compressible substance can be described using independent state variables such as temperature and specific volume. Pressure \(p\), volume \(V\), temperature \(T\) and internal energy \(U\) are properties. An equation of state relates some of them. For a dilute ideal gas \(pV=mRT\), where \(R\) is the specific gas constant. Always use absolute pressure and absolute temperature in this relation. Two initial and final states determine changes in properties, but do not specify the heat and work transfers between them.

Construct the process energy balance

Take work done by the system as positive and heat entering it as positive. The first law is

\[\Delta U+\Delta KE+\Delta PE=Q-W.\]

For a stationary vessel ignore changes in kinetic and gravitational energy of the system as a whole. A moving piston of area \(A\) against external pressure \(p_{\rm ext}\) performs incremental work \(p_{\rm ext}A\,dx=p_{\rm ext}dV\). For a quasistatic mechanically reversible motion the boundary pressure can be represented by the gas pressure throughout the process.

If gas expands from \(V_1\) to \(V_2\) at constant external pressure,

\[W=p_{\rm ext}(V_2-V_1).\]

If it instead follows a reversible isothermal ideal-gas path, inserting \(p=mRT/V\) and integrating gives

\[W=mRT\ln(V_2/V_1).\]

Both expressions have units of energy. Their different volume dependence makes a pressure-volume diagram useful: the area under each pressure path has a direct work interpretation when its boundary-force assumptions hold.

Background · Which pressure enters the boundary-work integral?

The traction at a moving boundary determines the mechanical work across it. For a piston use the pressure exerted against the load. Replacing it by a uniform equilibrium gas pressure additionally assumes slow mechanical evolution. A limiting comparison makes this distinction measurable.

\[W_{\mathrm{by}}=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p_{\mathrm{ext}}\,dV.\]

Remove a partition in an insulated rigid vessel so that an ideal gas expands into a vacuum. The external pressure against the advancing gas is zero. There is no heat transfer or boundary work, so the gas returns to the same equilibrium temperature. A reversible isothermal expansion between those same gas volumes requires heat from a bath and yields positive work \(nRT\ln(V_2/V_1)\). The two endpoint states alone do not define a work measurement.

Heat air below a freely moving piston

A weight-loaded, frictionless piston keeps 0.50 kg of ideal air at 150 kPa absolute as a heater raises its temperature from 300 to 360 K. Use constant properties \(R=0.287\) and \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K). The ideal-gas law gives the volumes at the two ends of the process.

\[\begin{aligned} V_1&=\frac{mRT_1}{p}=0.2870~\mathrm{m^3},\\ V_2&=\frac{mRT_2}{p}=0.3444~\mathrm{m^3}. \end{aligned}\]

The pressure is constant, so the work is the area of a rectangle on a pressure–volume diagram. Internal energy depends on the gas temperature. These are separate contributions to the heat input.

\[\begin{aligned} W&=p(V_2-V_1)=150(0.0574)~\mathrm{kJ}=8.61~\mathrm{kJ},\\ \Delta U&=mc_v(T_2-T_1)=0.50(0.718)(60)~\mathrm{kJ}\\ &=21.54~\mathrm{kJ}. \end{aligned}\]

The required heat is

\[Q=\Delta U+W=30.15\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

The same result follows from \(mc_p\Delta T\) because for this constant-pressure ideal-gas process with only boundary work \(c_p=c_v+R=1.005\) kJ/(kg K). The extra heat above the internal-energy increase moved the piston weight. Using \(Q=mc_p\Delta T\) without inspecting the constraint would give the wrong heat input to a rigid tank.

Compare a rigid-wall experiment

Lock the piston at its initial volume before heating an identical mass from 300 to 360 K. Now \(W=0\), so only 21.54 kJ of heat is needed. The final pressure is

\[150(360/300)=180\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

Equal temperature changes in an ideal gas imply equal changes in internal energy, even though final pressures and energy transfers differ. For a real fluid near a phase boundary, constant ideal-gas heat capacities may fail; use tabulated state properties or a suitable equation of state. In either case the first-law accounting is unchanged once the initial and final internal energies and the actual boundary work are known.

A different path between specified volumes

An ideal gas initially at 300 kPa and 0.020 m³ expands quasistatically and isothermally to twice its initial volume. Since \(pV=p_1V_1\), work out is

\[W=p_1V_1\ln(V_2/V_1)=6\ln2=4.16\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

Ideal-gas internal energy depends only on temperature, so \(\Delta U=0\) and heat entering equals 4.16 kJ. A freely expanding ideal gas in an insulated rigid outer vessel can reach the same final temperature and occupied volume while doing zero boundary work. The state variables alone do not record which process occurred.

Determine heat and work along a measured compression path

A measured pressure–volume trace contains more information than its endpoints. For some slow piston processes an empirical polytropic law \(pV^n=\text{constant}\) fits the path. Here \(n\) is a fitted process exponent and need not equal the gas heat-capacity ratio. Assume a closed cylinder with negligible kinetic and potential energy change, a quasistatic pressure load and a calorically perfect ideal gas. Work out of the gas is the area \(W=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p\,dV\), which is negative for compression. Substitute \(p=p_1V_1^n V^{-n}\) and integrate:

\[W=\frac{p_2V_2-p_1V_1}{1-n}\quad(n\ne1),\]
\[W=p_1V_1\ln(V_2/V_1)\quad(n=1).\]

The logarithmic case is the continuous limit as \(n\) approaches one. The ideal-gas equation also gives \(T_2/T_1=(V_1/V_2)^{n-1}\), so \(\Delta U=mc_v(T_2-T_1)\). With \(Q-W=\Delta U\) and \(pV=mRT\), the heat input is \(Q=m[c_v+R/(1-n)](T_2-T_1)\) for \(n\ne1\). Setting \(n=\gamma=c_p/c_v\) eliminates \(Q\). This inference relies on the ideal-gas, constant-property, quasistatic assumptions. An arbitrary irreversible compression with the same endpoints cannot be called isentropic from the fitted exponent alone.

Recover a cylinder heat loss from three measurements

A test traps air at \(p_1=100\,\mathrm{kPa}\) absolute, \(V_1=0.0100\,\mathrm{m^3}\), \(T_1=300\,\mathrm K\), then compresses it to \(V_2=0.0040\,\mathrm{m^3}\). The fitted exponent is 1.25. Using \(R=0.287,\ c_v=0.718\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), the trapped mass is

\[p_1V_1/(RT_1)=0.01161\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

The predicted pressure is

\[100(2.5)^{1.25}=314.4\,\mathrm{kPa},\]
\[T_2=300(2.5)^{0.25}=377.2\,\mathrm K.\]

Note that absolute pressure is required in both the fit and the gas law. A pressure transducer referenced to atmosphere would need its reference restored.

The gas does \(W=[314.4(0.0040)-100(0.0100)]/(1-1.25)=-1.03\,\mathrm{kJ}\). Its internal energy rises by \(0.01161(0.718)(77.2)=0.644\,\mathrm{kJ}\), giving \(Q=\Delta U+W=-0.386\,\mathrm{kJ}\). Compression work enters the gas while part leaves as heat through the cylinder wall. If the process were isothermal at 300 K, the same volume change would require only \(-W=0.916\,\mathrm{kJ}\) and the full amount would leave as heat. A mid-stroke measurement gives a stronger check than the end pressure: the fitted law predicts \(100(0.010/0.007)^{1.25}=156.2\,\mathrm{kPa}\) at 7.0 litres. A substantial discrepancy would favour direct numerical integration of the measured trace over using a single exponent.

An apparent exponent can also be estimated directly from pressure ratios:

\[n=\ln(p_2/p_1)/\ln(V_1/V_2).\]

Compare estimates from the first and second halves of the stroke. Agreement means the same log–log slope describes both halves, while disagreement may indicate changing heat transfer, leakage or phase lag between the pressure and position sensors. It does not identify which of those mechanisms is responsible. As a separate check, suppose a water jacket gains the estimated 0.386 kJ each stroke and the compressor completes ten such strokes per minute. The mean heat removal from these strokes would be \(0.386(10)/60=0.0643\,\mathrm{kW}\). A jacket measurement of the complete machine includes intake and exhaust processes and mechanical friction as well, so experimental comparison requires either an isolated compression interval or a balance around the rest of the cycle.

A change in operating speed can also change the fitted exponent because it changes the time available for heat exchange during a single compression stroke.

Chapter 2 · Open systems, enthalpy and steady-flow devices

Use an inlet-outlet balance to distinguish shaft work, kinetic-energy conversion and pressure reduction.

From first principles

Derive the steady-flow energy balance including flow work

Surround a turbine, nozzle or mixing chamber with a stationary control surface. In addition to transporting its internal, kinetic and gravitational energies, an entering fluid is pushed across the inlet by upstream pressure. A mass increment \(dm\) occupies volume \(v\,dm\) and requires pressure work \(pv\,dm\) to enter. The outlet carries the corresponding work away. Combining internal energy \(u\) and flow work defines enthalpy:

\[h=u+pv,\]
\[e_{\rm stream}=h+V^2/2+gz .\]

Here \(V\) denotes speed, \(v\) specific volume and \(z\) elevation. In steady operation, sum these stream contributions and include heat into and shaft work out of the control volume. For one inlet and one outlet with equal mass rate, rearrangement gives

\[\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\,[h_2-h_1+(V_2^2-V_1^2)/2+g(z_2-z_1)] .\]

All terms have units of power. A nozzle can accelerate flow without shaft work by lowering static enthalpy. A throttle with no heat, no shaft work and negligible speed and elevation changes is instead approximately isenthalpic. Omitting the pressure work while also using internal energy would miss both conclusions.

Background · Specific enthalpy
\[h=u+pv\]

Specific quantities are per unit mass. Enthalpy accounts for internal energy and flow work when mass passes through a control surface.

Derivation and applications

Convert a nozzle energy balance into a mass-flow limit

A steady nozzle converts static enthalpy into bulk kinetic energy. A converging passage has a maximum possible mass flow at a specified inlet stagnation state. Derive this for a steady, one-dimensional, adiabatic ideal gas with constant \(c_p\) and \(\gamma\), reversible acceleration and negligible inlet-reservoir velocity. Write reservoir conditions as \(T_0,p_0\) and the local static conditions as \(T,p\). With no shaft work the energy balance gives

\[c_pT_0=c_pT+u^2/2,\]
\[u=\sqrt{2c_pT_0(1-T/T_0)} .\]

Isentropic acceleration links temperature and pressure. Let \(r=T/T_0\), so \(p/p_0=r^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)}\). The mass flux is \(\rho u\) and ideal-gas density is \(p/(RT)\). Substitution gives a function of just one local variable:

\[G(r)=\frac{p_0}{RT_0}\sqrt{2c_pT_0}\,r^{1/(\gamma-1)}\sqrt{1-r}.\]

To find its maximum differentiate \(\ln G=(\ln r)/(\gamma-1)+\ln(1-r)/2+\text{constant}\). Equating the two derivative terms gives the sonic temperature and pressure ratios:

\[r_*=\frac{2}{\gamma+1},\]
\[p_*/p_0=\left(\frac{2}{\gamma+1}\right)^{\gamma/(\gamma-1)},\]
\[u_*^2=\gamma R T_* .\]

The flow through a converging exit is choked when downstream pressure is low enough for this state to be reached. Further lowering downstream pressure changes the flow after the exit; the ideal mass flux at that exit remains fixed. The model omits wall friction, heat transfer and discharge-coefficient corrections.

Worked example

Size a sonic air metering aperture

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

For \(p_0=200\) kPa absolute, \(T_0=300\) K, \(\gamma=1.40\) and \(R=287\) J/(kg K), the critical state has \(T_*=250\) K and \(p_*=105.7\) kPa. Then \(\rho_*=1.47\) kg/m³ and \(u_*=317\) m/s, giving \(G_*\simeq467\) kg/(m² s). A \(20.0\) mm² effective aperture meters about \(9.33\) g/s if the downstream pressure allows choking.

Second application

Decide whether a higher receiver pressure matters

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Raise the receiver pressure to \(160\) kPa. Its ratio to reservoir pressure is now \(0.800\), above the critical \(0.528\). An ideal converging nozzle discharges at this pressure with \(r=0.800^{(0.4/1.4)}=0.938\). The energy equation gives \(u\approx193\) m/s, static density about \(1.98\) kg/m³, and mass flux about \(382\) kg/(m² s). Through the same aperture the predicted flow falls to \(7.64\) g/s. A model assuming choked flow at both settings would miss this change.

Start from this chapter. Enclose each stream in a steady-flow control volume. Use its inlet and outlet enthalpy to infer heat rate. Check the hot- and cold-side estimates against one another in the numerical run.

OneSimulation laboratory · Explain why greater exchanger effectiveness can give less power

Account for fluid entering and leaving

A closed-system piston can be followed with its gas. A continuously operating turbine has fresh fluid crossing its boundary every second. Enclose the hardware and its internal flow in a stationary control volume. Flowing mass carries internal energy \(u\), kinetic energy \(v^2/2\) and gravitational energy \(gz\) per kilogram. Upstream pressure also does work pushing fluid in, while downstream pressure opposes its exit. Per unit mass this flow work is \(p/\rho\), motivating the specific enthalpy \(h=u+p/\rho\).

Adding all incoming and outgoing energy rates gives, for one inlet, one outlet and constant stored energy,

\[\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\left[h_2-h_1+(v_2^2-v_1^2)/2+g(z_2-z_1)\right].\]

Here \(\dot W_s\) is shaft power out of the control volume. Pressure work at the flowing boundaries is already contained in enthalpy; adding it once more would count it twice. One inlet and outlet have the same steady mass rate even when their volumetric flow rates differ dramatically because gas expands.

Read the dominant energy conversion

In a short, insulated nozzle there is no shaft, the elevation difference is small and falling enthalpy raises velocity. The property \(h+v^2/2\) is then constant, often called stagnation enthalpy. In an insulated turbine with small inlet and outlet speeds, the enthalpy drop instead appears mainly as positive shaft work. An insulated compressor takes work in, raising enthalpy. An insulated throttling valve has neither shaft power nor a large net change of bulk kinetic energy between distant ports, so its inlet and outlet enthalpies are approximately equal. These components can all drop pressure during expansion, yet their energy effects differ.

For ideal-gas air with nearly constant \(c_p\), an enthalpy difference may be approximated by \(c_p\Delta T\). Keeping h in kJ/kg while using \(v^2/2\) in J/kg is a common factor-of-one-thousand error. At 100 m/s the kinetic energy is 5 kJ/kg. That estimate often decides whether an inlet velocity may be omitted from a turbine work calculation.

Evaluate heat loss and exhaust speed

A turbine passes 2.0 kg/s of gas from \(h_1=820\) to \(h_2=600\) kJ/kg. Inlet speed is 40 m/s, exhaust speed 160 m/s and the casing loses 10 kW of heat. Elevations are equal. The gas loses \(2(820-600)=440\) kW of enthalpy flow. Its kinetic-energy rate grows by \(2(160^2-40^2)/2000=24.0\) kW. The unit delivers \(440-24-10=406\) kW shaft power. Counting all 440 kW as shaft output would overstate the result by both the residual exhaust energy and the heat leak.

A separate nozzle using gas at \(h_1=820\) kJ/kg and 40 m/s could reach \(h_2=800\) kJ/kg without a shaft if its speed rose to \(\sqrt{40^2+2(20\,000)}=204\) m/s. This estimate is an energy condition. The required area ratio, pressure drop and possibility of choking also depend on compressible-flow relations.

Extend the same balance to a mixer

Two insulated water lines mix at negligible speed change. One supplies 0.20 kg/s at 360 K and the other 0.30 kg/s at 300 K. A mass balance gives 0.50 kg/s at exit. With equal, constant \(c_p\) and no shaft, summing the two inlet enthalpy rates yields an outlet temperature \([0.20(360)+0.30(300)]/0.50=324\) K. The temperature lies between the inlet values, closer to the higher mass rate. The mixer conserves energy while creating entropy. The next lecture explains how both statements can hold simultaneously and why reversing the mixer without another effect is impossible.

Interpret a measured stagnation temperature

For an insulated diffuser with negligible shaft work, reducing speed from 200 to 40 m/s raises static enthalpy by \((200^2-40^2)/2=19.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). For air with \(c_p=1.005\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\) that corresponds to a 19.1 K static temperature rise. Stagnation enthalpy \(h+V^2/2\) stays constant even if internal friction produces entropy. A stationary temperature probe that slows the arriving air may approach a stagnation reading, so comparison with a predicted static temperature requires knowing where and how the measurement was taken.

Minimise compression work by selecting an intermediate pressure

A continuous air supply can be compressed in two stages and cooled at intermediate pressure. For a steady adiabatic compressor with negligible kinetic and potential changes, shaft work input is the stream enthalpy rise. With an ideal gas, constant \(c_p,\gamma\), and a reversible stage,

\[w=c_p T_{\rm in}[(p_{\rm out}/p_{\rm in})^a-1],\]

where \(a=(\gamma-1)/\gamma\). This follows by integrating \(ds=c_p\,dT/T-R\,dp/p=0\), then applying \(dh=c_p\,dT\). If both stages use the same isentropic efficiency, divide each ideal rise by that efficiency.

Assume complete intercooling to the original inlet temperature \(T_1\), no pressure losses and prescribed first and last pressures. Put \(r_1=p_i/p_1,\ r_2=p_3/p_i\), so \(r_1r_2=p_3/p_1=r\). Total ideal work is \(c_pT_1[r_1^a+(r/r_1)^a-2]\). Differentiate with respect to \(\ln r_1\); the derivative is \(c_pT_1a(r_1^a-r_2^a)\). The sum has a minimum at equal ratios, giving intermediate pressure \(p_i=\sqrt{p_1p_3}\). This is a geometric mean of absolute pressures. A gauge-pressure ratio would give a completely different design.

Compare two cooled stages with a single compression

Supply air at 100 kPa and 300 K and deliver it at 900 kPa absolute. For \(\gamma=1.4,\ c_p=1.005\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), an ideal single stage gives \(T_3=300(9^{0.2857})=562.0\,\mathrm K\) and work about 263 kJ/kg. Two equal-ratio stages operate through 300 kPa. Each reaches \(300(3^{0.2857})=410.6\,\mathrm K\) before its outlet or intercooler, requiring \(1.005(110.6)=111.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Total work is about 222.4 kJ/kg, roughly 15.5% lower. The intercooler must remove 111.2 kJ/kg between the stages under the ideal complete-cooling assumption.

With equal stage efficiencies of 0.80, each real enthalpy rise becomes 139.0 kJ/kg and the intermediate discharge temperature is about 438 K. Cooling back to 300 K then removes 139.0 kJ per kilogram, with a corresponding load of 27.8 kW at 0.20 kg/s. The equal-ratio choice survives because both stage work expressions acquired the same multiplier. An intercooler that can cool only to 330 K increases the weight of second-stage compression work; the new optimum shifts towards a larger first-stage pressure ratio. A design comparing only compressor shaft power would also need to account for coolant pumping, fan power and pressure losses before choosing the number of stages.

For an incomplete intercooler the optimal shift can be calculated. Take stage two inlet temperature \(T_m=330\) K independently maintained and identical efficiencies. The variable portion of total work is proportional to \(T_1r_1^a+T_m(r/r_1)^a\). Differentiating with respect to log first-stage ratio gives \(T_1r_1^a=T_mr_2^a\), or \(r_1/r_2=(330/300)^{1/a}=1.40\). At overall ratio nine,

\[r_1=\sqrt{9(1.40)}=3.54,\ r_2=2.54.\]

Compressing farther while the air is colder is energetically preferable within this simplified comparison. These ratios presume the cooler can maintain 330 K over its revised duty and that both discharge temperatures stay within their allowable ranges. A compressor map may impose further flow and speed limits beyond the thermodynamic minimum.

At fixed pressure endpoints, the measured intermediate pressure alone would thus be insufficient for diagnosing whether the cooler or a compressor caused an observed power increase.

How much static temperature does a nozzle convert to speed?

Air flows steadily through an insulated nozzle with negligible shaft work and elevation change. Take \(c_p=1005\) J/(kg K). Divide the conserved stagnation enthalpy \(c_pT+u^2/2\) by \(c_p\) to find the exit temperature.

Read values from the plot
Static temperature (K) at selected values of Exit speed (m/s).

Staged compression with an imperfect intercooler · Thermal processes and transport in engineering

A laboratory compressed-air supply has two compressor stages and an intercooler. Equal pressure ratios minimise work only under particular assumptions. Derive the optimum, compare it with a single-stage machine and identify which temperature and power measurements reveal imperfect cooling or unequal efficiency.

Problem sheet 1 · Energy accounts for stored and flowing matter

Draw a boundary, identify work and heat with consistent signs, and test the effect of changing the boundary.

A gas under a spring-loaded piston

A closed gas expands from 0.010 to 0.016 m³. The resisting absolute pressure rises linearly from 160 to 280 kPa. Its internal energy increases by 0.900 kJ. Compute the boundary work and the heat entering the gas. If the ambient pressure is 100 kPa, how much of the boundary work is available after displacing the atmosphere?

Work, heat and the atmosphere

The area under the straight pressure–volume line is the mean ordinate times the volume interval. With quasistatic motion,

\[W=(160+280)(0.006)/2=1.320\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

The first law with work positive out gives

\[Q=\Delta U+W=2.220\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

Displacing the atmosphere consumes \(100(0.006)=0.600\,\mathrm{kJ}\). The remaining 0.720 kJ raises the piston or compresses the spring. Subtract atmospheric work when assessing useful work, but use absolute pressure for the closed gas energy balance.

Is the exit speed consistent?

Air enters a steady insulated nozzle at 360 K and 30 m/s, and leaves at 325 K. Take constant specific heat 1.005 kJ/(kg K). Find the exit speed and the exit area for 0.24 kg/s if the exit density is 1.08 kg/m³. Explain what changes if the nozzle loses 3 kJ/kg as heat at the same temperatures.

Balance and continuity

Per unit mass,

\[V_2^2=V_1^2+2c_p(T_1-T_2)=900+2(1005)(35)=71250\,\mathrm{m^2/s^2}.\]

Thus \(V_2=266.9\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The area is

\[\dot m/(\rho_2 V_2)=8.33\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}.\]

Losing 3000 J/kg reduces \(V_2^2\) by 6000, giving 255.4 m/s. Energy that leaves the control volume as heat is unavailable to accelerate the stream.

Filling an evacuated rigid receiver

An insulated, initially evacuated tank is filled from a large air line at 300 K. The final mass is 0.80 kg. Assume perfect gas, \(c_p=1.005\), \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K), negligible kinetic energy in the final tank, no shaft work. Find the final energy and temperature. Why would a closed, constant-volume calculation yield a different interpretation?

Integrate the unsteady balance

Each kilogram carries inlet enthalpy 301.5 kJ/kg. Integrating over filling gives \(U_2=m_2 h_{\rm in}=241.2\,\mathrm{kJ}\) with a common zero-energy convention. Hence

\[T_2=h_{\rm in}/c_v=419.9\,\mathrm K.\]

The temperature rise accompanies the flow work carried in with the gas. A closed balance that follows the entering parcel must explicitly include the work that pushes the parcel into the receiver.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: State, process and the first law for a closed system; Open systems, enthalpy and steady-flow devices. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 3 · Entropy, irreversibility and useful-work loss

Check a proposed process with the second law and quantify the effect of a finite temperature difference.

From first principles

Separate entropy transfer from entropy generated inside a boundary

For a reversible differential process the thermodynamic definition of entropy is

\[dS=\delta Q_{\rm rev}/T.\]

Irreversible changes reach the same state-function difference but can generate entropy internally. For a closed system exchanging heats \(Q_j\) across boundary portions at known local temperatures \(T_j\), the second law gives an experimentally testable inequality:

\[S_2-S_1=\sum_j\int\frac{\delta Q_j}{T_j}+S_{\rm gen},\qquad S_{\rm gen}\ge0 .\]

To evaluate the left side independently of the actual path, use a reversible path between the same equilibrium states. For an ideal gas the fundamental relation \(Tds=du+p\,dv\), together with \(du=c_vdT\) and \(pv=RT\), yields

\[s_2-s_1=c_v\ln(T_2/T_1)+R\ln(v_2/v_1)\]

for constant specific heat. Inserting this property change into the balance isolates entropy generation of the actual process. A gas might cool and lose entropy while the combined gas plus surroundings generates entropy. For heat exchanged across a wall separating two reservoirs, the two reservoirs’ boundary transfer terms have opposite signs and different temperature denominators.

Background · Entropy generation
\[\dot S_{\rm gen}=\dot S_{\rm system}-\sum\dot Q_j/T_j-\dot S_{\rm mass,in}+\dot S_{\rm mass,out}\]

Use boundary temperatures at which heat actually crosses. All flows, storage and units must refer to the same control volume.

Derivation and applications

Count the entropy generated when two ideal gases mix

Two separated ideal gases can have the same temperature and pressure, yet their mixing increases entropy. Consider distinct nonreacting species in insulated rigid compartments. Remove the separator and let them equilibrate in the common volume. Initially both sides have temperature \(T\) and pressure \(p\), and no shaft work is performed. The total internal energy of ideal gases depends only on temperature, so the final equilibrium temperature stays \(T\). Denote mole numbers by \(n_i\) and total volume by \(V\).

For each species, the entropy change can be calculated along a reversible isothermal expansion into its accessible final volume, even though the actual mixing is irreversible. Integrating \(\delta Q_{\rm rev}/T=p_i\,dV/T=n_iR\,dV/V\) gives

\[\Delta S_i=n_iR\ln(V/V_i).\]

The initial volumes were proportional to mole numbers because \(p\) and \(T\) were common. Thus \(V_i/V=n_i/\sum_jn_j=y_i\), the final mole fraction. Add the species changes and use zero boundary entropy transfer:

\[\Delta S_{\rm mix}=S_{\rm gen}=-R\sum_i n_i\ln y_i .\]

For two gases with \(y\) and \(1-y\), the generation per mole of mixture is maximal when both are equally abundant. The derivative and its zero are

\[\frac{d}{dy}\frac{\Delta S_{\rm mix}}n=R\ln[(1-y)/y]=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad y=\tfrac12.\]

The entropy change tends to zero when either fraction tends to one. If the compartments contain the same gas and the initial temperatures and pressures already agree, removing the partition does not create distinguishable species; assigning artificial labels would predict a spurious mixing term.

Worked example

Estimate the lost work of mixing two samples

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Mix \(1.00\) mol of one ideal gas and \(3.00\) mol of another at \(300\) K. The mole fractions are \(0.250\) and \(0.750\). The generated entropy is

\[-8.314[\ln0.25+3\ln0.75]=18.7\,\mathrm{J/K}.\]

Relative to an environment at \(300\) K, the destroyed work potential is \(300(18.7)=5.61\) kJ. No energy has disappeared from the insulated vessel.

Second application

Compare equal and strongly unequal amounts

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

For a fixed total of \(4.00\) mol, an equal mixture generates \(4R\ln2=23.1\) J/K. With only \(0.0400\) mol of one species the fractions become \(0.0100\) and \(0.990\). The generation falls to

\[4R[-0.01\ln0.01-0.99\ln0.99]=1.86\,\mathrm{J/K}.\]

Each rare molecule gains access to much more volume, but there are few such molecules; both contributions must be counted before comparing samples.

Try it · Mixing

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Energy bookkeeping is not sufficient

In an insulated two-body experiment, heat lost by one body is gained by the other. That energy balance alone would allow heat to move spontaneously from a colder body to a hotter one. Entropy supplies the missing direction constraint. It is a property of a state. For a simple equilibrium system a reversible differential heat transfer defines \(dS=\delta Q_{\rm rev}/T\). An irreversible process between the same endpoints has the same entropy difference; the actual heat transfer divided by the body temperature need not equal that difference.

For a closed system, the integrated entropy balance reads \(\Delta S=\int \delta Q/T_b+S_{\rm gen}\), where \(T_b\) is the absolute temperature at the boundary where the heat crosses. Entropy generation satisfies \(S_{\rm gen}\ge0\). A system can lose entropy by exporting it in heat or in a mass stream. A negative change in the entropy of a single hot body therefore need not violate the second law.

Background · Why does a small temperature difference produce a second-order loss at fixed conductance?

Let a link of constant conductance \(G\) join reservoirs held at \(T+\Delta T/2\) and \(T-\Delta T/2\). In steady operation the heat current is \(G\Delta T\). Subtract the entropy leaving the hot reservoir from that entering the cold reservoir.

\[\dot S_{\rm gen}=G\Delta T\left(\frac{1}{T-\Delta T/2}-\frac{1}{T+\Delta T/2}\right)=\frac{G(\Delta T)^2}{T^2-(\Delta T/2)^2}.\]

At fixed conductance, halving a small difference approximately quarters the entropy production per second, while it also halves the heat current. If a design must keep the heat rate fixed, halving the difference requires nearly twice the conductance and only halves the production. This distinction matters when comparing a bigger exchanger with the original one.

Calculate a property change with a convenient path

For a calorically perfect ideal gas, combine \(T\,ds=du+p\,dv\) with \(du=c_v\,dT\) and \(p=RT/v\). This gives

\[ds=c_v\,dT/T+R\,dv/v.\]

Replacing \(v\) using the ideal-gas law yields the alternative \(ds=c_p\,dT/T-R\,dp/p\). Integrate between the initial and final equilibrium states. These are property relationships, even when the actual path between states includes friction, throttling or free expansion.

An ideal gas passing through an insulated valve with equal inlet and outlet speeds has \(h_2=h_1\), so its temperatures are equal in the constant-\(c_p\) approximation. Dropping air from 400 to 100 kPa gives \(s_2-s_1=-0.287\ln(100/400)=0.398\) kJ/(kg K). With no heat transfer or other streams, the positive entropy change per kilogram is entirely generated. A reversible insulated expansion turbine would generally produce a lower outlet temperature and shaft work.

Trace entropy across two reservoirs

An insulated conductor transfers 600 W from a reservoir at 400 K to another at 300 K. Its stored energy and entropy are steady. The hot reservoir loses entropy at \(600/400=1.50\) W/K, while the cold one gains \(600/300=2.00\) W/K. The total generation rate is 0.50 W/K. Heat rate is the same at both ends; entropy transfer in that heat differs because the temperatures differ.

\[\dot S_{\rm gen}=\dot Q\left(\frac1{T_c}-\frac1{T_h}\right).\]

If the surroundings defining the available-work reference are at \(T_0=300\) K, the corresponding destruction of available work is \(T_0\dot S_{\rm gen}=150\) W. For these particular reservoirs a reversible engine supplied with 600 W at 400 K could have produced \(600(1-300/400)=150\) W before rejecting the remainder. The conductor instead rejects all 600 W to the cold sink. Energy did not disappear; an opportunity for work was lost.

Locate sources and choose a boundary

A heat exchanger can have little external heat leak yet generate appreciable entropy internally through finite temperature differences and pressure losses. Write \(\dot S_{\rm gen}=\sum_{\rm out}\dot m s-\sum_{\rm in}\dot m s-\sum_j\dot Q_j/T_{b,j}\) for steady operation. Both streams belong inside the same control volume when testing internal heat exchange. If the boundary encloses only one stream, heat crossing the wall carries entropy with it and must appear explicitly. The largest reduction in entropy generation per extra unit of area or pumping effort is a design question. The thermodynamic balance identifies losses without alone determining the economically preferred geometry.

Compare the conditions on a valve and a turbine

Air passes through a steady insulated throttling valve from 400 to 100 kPa with negligible changes of speed. With no shaft work the first law gives equal inlet and outlet enthalpy. For ideal air this implies equal temperature. Its entropy change follows from \(\Delta s=c_p\ln(T_2/T_1)-R\ln(p_2/p_1)=0.287\ln4=0.398\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\). At 0.10 kg/s the entropy generation is 0.0398 kW/K. An ideal adiabatic turbine undergoing the same pressure drop has constant entropy and delivers work as its temperature falls. Pressure ratio alone therefore cannot determine the temperature change across an unfamiliar device.

Obtain a work bound for two finite bodies

The reversible efficiency \(1-T_c/T_h\) describes operation between reservoirs held at fixed temperatures. If the two bodies are finite, each increment of transferred heat changes the temperatures and the instantaneous efficiency. Consider two incompressible blocks in a common insulated enclosure. Their heat capacities \(C_h,C_c\) are taken as constant. A reversible engine operates between them and exports mechanical work until no temperature difference remains. The working fluid returns to its starting state after each incremental cycle, so conservation of entropy for this reversible composite system determines the final temperature.

\[C_h\ln(T_f/T_h)+C_c\ln(T_f/T_c)=0,\]
\[T_f=T_h^{C_h/(C_h+C_c)}T_c^{C_c/(C_h+C_c)}.\]

Energy conservation gives maximum work \(W_{\max}=C_hT_h+C_cT_c-(C_h+C_c)T_f\) for consistent internal-energy references. The reversible final temperature is a weighted geometric mean. If the two blocks are simply put in thermal contact without work output, first-law conservation instead gives the weighted arithmetic mean. The arithmetic mean is larger for unequal initial temperatures; additional energy remains as sensible heat after irreversible contact. These different final temperatures matter when comparing two ways of using the same finite resources.

In a real engine running at finite heat-transfer rates there are temperature differences between each block and the working fluid. Total entropy then increases. For a specified common final temperature the entropy change of the blocks is fixed by state properties; measuring it indicates the departure from the ideal reversible construction without needing the working-fluid cycle.

Compare direct contact and a reversible engine quantitatively

Take a hot assembly of heat capacity 2.0 kJ/K at 450 K and a cooler assembly of 1.0 kJ/K at 300 K. Solving the log balance gives

\[T_f=(450^2\,300)^{1/3}=393.1\,\mathrm K.\]

The hot assembly releases about \(2(450-393.1)=113.8\,\mathrm{kJ}\) and the cold one receives 93.1 kJ. Their difference gives approximately 20.7 kJ of maximum work. Acting as though the initial temperatures stayed at 450 and 300 K throughout would assign an incorrectly high efficiency to later parts of the transfer.

By direct contact the common temperature would be \((2\times450+300)/3=400\,\mathrm K\). The entropy generated is

\[2\ln(400/450)+\ln(400/300)=0.0521\,\mathrm{kJ/K}.\]

If instead a device delivers a measured 12 kJ of work before the blocks reach a common temperature, energy fixes that temperature as \((1200-12)/3=396\,\mathrm K\). The measured process then generates \(2\ln(396/450)+\ln(396/300)=0.0220\,\mathrm{kJ/K}\), a positive value between the two limiting cases. Entropy generation multiplied by an environmental temperature gives destroyed exergy relative to that environment; comparing the finite-body work outputs also requires acknowledging their different final stored energies.

The geometric-mean result has an informative limiting case. If the cold assembly is so large that \(C_c/C_h\) tends to infinity, its temperature remains effectively fixed, and the engine work reduces to \(C_h[(T_h-T_c)-T_c\ln(T_h/T_c)]\). This is the integral of the reversible work recovered as the finite hot block approaches a constant sink. It is smaller than applying the initial Carnot efficiency to all of the hot block’s released heat.

Which mixture generates the most entropy?

Mix streams of the same incompressible liquid at 370 K and 290 K, at equal pressure, with constant \(c_p=4180\) J/(kg K). The energy balance fixes the outlet temperature. Evaluate the outlet entropy minus the mass-weighted inlet entropy using \(c_p\ln T\).

Chapter 4 · Thermodynamic cycles, refrigeration and phase change

Analyse a vapour-compression cycle from supplied state properties and compare its performance with a reversible bound.

From first principles

Obtain cycle efficiency and refrigerator performance from two balances

A working fluid completing a cycle returns to its original thermodynamic state, so its internal energy and entropy have zero net changes. Denote positive magnitudes of heat received from the hot reservoir and given to the cold reservoir by \(Q_h,Q_c\), with absolute temperatures \(T_h>T_c\). For a heat engine the first law then gives work delivered as their difference, and the cycle entropy balance relates both heat amounts:

\[W_{\rm out}=Q_h-Q_c,\]
\[0=Q_h/T_h-Q_c/T_c+S_{\rm gen}.\]

Since generated entropy is nonnegative, \(Q_c/Q_h\ge T_c/T_h\). Thus the maximum fraction of the absorbed heat available as work occurs for a reversible cycle:

\[\eta=W_{\rm out}/Q_h=1-Q_c/Q_h\le1-T_c/T_h .\]

For a refrigerator run in the opposite direction the useful output is extracted cold heat and the input is compressor work. Applying the same balances with heat directions reversed gives

\[\mathrm{COP}=Q_c/(Q_h-Q_c)\le T_c/(T_h-T_c).\]

These limits assume exactly two thermal reservoirs. The actual cycle depends on how its individual compression, heat exchange and expansion processes are carried out.

Derivation and applications

Combine two refrigeration stages without counting intermediate heat twice

A cascade refrigerator can move heat across a large temperature interval using two separate loops. For an ideal limit, assume each stage is reversible, works steadily, and exchanges heat with constant-temperature reservoirs. The lower stage extracts \(Q_c\) per cycle at \(T_c\) and rejects \(Q_i\) at intermediate temperature \(T_i\). The upper stage takes precisely this \(Q_i\) and rejects \(Q_h\) at \(T_h\). All temperatures are absolute; ignore other heat leaks, pressure drops and energy storage at the intermediate exchanger.

For a reversible cycle the working fluid returns to its initial entropy, so its incoming and outgoing heat-to-temperature ratios match. Apply this statement independently to both stages:

\[Q_i/Q_c=T_i/T_c,\]
\[Q_h/Q_i=T_h/T_i.\]

Energy conservation gives the work delivered to each compressor. In the sum the intermediate heat cancels because it leaves the first loop and enters the second:

\[W_1=Q_i-Q_c,\]
\[W_2=Q_h-Q_i,\]
\[W_{\rm tot}=Q_h-Q_c.\]

Combine the two ratios to remove \(T_i\). The minimum work and highest possible overall cooling coefficient of performance are

\[W_{\rm min}=Q_c(T_h/T_c-1),\]
\[\mathrm{COP}_{\rm cascade}=Q_c/W_{\rm min}=T_c/(T_h-T_c).\]

The ideal intermediate temperature controls how work is split but cannot change the reversible overall limit. A real cascade can benefit from smaller pressure ratios in its components. Heat exchanged across a finite temperature difference at the interstage exchanger produces entropy and raises work above the reversible value.

Worked example

Allocate heat and work in a laboratory freezer

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Take \(T_c=240\) K, \(T_i=270\) K, \(T_h=300\) K and cooling duty \(2.00\) kW. The lower stage rejects \(2.25\) kW, consuming \(0.250\) kW. The upper rejects \(2.50\) kW and also consumes \(0.250\) kW. The overall COP is

\[2.00/0.500=4.00.\]

The upper loop has to process all the heat extracted from the cold space plus the lower compressor work.

Second application

Quantify the cost of a finite interstage temperature difference

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Keep the end temperatures and cooling duty, but let the lower stage reject heat at \(275\) K while the upper stage takes it at \(265\) K. Treat each separate stage as internally reversible. The shared heat rate is now \(2(275/240)=2.292\) kW. Upper rejection is

\[2.292(300/265)=2.594\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

The total work rises to \(0.594\) kW and COP falls to \(3.37\). The exchanger generates \(2.292(1/265-1/275)=0.000315\) kW/K; multiplied by \(300\) K this accounts for the extra compressor work.

Try it · Cascade

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Account for transfers over a complete cycle

A cyclic machine returns a working fluid to its initial state. Its internal energy and entropy changes over one complete cycle are zero. Heat and shaft work can nevertheless have nonzero net values. For an engine receiving \(Q_h\), rejecting positive magnitude \(Q_c\) and delivering work \(W_{\rm out}\), the first law gives

\[W_{\rm out}=Q_h-Q_c.\]

Thermal efficiency is \(W_{\rm out}/Q_h\). A refrigerator instead consumes work to remove heat from a cold space. Its useful effect is cooling, so its performance measure is \(\mathrm{COP}_R=Q_c/W_{\rm in}\).

Between reservoirs at absolute temperatures \(T_h,T_c\) a reversible cycle transports equal entropy \(Q_h/T_h=Q_c/T_c\). Combining this condition with the first law gives \(\eta_{\rm rev}=1-T_c/T_h\) for an engine and \(\mathrm{COP}_{R,\rm rev}=T_c/(T_h-T_c)\) for a refrigerator. The latter can exceed one because cooling duty includes transported heat. A heat pump uses \(Q_h\) as useful output and has \(\mathrm{COP}_{HP}=\mathrm{COP}_R+1\) for the same cycle.

Follow the refrigerant once around the loop

Label the compressor inlet 1, discharge 2, condenser outlet 3 and expansion-valve outlet 4. Assume steady operation, negligible kinetic and elevation changes and insulated compressor and valve. The evaporator absorbs \(q_c=h_1-h_4\) per kilogram, the compressor consumes \(w=h_2-h_1\), the condenser rejects \(q_h=h_2-h_3\) and throttling preserves enthalpy \(h_4=h_3\). Enter enthalpies from a consistent refrigerant table or supplied state data. A constant ideal-gas heat capacity cannot represent latent heat across a two-phase region.

At saturation a mixture at quality \(x\) has \(h=(1-x)h_f+xh_g\), where \(x\) is vapour mass fraction and \(h_f,h_g\) are saturated liquid and vapour enthalpies at the same pressure. A throttle can create vapour as the pressure falls even though no external heat enters the valve. The mixture redistributes its enthalpy between phases at the new saturation temperature.

Determine cooling, power and flash fraction

For an illustrative refrigerant take enthalpies \(h_1=390\), \(h_2=430\), \(h_3=h_4=250\) kJ/kg. At evaporator pressure the saturated values are \(h_f=200,\ h_g=390\) kJ/kg. With mass flow 0.060 kg/s, the evaporator removes \(0.060(390-250)=8.40\) kW, the compressor needs 2.40 kW and the condenser rejects 10.80 kW. The powers satisfy \(8.40+2.40=10.80\) kW, and cooling COP is 3.50. The valve outlet quality is \((250-200)/(390-200)=0.263\): just over a quarter of the mass is already vapour when it enters the evaporator.

Suppose that at the actual discharge pressure an isentropic compression would give \(h_{2s}=420\) kJ/kg. The compressor isentropic efficiency is

\[(h_{2s}-h_1)/(h_2-h_1)=30/40=0.75.\]

Losses raise the required shaft power and increase condenser duty at unchanged evaporator inlet and outlet conditions.

Distinguish a property assumption from a bound

If heat is removed from a 268 K reservoir and rejected to a 308 K reservoir, the reversible refrigeration COP is

\[268/(308-268)=6.70.\]

A measured COP of 3.50 lies below that limit. Real heat exchangers need finite temperature differences, compressors generate entropy and valves destroy expansion work. Reducing the required temperature lift improves the reversible limit. The working fluid’s actual pressures and enthalpies must be redetermined to estimate a real COP change. A cycle sketch and a numbered state table prevent accidentally using a compressor outlet temperature as the external hot-reservoir temperature in the bound.

Follow the energy after the compressor stops

Consider a cold cabinet held at constant temperature by a refrigerator. If it receives a 100 W heat leak and a 25 W electric fan dissipates power inside it, the evaporator must remove 125 W in steady operation. At cooling COP 2.5 the compressor draws 50 W, and the condenser releases \(125+50=175\) W into the room. If both the condenser and the cabinet exterior exchange heat with that room, its net thermal gain from the refrigerator system is

\[175-100=75\,\mathrm{W}.\]

This equals the total electrical input to fan and compressor. Boundary selection explains why the condenser duty and the net heating of the entire room have different values.

Derive a gas-turbine efficiency from pressure ratio

A gas turbine running on a closed idealised Brayton cycle provides a useful counterpart to a refrigerating cycle. Model compression and expansion as isentropic ideal-gas processes with constant heat capacities. Heat is supplied and rejected at constant pressure. Let \(T_1\) be compressor inlet, \(T_2\) compressor outlet, \(T_3\) turbine inlet and \(T_4\) exhaust, and set \(r_p=p_2/p_1=p_3/p_4\). For \(a=(\gamma-1)/\gamma\), the isentropic relations give

\[T_2/T_1=T_3/T_4=r_p^a.\]

The heat quantities per kilogram are \(q_{\rm in}=c_p(T_3-T_2)\) and \(q_{\rm out}=c_p(T_4-T_1)\).

Rewriting the latter difference as \((T_3-T_2)/r_p^a\) yields the ideal thermal efficiency \(1-r_p^{-a}\). This simple rise with pressure ratio does not mean that available shaft work rises indefinitely for a fixed permissible turbine inlet temperature. Net specific work divided by \(c_p\) is \(T_3(1-1/y)-T_1(y-1)\) with \(y=r_p^a\). Differentiating gives \(T_3/y^2-T_1=0\), so maximum work at fixed end temperatures occurs at \(y=\sqrt{T_3/T_1}\). When compression heats the gas all the way to \(T_3\), external heat input and net output both vanish in the ideal model.

Calculate the fraction of turbine output consumed by compression

Let \(T_1=300\,\mathrm K,\ T_3=1200\,\mathrm K,\ r_p=8,\ \gamma=1.4\), and \(c_p=1.005\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\). Then \(y=1.811\),

\[T_2=543.4\,\mathrm K,\]
\[T_4=662.5\,\mathrm K.\]

Compressor work is 244.7 kJ/kg, turbine work is 540.2 kJ/kg and net output is about 295.6 kJ/kg. The back-work ratio is \(244.7/540.2=0.453\), and the thermal efficiency is 44.8%. The ideal maximum-work pressure ratio would be \(2^{1/a}=11.31\) for these temperature limits.

Now specify compressor isentropic efficiency 0.82 and turbine efficiency 0.87 at the same pressure ratio and inlet temperatures. Actual compressor outlet is \(300+(543.4-300)/0.82=596.9\,\mathrm K\) and actual turbine outlet is

\[1200-0.87(1200-662.5)=732.3\,\mathrm K.\]

The net work becomes

\[1.005[(1200-732.3)-(596.9-300)]=171.7\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Heat supplied is \(1.005(1200-596.9)=606.1\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), giving efficiency 28.3%. A relatively small fractional degradation in each machine causes a larger fractional drop in net power because compression consumes a large portion of gross turbine work. If a recuperator is considered, the actual exhaust being hotter than the compressor discharge offers a possible heat-recovery driving temperature difference.

The actual exhaust and compressor temperatures also quantify the limit of regeneration. If both streams have the same heat capacity rate and an idealised recuperator has effectiveness 0.70, the compressed stream can gain \(0.70(732.3-596.9)=94.8\) K before entering the external heater. Its required heat input falls by \(1.005(94.8)=95.3\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), to about 511 kJ/kg. If turbine and compressor pressure ratios, inlet temperatures and work remain unchanged, thermal efficiency rises to

\[171.7/511=33.6\%.\]

Check both exchanger terminal temperatures to ensure heat still flows in the intended direction. Pressure drop in an actual recuperator can reduce net work, while the lower fuel requirement might still justify the component. A recuperator cannot improve the ideal cycle at an operating condition where turbine exhaust is already colder than compressor delivery.

Counterflow heat exchange here transfers energy internally, so the cycle energy account includes the reduced external heating requirement and leaves the recuperator transfer out of its net external balance.

How does piston compression ratio set the ideal cycle efficiency?

Consider a fixed mass of air in a piston cylinder. Represent heat exchange by two constant-volume processes and represent rapid compression and expansion by reversible adiabatic paths. This air-standard Otto cycle is a closed reference cycle. With constant specific heats, \(c_v\) determines the change in internal energy and \(\gamma=c_p/c_v\) determines the adiabatic temperature change. Let \(r=V_1/V_2\), with state 1 just before compression and state 2 just after. Heat is supplied from 2 to 3 at the smaller volume and rejected from 4 to 1 at the larger.

The isentropic law \(TV^{\gamma-1}=\mathrm{constant}\) gives \(T_2=r^{\gamma-1}T_1\) for compression and \(T_3=r^{\gamma-1}T_4\) for expansion. The constant-volume heat transfers have magnitudes \(q_\mathrm{in}=c_v(T_3-T_2)\) and \(q_\mathrm{out}=c_v(T_4-T_1)\), since the boundary work vanishes on each of those legs. Subtract the two temperature relations to obtain

\[T_3-T_2=r^{\gamma-1}(T_4-T_1).\]

The complete cycle returns to the same internal energy, giving

\[\eta=\frac{w_\mathrm{net}}{q_\mathrm{in}}=1-\frac{q_\mathrm{out}}{q_\mathrm{in}}=1-r^{1-\gamma}.\]

For \(T_1=300\,\mathrm K\), \(T_3=1500\,\mathrm K\), \(r=8\), \(\gamma=1.40\), and \(c_v=0.718\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}K^{-1}}\), compression ends at \(689.2\,\mathrm K\) and expansion at \(652.9\,\mathrm K\). The supplied heat is \(582.1\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\) and rejected heat \(253.4\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\). The difference gives \(328.7\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\) of net work and \(\eta=56.5\%\). Raising \(r\) to 10 while retaining both temperature limits yields \(\eta=60.2\%\), but only \(535.9\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\) can now be supplied. Net work falls to \(322.6\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\). Thus a larger fraction of the available heat becomes work, although the specified cycle does less work per kilogram. The temperature constraint matters when comparing design alternatives. Varying specific heats would change these numerical estimates.

Practise on sheet 2 · Test a heat input against a peak-temperature limit. Further reading · MIT · 16.Unified Thermodynamics and Propulsion · §3.5.1 Efficiency of an ideal Otto cycle.

Where does raising compression stop increasing work?

Set the cold state to 300 K and keep each upper temperature fixed. With constant \(\gamma=1.4\) and \(c_v=0.718\) kJ/(kg K), the isentropic legs give

\[T_2=T_1 r^{\gamma-1},\]
\[T_4=T_3/r^{\gamma-1}.\]

Subtract the rejected heat from the supplied heat.

Problem sheet 2 · Entropy production and performance limits

Calculate an irreversibility and use a reversible benchmark to assess engines and refrigerators.

Two insulated copper blocks

Blocks of masses 2 and 1 kg at 400 and 280 K touch inside an insulated enclosure. Use \(c=0.390\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\). Find the final temperature and total entropy generation. Why may one block lose entropy?

Combine energy and entropy balances

Energy conservation gives

\[T_f=(2\times400+280)/3=360\,\mathrm K.\]

The warmer block has \(\Delta S_h=0.780\ln(360/400)=-0.0822\,\mathrm{kJ/K}\). The other gains \(0.390\ln(360/280)=0.0980\,\mathrm{kJ/K}\). The net 0.0158 kJ/K is generated internally. Entropy crosses the interface with heat, so only the total for the insulated pair has to be nondecreasing.

Interpret a heat-engine test

An engine absorbs 15 kW from a reservoir at 750 K and rejects 10 kW to a second reservoir at 300 K. Find its power, thermal efficiency, entropy generation and reversible maximum power at the same heat input.

Interpret the missing output

The cyclic energy balance gives \(15-10=5\,\mathrm{kW}\) of power and efficiency \(5/15=0.333\). The reservoirs generate a combined entropy change rate \(10/300-15/750=0.01333\,\mathrm{kW/K}\); the working substance returns to its initial state. A reversible engine reaches \(1-300/750=0.600\), or 9 kW. The difference of 4 kW equals \(300\dot S_{\rm gen}\) at this sink temperature.

Measure cooling at the evaporator

Refrigerant enthalpies before and after compression are 405 and 451 kJ/kg. Liquid leaves the condenser at 257 kJ/kg and throttles into the evaporator. For 0.045 kg/s, calculate cooling duty, compressor input and heating COP. If the evaporator and condenser reservoirs are at 263 and 308 K, check the heating COP against its reversible bound.

Close the cycle

The valve gives

\[h_4=h_3=257\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Evaporation absorbs \(0.045(405-257)=6.66\,\mathrm{kW}\); compression requires

\[0.045(451-405)=2.07\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

The condenser duty is 8.73 kW, hence the heating COP is 4.22. The reversible heating bound is

\[308/(308-263)=6.84.\]

Both COPs refer to heat delivered to the warm side per unit work, which permits values above unity.

Test a heat input against a peak-temperature limit

Start an ideal air-standard Otto cycle at 290 K with \(r=7.00\), \(\gamma=1.40\) and \(c_v=0.718\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}K^{-1}}\). An electrical heater supplies \(500\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\) at constant volume. Determine the peak temperature and the net work. If the permitted peak is 1250 K, find the allowable input and the resulting net work.

Show the worked solution

Compression gives

\[T_2=290(7^{0.4})=631.6\,\mathrm K.\]

The nominal heat input raises this to \(T_3=T_2+500/0.718=1328.0\,\mathrm K\), exceeding the limit by 78 K. Expansion would give

\[T_4=609.7\,\mathrm K.\]

At this compression ratio \(\eta=1-7^{-0.4}=0.54084\), so the nominal work is \(270.4\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\). Limiting the supplied heat to \(0.718(1250-631.6)=444.0\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\) respects the peak. Net work becomes \(240.1\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}\). The compression ratio and ideal efficiency are unchanged.

Revisit chapter 4 · How does piston compression ratio set the ideal cycle efficiency?.

Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 5 · Steady conduction, thermal resistance and insulation

Derive the heat leak through a cylindrical wall and identify when increasing insulation thickness can increase heat transfer.

From first principles

Derive series thermal resistance from conservation of heat rate

Consider conduction along a prescribed path through a stationary material, with local area \(A(x)\) normal to the heat flow and conductivity \(k(x)>0\). Assume a one-dimensional steady state, no side losses and no heat generation. A balance on an elementary slice gives a single heat rate \(\dot Q\) at every position. Fourier's measured constitutive relation links that heat rate to the temperature gradient:

\[\frac{d\dot Q}{dx}=0,\]
\[\dot Q=-k(x)A(x)\frac{dT}{dx}.\]

Separate variables and integrate from the hot to the cold surface. An exterior fluid film described by \(\dot Q=h A_s(T_s-T_\infty)\) introduces an additional drop proportional to the same heat rate:

\[T_{\rm hot}-T_\infty=\dot Q\left[\int_{\rm solid}\frac{dx}{k(x)A(x)}+\frac{1}{h A_s}\right].\]

The bracket is a thermal resistance in K/W. For a plane homogeneous layer it gives \(L/(kA)\); for radial conduction in a cylinder the local area \(2\pi rL\) varies, giving a logarithm. Adding resistances is justified by a common heat rate. Heat-generating layers or side branches require a separate balance before using a series formula.

Derivation and applications

How the changing area sets resistance in a spherical shell

Heat passing through the insulation of a spherical vessel spreads over a larger surface as it travels outward. Treat an inner surface at radius \(r_i\) as isothermal, a concentric outer radius \(r_o\), constant thermal conductivity \(k\), and uniform outside convection coefficient \(h\). Assume steady radial conduction with no internal heat generation, perfect interfaces and a uniform ambient temperature \(T_\infty\). With no accumulation between concentric spheres, the heat rate is constant even though local heat flux decreases outward.

Fourier’s empirical law specifies flux from temperature gradient. Multiply it by the local area and rearrange so that each radial slice contributes its own temperature drop:

\[\dot Q=-4\pi r^2 k\,dT/dr,\]
\[T_i-T_o=\frac{\dot Q}{4\pi k}\int_{r_i}^{r_o}\frac{dr}{r^2}.\]

The outer convective drop equals the same heat rate divided by the outer film conductance. Adding both drops gives a series relation:

\[\frac{T_i-T_\infty}{\dot Q}=R=\frac{1}{4\pi k}\!\left(\frac1{r_i}-\frac1{r_o}\right)+\frac1{4\pi h r_o^2}.\]

Increasing \(r_o\) adds conduction resistance but also raises film area. The sign of the derivative decides which effect dominates:

\[\frac{dR}{dr_o}=\frac{1}{4\pi k r_o^2}-\frac{2}{4\pi h r_o^3},\]
\[r_{\rm critical}=2k/h .\]

For an initially smaller radius, adding a thin layer can reduce total resistance; after this minimum the resistance grows. The result presumes fixed \(h\) and surface temperature \(T_i\). If changes in geometry substantially change natural convection or radiation, the assumed film law must be revisited.

Worked example

Evaluate an insulated sensor bead

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Take \(r_i=3.00\) mm, \(r_o=6.00\) mm, \(k=0.0400\) W/(m K) and \(h=10.0\) W/(m² K). The shell contributes \(331.6\) K/W and the film \(221.0\) K/W. A \(40.0\) K inner-to-ambient difference gives \(0.0724\) W. The uncoated bead had outer resistance \(884\) K/W and lost only \(0.0452\) W. At \(6\) mm the coating is still below the \(8.00\) mm critical radius.

Second application

Determine whether a change in airflow reverses the trend

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

With forced airflow raising \(h\) to \(40.0\) W/(m² K), the critical radius falls to \(2.00\) mm, inside the bare bead. The bare and coated resistances become \(221\) and \(331.6+55.3=386.9\) K/W. The coating now reduces heat loss from \(0.181\) to \(0.103\) W. The same layer can change the sign of its effect because the surrounding film is part of the experiment.

Turn heat flux into a temperature field

Energy moves through a stationary homogeneous solid down its temperature gradient. For isotropic conduction Fourier’s law is

\[\mathbf q=-k\nabla T,\]

where \(\mathbf q\) is heat flux in W/m² and \(k\) is thermal conductivity. A steady one-dimensional wall with no internal heat generation passes the same heat rate at every cut. Heat flux is uniform only when the area of the cuts is uniform. That distinction matters for pipes and spherical shells.

Let an inner cylindrical surface at radius \(r_i\) be warmer than the outer one at \(r_o\). Assume a long concentric wall of length \(L\), constant \(k\), negligible end loss and axisymmetry. Fourier’s law gives

\[\dot Q=-2\pi rLk\,dT/dr,\]

with outward rate taken positive. The area grows with radius, so the magnitude of \(dT/dr\) decreases outward even though the heat rate is unchanged.

Derive and combine thermal resistances

Separate and integrate over the cylindrical wall. This yields

\[T_i-T_o=\dot Q\ln(r_o/r_i)/(2\pi k L).\]

The cylindrical conduction resistance is the factor multiplying \(\dot Q\). At an outer convection boundary \(\dot Q=h\,2\pi r_oL(T_o-T_\infty)\), giving another resistance between the surface and the bulk surroundings. For a prescribed inner surface temperature,

\[\dot Q=\frac{T_i-T_\infty}{\ln(r_o/r_i)/(2\pi kL)+1/(2\pi r_oLh)}.\]

For multiple concentric layers, add the conduction resistance of each. The drops across series resistances sum because the same rate passes through them all. Poor contact between layers can add another temperature jump. A parallel heat leak bypassing the insulation carries part of the total heat under a common temperature difference, so its conductance adds instead.

Estimate the heat leak from a warm pipe

Take \(L=1.0\) m, \(r_i=0.020\) m and \(r_o=0.040\) m. The insulation has \(k=0.050\) W/(m K) and the surroundings have \(h=10\) W/(m² K), \(T_\infty=293\) K. The inner insulation surface is held at 353 K. The conduction resistance is \(\ln2/[2\pi(0.050)(1)]=2.206\) K/W, and the outer film resistance is

\[1/[10(2\pi)(0.040)(1)]=0.398\,\mathrm{K/W}.\]

Total heat loss is \(60/(2.206+0.398)=23.0\) W per metre of pipe.

The outer surface is

\[293+(23.0)(0.398)=302.2\,\mathrm{K}.\]

The rest of the 60 K difference is within the insulation. The heat flux at the outer surface is half that at the inner surface, because the heat-transfer area is twice as large. Assigning the outer circumference to the entire wall in a flat-wall formula would miss this variation. When thickness is very small relative to radius, expanding the logarithm recovers the plane-wall approximation.

Explain the critical radius

Increasing \(r_o\) makes the conduction resistance grow but makes the convection resistance shrink. At fixed \(T_i,T_\infty,h,k\), differentiating the total resistance gives

\[dR/dr_o=1/(2\pi Lk r_o)-1/(2\pi Lh r_o^2).\]

Its minimum occurs at \(r_c=k/h\); that radius maximises heat loss. Here \(r_c=0.0050\) m, already below the bare pipe radius, so adding this insulation reduces loss from the outset. A much thinner wire could initially lose more heat because its exposed area increases faster than the wall resistance. This conclusion assumes the same external coefficient as the diameter changes. If natural convection or radiation changes appreciably, the boundary model and its temperature dependence must be reconsidered.

Recover an unknown contact resistance

A 0.010 m² test sandwich passes 8 W at steady state between measured outer face temperatures of 50 and 40 °C. Its measured total resistance is

\[10/8=1.25\,\mathrm{K/W}.\]

The two known solid layers together account for 0.75 K/W, leaving 0.50 K/W attributable to the interface if other parasitic paths are small. The area-normalised contact resistance is \(R''=A R=0.0050\,\mathrm{m^2K/W}\), corresponding to conductance \(1/R''=200\,\mathrm{W/(m^2K)}\). A pair of thermocouples on opposite sides of the interface should then show a 4 K temperature difference. That independent measurement helps distinguish contact resistance from an unaccounted external heat loss.

Derive the temperature field along a cooling fin

A fin adds area for convection but must first conduct heat from its base to that area. Treat a straight fin with constant cross-sectional area \(A_c\), exposed perimeter \(P\), conductivity \(k\) and uniform film coefficient \(h\). Its base is held at \(T_b\) and surrounding air at \(T_\infty\). Assume the temperature is nearly uniform across each fin section; a small transverse Biot number supports this approximation. Take the tip as adiabatic, appropriate when tip area is small or insulated. Define excess temperature \(\theta=T-T_\infty\).

A slice \(dx\) loses heat \(hP\theta\,dx\) to convection. Its decrease in axial heat rate must supply that loss. Fourier’s law \(Q_x=-kA_c\,d\theta/dx\) then yields \(\theta''-m^2\theta=0\), where \(m=\sqrt{hP/(kA_c)}\). The boundary conditions are

\[\theta(0)=\theta_b,\ \theta'(L)=0.\]

Using a hyperbolic cosine referenced to the insulated end gives

\[\theta(x)=\theta_b\frac{\cosh[m(L-x)]}{\cosh(mL)},\]
\[Q_b=\sqrt{hPkA_c}\,\theta_b\tanh(mL).\]

If the whole fin stayed at base temperature, heat removal would be \(hPL\theta_b\). The ratio \(\eta_f=\tanh(mL)/(mL)\) is the fin efficiency under the stated tip model. A very long fin can have low efficiency although it removes more heat than a short one; efficiency measures use of installed area, while total heat removal measures cooling duty.

Decide whether doubling fin length is worthwhile

An aluminium fin has rectangular section 20 mm by 2 mm, length 50 mm and conductivity 180 W/(m K). Air at 25 °C flows over it with \(h=35\,\mathrm{W/(m^2K)}\), and the base is at 75 °C. Thus

\[A_c=4.0\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^2},\ P=0.044\,\mathrm m,\ \theta_b=50\,\mathrm K.\]

The transverse length \(A_c/P=0.909\,\mathrm{mm}\) gives \(hA_c/(kP)=0.000177\), consistent with small cross-sectional gradients. The fin parameter is \(m=\sqrt{35(0.044)/[180(4\times10^{-5})]}=14.6\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}\); hence \(mL=0.731\) and \(\tanh(mL)=0.624\).

The base heat rate is

\[\sqrt{35(0.044)(180)(4\times10^{-5})}(50)(0.624)=3.29\,\mathrm W.\]

Fin efficiency is

\[0.624/0.731=0.854.\]

Doubling length makes \(mL=1.46\) and raises heat removal to about \(5.27\tanh(1.46)=4.73\,\mathrm W\). Twice the material therefore gives only 44% more cooling. The original covered base area by itself would convect \(35(4\times10^{-5})(50)=0.070\,\mathrm W\), so the fin still offers a large improvement. Adding several fins can change the film coefficient through crowding, and finite contact resistance can lower the effective base temperature. Those effects must be checked before multiplying a single-fin heat rate by the number installed.

A surface thermometer offers a second way to test the heat-transfer model. The insulated-tip solution gives

\[T(L)=25+50/\cosh(0.731)=64.1^\circ\mathrm C.\]

If a thin thermocouple instead measures nearly 75 °C all along a fin while the base still sheds only the predicted few watts, the effective film coefficient may be smaller than assumed. A much colder measured tip could indicate strong local tip convection or poor base contact. Interpret a profile together with the base heat rate: several combinations of conductance, base temperature and film conditions can reproduce one measured temperature, but they need not reproduce both the profile and the total cooling duty.

The root of the fin offers a particularly direct simultaneous measurement of axial temperature gradient and supplied heat rate.

Infer wall cooling from a measured skin-friction force

The film coefficient in a thermal-resistance calculation carries information about transport within the flow. For a smooth isothermal plate, take a steady parallel free stream of speed \(U\) and temperature \(T_e\). Define \(\theta=(T-T_w)/(T_e-T_w)\). Both \(u/U\) and \(\theta\) rise from zero at the wall to one in the outer stream. If momentum and heat diffuse at equal rates, their transport equations and boundary conditions match for a constant-property, zero-pressure-gradient boundary layer starting at the same leading edge. Under this approximation their profiles and their normalized wall gradients coincide. In turbulent flow the analogy additionally relies on similar turbulent diffusivities.

For a wall hotter than the stream the heat flux into the fluid and the magnitude of skin friction are \(q''=k(T_w-T_e)\partial_y\theta|_w\) and \(\tau_w=\mu U\partial_y(u/U)|_w\). Divide the equations. At \(Pr=\mu c_p/k=1\), the unknown gradient cancels. With \(q''=h(T_w-T_e)\) the Reynolds analogy becomes

\[h\simeq\frac{c_p\tau_w}{U},\]
\[St=\frac{h}{\rho Uc_p}\simeq \frac{C_f}{2},\]
\[C_f=\frac{\tau_w}{\tfrac12\rho U^2}.\]

A small heat-transfer tunnel has effective \(Pr\) close to one. At \(U=10.0\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\), the stream has \(\rho=1.20\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\) and \(c_p=1000\,\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}K^{-1}}\). A force balance isolates the skin friction on one flush plate face of area \(0.50\,\mathrm{m^2}\), and measures \(0.180\,\mathrm N\). The mean shear is \(0.360\,\mathrm{Pa}\), hence \(\bar C_f=0.00600\) and \(\bar h\simeq36.0\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}K^{-1}}\). A uniform wall 20 K above the stream loses approximately \(36(0.50)(20)=360\,\mathrm W\) by convection. Average drag produces an average coefficient because both quantities are integrated over the same face. Pressure drag from a protruding instrument, an unheated entry length or a fluid with a very different Prandtl number would invalidate that identification.

Practise on sheet 3 · How much skin friction is consistent with a cooling requirement?. Further reading · MIT · 16.Unified Thermodynamics and Propulsion · §17.1 The Reynolds Analogy.

A long wire of radius 2 mm is held 50 K above ambient. Insulation of conductivity 0.08 W/(m K) adds radial resistance but increases exterior convection area. Add cylindrical conduction and convection resistances per unit length before converting back to heat rate.
