How a gravity-drained condensate film limits heat transfer · Thermal processes and transport in engineering

Saturated vapour condenses on a cool vertical plate. A liquid film thickens as it drains, adding thermal resistance toward the bottom. Derive the local heat-transfer coefficient from coupled momentum and energy balances, then determine which observations would invalidate the smooth-film model.

Chapter 6 · Thermal diffusion and transient surface cooling

Use diffusion time, Biot number and penetration depth to choose between a uniform-body model and a spatial temperature field.

From first principles

Derive thermal diffusion from stored energy and conductive flux

Let a stationary homogeneous solid have density \(\rho\), specific heat \(c\) and thermal conductivity \(k\). Assume no heat generation and consider one-dimensional temperature variation. A slice of area \(A\) and thickness \(dx\) stores energy at rate \(\rho c A\,dx\,\partial_tT\). The difference between the conductive flux entering and leaving gives

\[\rho c A\,dx\,\partial_tT=A[q''(x)-q''(x+dx)]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \rho c\,\partial_tT=-\partial_xq'' .\]

Fourier's law \(q''=-k\partial_xT\) closes this balance. For constant properties the diffusion equation and its characteristic time over distance \(L\) follow:

\[\partial_tT=\alpha\,\partial_{xx}T,\]
\[\alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c},\qquad t_{\rm diff}\sim L^2/\alpha .\]

Initial temperatures and boundary heat-flux or temperature conditions are needed to select a specific solution. If conduction inside the solid is sufficiently rapid relative to transfer at its surface, one can integrate the balance using an almost uniform interior temperature. Comparing the internal and external resistance scales yields the Biot number \(hL_c/k\) used for that simplification.

Background · Biot number
\[\mathrm{Bi}=hL_c/k\]

Here h is a surface heat-transfer coefficient and k is conductivity. A small Biot number supports a nearly uniform specimen temperature for the specified boundary condition.

Derivation and applications

Predict how a thermal probe follows a changing fluid temperature

A sensor calibrated by an abrupt temperature step can lag behind a fluid that is itself heating steadily. Take a body of mass \(m\), heat capacity \(c\), exposed area \(A\) and film coefficient \(h\) with negligible internal temperature gradients. This lumped description requires internal conduction to be fast compared with surface heat transfer. Treat properties and \(h\) as constant and neglect self-heating, radiation and lead conduction. Denote the body temperature by \(T\), fluid temperature by \(T_f\), and set \(\tau=mc/(hA)\).

An instantaneous energy balance gives a first-order differential equation. For a linearly increasing fluid temperature \(T_f=T_0+\beta t\), define the lag \(D=T_f-T\):

\[mc\,\dot T=hA(T_f-T),\]
\[\dot D + D/\tau=\beta .\]

Suppose the probe and fluid start in equilibrium at \(T_0\). Multiply the lag equation by \(e^{t/\tau}\) and integrate from zero. The integrating factor converts the left side to a single derivative:

\[e^{t/\tau}D(t)=\beta\int_0^t e^{s/\tau}\,ds=\beta\tau(e^{t/\tau}-1).\]

Subtract the resulting lag from the prescribed fluid history to obtain the reading:

\[T(t)=T_0+\beta[t-\tau(1-e^{-t/\tau})] .\]

For \(t\ll\tau\) the rise begins quadratically, since the initial film temperature difference was zero. For \(t\gg\tau\) the probe warms at the same rate as the fluid but reads low by \(\beta\tau\). Cooling ramps reverse the sign. A measured constant lag is therefore evidence for this model only after the startup transient has decayed.

Worked example

Evaluate the reading during a ramp calibration

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

A probe has \(mc=0.800\) J/K and \(hA=0.100\) W/K, hence \(\tau=8.00\) s. Start at \(20.0^\circ\)C and heat the fluid by \(0.500\) K/s. At \(t=16.0\) s the fluid is at \(28.0^\circ\)C and the lag is \(4.00(1-e^{-2})=3.46\) K, so the probe reads \(24.5^\circ\)C. Simply subtracting the eventual \(4.00\) K would overcorrect during startup.

Second application

Estimate a time constant without an abrupt temperature step

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

During a long calibration ramp of \(0.200\) K/s, a second probe remains \(1.20\) K below an independent fluid reference. Its inferred time constant is

\[\tau=1.20/0.200=6.00\,\mathrm{s}.\]

If the fluid is then held fixed, the remaining lag decays exponentially. Waiting \(6.00\ln(1.20/0.100)=14.9\) s brings the error below \(0.100\) K. The hold and the ramp test two different responses using the same fitted parameter.

Try it · Probe ramp

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Allow energy to accumulate in the material

A surface thermocouple responds rapidly to a cold wash, while the interior of a thick part can remain hot. To predict the time needed to cool the centre, steady thermal resistance alone is insufficient. Enclose a differential slice of material. Incoming conductive heat minus outgoing heat plus local generation equals the rate of change of its stored energy. Substituting Fourier’s law in a homogeneous stationary solid gives

\[\rho c_p\frac{\partial T}{\partial t}=k\nabla^2T+\dot q_v,\]
\[\alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c_p}.\]

Thermal diffusivity \(\alpha\) in m²/s compares a material’s ability to spread heat with its ability to store it. A large conductivity does not alone guarantee fast adjustment if volumetric heat capacity is also large. Without heat generation, a length \(L\) gives a characteristic diffusion time \(t_d=L^2/\alpha\). The dimensionless Fourier number \(Fo=\alpha t/L^2\) measures how long diffusion has acted relative to that scale.

Decide whether the interior stays nearly uniform

At a convecting boundary \(-k\partial T/\partial n=h(T_s-T_\infty)\). Comparing the resistance of the interior with a fluid film leads to \(Bi=hL_c/k\), often using \(L_c=V/A_s\) for a lumped model. If this ratio is small, commonly below about 0.1 as a first engineering check, the internal temperature difference is small compared with that across the film. Integrating energy over a uniform body then gives

\[\rho c_p V\,dT/dt=-hA_s(T-T_\infty),\]
\[(T-T_\infty)/(T_0-T_\infty)=e^{-t/\tau},\]

with \(\tau=\rho c_pV/(hA_s)\).

The Biot and Fourier numbers answer different questions. A small Fourier number says a disturbance has penetrated only a short distance compared with \(L\). A small Biot number says the boundary transfers heat slowly enough for internal gradients to remain small. Choose the length for each comparison explicitly, especially for a slab cooled on one side rather than both.

Solve a sudden prescribed-temperature change

Consider initially uniform material occupying \(x>0\) whose surface is suddenly held at \(T_s\), with its far interior still at \(T_i\). Neglect internal generation and use constant properties. The initial and boundary conditions have no geometric length scale. Try the similarity coordinate \(\eta=x/(2\sqrt{\alpha t})\) and set \(\Theta=(T-T_s)/(T_i-T_s)\). Differentiating and substituting into the diffusion equation yields \(\Theta''+2\eta\Theta'=0\), with derivatives in \(\eta\). Integrating once gives

\[\Theta'=C e^{-\eta^2}.\]

The limits \(\Theta(0)=0,\ \Theta(\infty)=1\) give

\[\Theta=\operatorname{erf}(\eta).\]

At \(x=0\) the imposed surface temperature holds exactly. At a fixed depth and very early time \(\eta\) is large, so the temperature tends to its initial value. The distance of any fixed fractional temperature change grows as \(\sqrt{t}\). Quadrupling exposure time doubles that distance, a scaling quite different from a body travelling at a constant speed.

Predict depth and validate the time window

A thick solid initially at 373 K is held at 293 K at its surface. With \(\alpha=1.0\times10^{-5}\) m²/s and \(t=10\) s, \(2\sqrt{\alpha t}=0.020\) m. At depth 10 mm, \(\eta=0.50\) and \(\operatorname{erf}(0.50)=0.5205\), giving \(T=293+80(0.5205)=334.6\) K. At 20 mm, \(\operatorname{erf}(1)=0.8427\) gives 360.4 K. The surface has cooled by 80 K while the deeper sensor has cooled by only 12.6 K.

At 40 s the 20 mm sensor reaches the same fraction previously measured at 10 mm. This prediction assumes the remote boundary has not begun to influence the measurement and that the surface temperature is actually prescribed. A finite slab or a convective wash may require a finite-domain diffusion solution instead. Record surface and interior histories to distinguish uncertain boundary conditions from uncertain diffusivity.

Interpret a penetration depth

If two embedded sensors are at 2 and 6 mm in the same semi-infinite specimen, the deeper sensor reaches the same normalised temperature at nine times the shallow sensor’s time because \(x/\sqrt{\alpha t}\) must match. An observed factor much smaller than nine can indicate a heat leak along a probe or a far boundary that has begun to influence the temperature field.

Resolve a slab with a finite surface film

A prescribed surface temperature and a uniformly cooling lump are two useful limits. A finite slab exchanging heat through a finite film generally satisfies neither. Let \(L\) be half-thickness, \(x=0\) the midplane, and \(T_i\) the initial uniform temperature. Both sides suddenly see fluid at \(T_\infty\) with constant \(h\). Using \(X=x/L,\ Fo=\alpha t/L^2\) and \(\Theta=(T-T_\infty)/(T_i-T_\infty)\), the diffusion equation becomes

\[\Theta_{Fo}=\Theta_{XX},\]

with symmetry \(\Theta_X(0)=0\) and film condition \(-\Theta_X(1)=Bi\,\Theta(1)\), where \(Bi=hL/k\).

Seek a separated mode \(\Theta=\cos(\lambda X)e^{-\lambda^2Fo}\). Its derivative vanishes at the midplane. Imposing the convective surface condition gives

\[\lambda\tan\lambda=Bi.\]

The uniform initial condition fixes modal amplitudes by cosine orthogonality on \(0<X<1\):

\[\Theta=\sum_n A_n\cos(\lambda_n X)e^{-\lambda_n^2Fo},\]
\[A_n=\frac{4\sin\lambda_n}{2\lambda_n+\sin(2\lambda_n)}.\]

For \(Bi\ll1\), the smallest root has \(\lambda_1^2\simeq Bi\), its shape approaches a constant, and \(A_1\simeq1\). The decay becomes \(e^{-Bi\,Fo}\), exactly the lumped time scale because \(Bi\,Fo=h t/(\rho cL)\). For a large film coefficient the first root tends towards \(\pi/2\), corresponding to a prescribed surface temperature. The same boundary-value model thus recovers both limiting behaviours.

Predict when the core and surface meet different targets

A 40 mm thick flat slab with \(k=2.0\,\mathrm{W/(m\,K)}\) and \(\alpha=1.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\) is cooled on both sides by \(h=50\,\mathrm{W/(m^2K)}\). It starts at 100 °C in fluid at 20 °C. Here \(L=0.020\) m and \(Bi=0.50\). The first root of \(\lambda\tan\lambda=0.5\) is 0.6533 and \(A_1=1.070\). After 600 s, \(Fo=1.50\). The first term gives centre excess ratio \(1.070\exp[-(0.6533)^2(1.50)]=0.564\), hence \(T_c=65.1^\circ\mathrm C\). At the surface multiply by \(\cos(0.6533)=0.794\), giving about 55.9 °C. Higher roots are strongly attenuated at this Fourier number.

A process requiring the centre to fall below 50 °C needs centre ratio \((50-20)/(100-20)=0.375\). Solve \(Fo=\ln(1.070/0.375)/0.6533^2=2.46\), or approximately 984 s. By contrast a lumped estimate gives \(Fo=-\ln(0.375)/0.5=1.96\), about 785 s. It underestimates the core cooling time by over three minutes because the film and internal gradients both matter. An early-time surface prediction requires more modes than this late-time centre criterion. In particular the first term alone gives a centre ratio above unity at time zero, signalling that the remaining terms are necessary to reconstruct the original uniform state.

If product quality depends on average energy content rather than the hottest spot, average the first-mode shape over half the thickness. Its factor \(\int_0^1\cos(\lambda_1 X)dX=\sin\lambda_1/\lambda_1=0.930\) gives mean excess ratio \(0.564(0.930)=0.525\) after 600 s. The predicted bulk temperature is therefore about 62.0 °C, between centre and surface as expected. A calorimetric measurement made after removing and mixing the material is closer to the mean than to either point thermometer. A decision to stop cooling based on such a reading can therefore satisfy an average enthalpy requirement while leaving the centre several kelvins above a local temperature limit.

The surface flux can be checked against the rate of thermal energy loss. At 600 seconds each face transfers \(50(55.9-20)=1795\,\mathrm{W/m^2}\) to the air. Per square metre of plan area the slab therefore loses approximately 3590 W. The volumetric heat capacity obtained independently from \(k/\alpha\) is \(2.0\times10^6\,\mathrm{J/(m^3K)}\). Multiplying by total 0.040 m thickness gives an areal capacity of 80000 J/(m² K), and a predicted instantaneous mean cooling rate \(3590/80000=0.0449\,\mathrm{K/s}\). Differentiating the one-term mean temperature gives the same decay rate to within rounding.

This independent balance is especially useful if measurements suggest two different film coefficients on opposite sides. An unequal pair of measured fluxes cannot be fitted by a strictly symmetric half-slab model even if it reproduces one centre temperature. The full-thickness conduction problem then requires both surface boundary conditions; simply replacing them by their arithmetic mean may match total heat over one interval while shifting the hottest location away from the midplane.

Periodic heating as a measurement of thermal diffusivity · Thermal processes and transport in engineering

Two buried thermometers see the same applied surface temperature oscillation with different amplitudes and phases. Derive how a solid filters that oscillation, recover diffusivity independently from both observations and identify when a rear boundary or a poor sensor contact changes the inference.

Problem sheet 3 · Heat paths and temperature histories

Identify which geometry sets a thermal resistance, and decide whether a transient can be represented by one temperature.

A wall with two films

A flat panel of area 2.0 m² and thickness 40 mm has conductivity 0.080 W/(m K). Inside and outside air are at 22 and 2 °C; film coefficients are 8 and 20 W/(m² K). Find the heat rate and both surface temperatures. Which design change would halve the rate at fixed film coefficients?

Combine the three resistances

The films give 0.0625 and 0.0250 K/W. The solid contributes \(0.040/(0.080\times2)=0.250\,\mathrm{K/W}\). Hence

\[Q=20/0.3375=59.26\,\mathrm W.\]

The inside face is \(22-59.26(0.0625)=18.30\) °C, the outside face \(2+59.26(0.025)=3.48\) °C. Halving the rate requires 0.3375 K/W of extra resistance, or 54 mm more of this material over the same area. Doubling just the existing insulation gives a smaller reduction.

Time to reach a handling temperature

A metal sphere of radius 12 mm cools in air at 20 °C from 140 to 50 °C. Its conductivity is 45 W/(m K), volumetric heat capacity 3.7 MJ/(m³ K), and \(h=35\,\mathrm{W/(m^2 K)}\). Assess the uniform-temperature approximation, estimate cooling time and determine the new time if the initial temperature becomes 80 °C.

Select a length and integrate

For a sphere \(V/A=R/3=0.004\,\mathrm m\), giving \(\mathrm{Bi}=35(0.004)/45=0.00311\). The time constant is

\[3.7\times10^6(0.004)/35=422.9\,\mathrm s.\]

The original excess falls from 120 to 30 K, taking \(\tau\ln4=586.2\,\mathrm s\). With a 60 K initial excess it takes \(\tau\ln2=293.1\,\mathrm s\). The time constant is unchanged under the given constant-property conditions.

Reproduce a centreline temperature

Two thick slabs have half-thicknesses 10 and 25 mm and the same diffusivity \(8.0\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2/s}\). Each starts at the same uniform temperature and suddenly acquires the same prescribed surface temperatures on both faces. If the thinner slab reaches a given centreline temperature after 18 s, find the corresponding Fourier number and the time for the thicker slab. What if surface temperatures are replaced by convective boundary conditions?

Match dimensionless time and boundary data

The thinner slab gives

\[\mathrm{Fo}=\alpha t/L^2=1.44.\]

Matching it gives

\[t_2=18(25/10)^2=112.5\,\mathrm s.\]

The surfaces and initial profile have the same dimensionless temperatures. For convective cooling the dimensionless temperature history also depends on \(hL/k\), so equal Fourier number alone is insufficient. With the same material, the thicker slab needs a film coefficient \(10/25\) times that of the thinner specimen to match Biot number.

How much skin friction is consistent with a cooling requirement?

An isothermal face of area \(0.20\,\mathrm{m^2}\) must lose 150 W to a \(Pr\simeq1\) stream. The allowable wall excess is 15 K,

\[c_p=1000\,\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}K^{-1}},\]
\[U=8.0\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.\]

Estimate the required area-averaged coefficient and mean skin-friction force. The balance reports 0.110 N, of which 0.040 N is support pressure drag. Estimate the achievable heat rate.

Show the worked solution

The target coefficient is

\[150/(0.20\times15)=50\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}K^{-1}}.\]

The corresponding wall stress is \(hU/c_p=0.400\,\mathrm{Pa}\), or \(0.0800\,\mathrm N\) on this face. After subtracting support drag the measured skin force is \(0.0700\,\mathrm N\), giving \(\bar\tau=0.350\,\mathrm{Pa}\) and \(\bar h\simeq43.75\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}K^{-1}}\). At 15 K excess the predicted heat rate is \(131.3\,\mathrm W\), roughly 19 W below target. Total balance force would overestimate cooling because pressure drag lacks the wall-gradient relation used in the analogy.

Revisit chapter 5 · Infer wall cooling from a measured skin-friction force.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Steady conduction, thermal resistance and insulation; Thermal diffusion and transient surface cooling. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Chapter 7 · Phase equilibrium and steam quality

Locate a two-phase state from two independent measurements and use mass fractions to find its volume and energy.

From first principles

Derive the slope of a pure-substance coexistence line

At liquid–vapour equilibrium, transferring a small amount of matter between phases cannot lower the total Gibbs energy. For a pure substance this requires equality of the specific Gibbs energies \(g_f=g_g\) at common temperature and pressure. To find how pressure must change when temperature changes while maintaining coexistence, differentiate this equality along the saturation curve. The fundamental differential \(dg=-s\,dT+v\,dp\) gives

\[-s_f\,dT+v_f\,dp=-s_g\,dT+v_g\,dp .\]

Rearranging expresses the measured slope as the entropy change of vaporization divided by its specific-volume change. Reversible phase change at constant temperature absorbs specific enthalpy \(h_{fg}=T(s_g-s_f)\), so

\[\left(\frac{dp}{dT}\right)_{\rm sat}=\frac{s_g-s_f}{v_g-v_f}=\frac{h_{fg}}{T(v_g-v_f)} .\]

The units are Pa/K. No ideal-gas approximation has been made. If the vapour is dilute and the liquid volume negligible, further substitution can simplify the slope, but this fails near the critical point. The slope describes neighbouring coexistence states; determining a system’s vapour mass fraction additionally requires an extensive property such as specific enthalpy or volume.

Background · Vapour quality
\[x=m_v/(m_v+m_l)\]

This mass fraction applies in a two-phase equilibrium region. Mole fraction, mass fraction and fraction of volume occupied by vapour can be very different.

Derivation and applications

Trace an isentropic pressure change through the saturation dome

A vapour fraction inferred from one property must reproduce the others at the same pressure. Consider a pure substance in local liquid–vapour equilibrium. Let \(x\) be the vapour mass fraction and use subscripts \(f,g\) for saturated liquid and vapour at the current pressure. Neglect interfacial contributions and assume each phase has the tabulated saturation state. Specific extensive properties then obey the lever rule because their totals add over the masses of both phases:

\[s=(1-x)s_f+xs_g=s_f+xs_{fg},\]
\[h=h_f+xh_{fg}.\]

In a steady adiabatic reversible expansion the entropy balance gives

\[s_2=s_1.\]

At the specified exit pressure solve the entropy relation before using the enthalpy relation:

\[x_{2s}=(s_1-s_{f,2})/s_{fg,2},\]
\[h_{2s}=h_{f,2}+x_{2s}h_{fg,2}.\]

This interpolation is physical only when \(0\le x_{2s}\le1\). For an actual adiabatic turbine with negligible kinetic-energy change, use isentropic efficiency \(\eta_t=(h_1-h_2)/(h_1-h_{2s})\) and check the actual phase state separately:

\[h_2=h_1-\eta_t(h_1-h_{2s}),\]
\[x_2=(h_2-h_{f,2})/h_{fg,2}\]

if the actual outlet is still a mixture. Irreversibility reduces extracted work, which can leave the exhaust drier at the same pressure. A drier exit therefore does not by itself demonstrate higher efficiency.

Worked example

Infer equilibrium quality for two expansion histories

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

At a low-pressure outlet take rounded steam properties \(h_f=192\), \(h_{fg}=2393\) kJ/kg, \(s_f=0.649\) and \(s_{fg}=7.50\) kJ/(kg K). Steam enters with \(h_1=3200\) kJ/kg and \(s_1=6.65\) kJ/(kg K). The reversible quality is \((6.65-0.649)/7.50=0.800\), giving \(h_{2s}=2107\) kJ/kg. For \(\eta_t=0.800\), the actual exit enthalpy is about \(2326\) kJ/kg and the quality \(0.892\). The actual specific work is about \(874\) kJ/kg.

Second application

Detect when a reported quality has no physical meaning

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

At the same outlet pressure an independent calculation gives \(h_2=2630\) kJ/kg. Interpolating would give

\[x=(2630-192)/2393=1.019.\]

Since the saturated vapour enthalpy is only \(2585\) kJ/kg, the enthalpy lies above the saturation range. The outlet is superheated; finding its temperature requires superheated-vapour properties. Reporting a vapour fraction above unity would hide a change of thermodynamic region.

Try it · Turbine quality

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Temperature can stay fixed while heat enters

In an open pan at a fixed ambient pressure, liquid water begins to boil at a particular temperature. Additional heat can then convert liquid to vapour while the temperature remains close to that value. Specifying both saturation temperature and saturation pressure of a pure substance does not locate a unique mixture state. Along the coexistence curve they are linked, and the fraction of material in each phase supplies the missing information. Outside that curve pressure and temperature can usually be used as independent coordinates for a simple compressible substance.

Define quality \(x=m_g/(m_f+m_g)\) as the vapour mass fraction, using subscripts \(f\) for saturated liquid and \(g\) for saturated vapour. A property such as volume or enthalpy is additive over the two masses when interfacial contributions are negligible. Writing \(V=m_f v_f+m_gv_g\) and dividing by total mass gives the mixing relation. The same derivation applies to specific internal energy, enthalpy and entropy.

\[v=v_f+x(v_g-v_f),\]
\[h=h_f+xh_{fg},\]
\[h_{fg}=h_g-h_f .\]

Quality lies between zero and one in a saturated mixture. A calculated value beyond either endpoint signals a single-phase state or inconsistent measurements. Temperature does not interpolate: both phases in equilibrium have the same temperature.

Distinguish vapour mass from space occupied

A closed vessel contains 2.00 kg of saturated water and steam at approximately 100 kPa in 0.500 m³. Use the supplied rounded values \(v_f=0.00104,\ v_g=1.694\,\mathrm{m^3/kg}\),

\[h_f=417.5,\ h_{fg}=2258\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

The measured specific volume is 0.250 m³/kg. Quality follows as \((0.250-0.00104)/(1.694-0.00104)=0.1471\). Thus vapour mass is 0.294 kg while 1.706 kg remains liquid.

The vapour occupies \(0.2941(1.694)=0.4982\,\mathrm{m^3}\), almost the entire vessel. The liquid occupies roughly 0.0018 m³. Specific enthalpy is

\[417.5+0.1471(2258)=749.6\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Specific internal energy follows from \(u=h-pv=749.6-100(0.250)=724.6\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) when using kPa and m³/kg. A level indicator measures occupied volume; using its reading directly as a mass fraction would give a large error.

Connect latent heat to the coexistence curve

For two pure phases in equilibrium their specific Gibbs free energies are equal. Along the coexistence line this equality persists, so \(dg_f=dg_g\). From \(dg=v\,dp-s\,dT\) one obtains

\[dp_{\rm sat}/dT=(s_g-s_f)/(v_g-v_f)=h_{fg}/[T(v_g-v_f)].\]

At about 373 K using the same values, the slope is

\[2258/[373(1.693)]=3.58\,\mathrm{kPa/K}.\]

A small increase of boiling pressure by 3.6 kPa therefore raises saturation temperature by about one kelvin locally. Larger changes require integrating the changing properties or reading a saturation table rather than treating this local slope as a constant.

Predict flash after throttling

A saturated liquid with inlet enthalpy 500 kJ/kg passes through an insulated valve to the pressure of the drum above. With negligible work and velocity change, enthalpy remains 500 kJ/kg. The downstream quality is therefore

\[(500-417.5)/2258=0.0365.\]

Only about 3.7% of the water flashes by mass, but the large specific volume of the vapour can make its occupied volume dominate the downstream pipe.

Trace a saturated mixture along a constant-volume path

The drum example determines one state. If a sealed rigid vessel is heated, its pressure and temperature change together along saturation while its specific volume remains fixed. Provided equilibrium is maintained and no other phase appears, the quality at every pressure is

\[x=(v-v_f)/(v_g-v_f).\]

The energy equation for the vessel is \(Q=\Delta U\) when it does no boundary or shaft work. Replacing internal-energy change by enthalpy change would add the spurious \(V\Delta p\) term of a rigid container.

Quality does not change solely because latent heat has entered. Differentiate \(v=(1-x)v_f+xv_g\) along the saturation curve at fixed \(v\). This gives

\[\frac{dx}{dT}=-\frac{(1-x)\,dv_f/dT+x\,dv_g/dT}{v_g-v_f}.\]

Saturated liquid usually expands as temperature rises while saturated vapour specific volume decreases over the ordinary water range. The sign therefore depends on the initial mixture and local property slopes. A vessel containing almost all liquid by volume can, under some conditions, follow the saturated-liquid boundary on heating rather than drying out. Determine the actual phase region from the property data after each change. When \(v\) lies outside the interval between \(v_f\) and \(v_g\), a saturated quality formula is no longer applicable.

Find how much energy changes phase in a sealed vessel

Take 1.00 kg of water in a rigid 0.300 m³ vessel. Use rounded saturation data at 100 kPa:

\[v_f=0.00104,\ v_g=1.694\,\mathrm{m^3/kg},\ u_f=417.4,\ u_g=2506\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

At 200 kPa use \(v_f=0.00106,\ v_g=0.8857,\ u_f=504.5,\ u_g=2529\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). The volume bounds contain 0.300 m³/kg at both pressures. Initial quality is \((0.300-0.00104)/(1.694-0.00104)=0.1766\); final quality is

\[(0.300-0.00106)/(0.8857-0.00106)=0.3379.\]

Thus about 0.161 kg additionally evaporates.

Mixture internal energies are \(417.4+0.1766(2506-417.4)=786.3\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) and \(504.5+0.3379(2529-504.5)=1188.6\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), giving heat input about 402 kJ. Merely multiplying evaporated mass by one latent heat would overlook the sensible energy changes in each phase and the variation of saturation properties. As an independent check, \(\Delta H=\Delta U+V\Delta p\) exceeds the required heat by \(0.300(200-100)=30.0\,\mathrm{kJ}\). If additional heating brings saturated vapour specific volume down to 0.300 m³/kg, the last liquid disappears. Beyond that state the fixed-volume path enters the superheated region and one must determine temperature using a single-phase equation of state or superheated-vapour data.

The vapour volume fraction in the second state is \(m x v_g/V=0.3379(0.8857)/0.300=0.998\) approximately. Although about one third of the mass is vapour, almost every part of the vessel volume is vapour-filled. A level probe that detects a very small liquid volume is consistent with the presence of substantial residual liquid mass. This has a direct consequence for rounding: replacing \(v_f\) by zero only gives a modest error in calculated quality here, but using that same approximation to predict the remaining liquid depth gives a nonsensical zero. Retain the finite liquid volume whenever the question concerns an interface height or the mass recovered through a bottom drain.

The units of the property table matter just as much as the phase labels when predicting a small liquid inventory.

Can an equation of state predict energy changes during expansion?

A caloric model \(du=c_v\,dT\) works for an ideal gas. To discover the extra term for a real fluid, let \(v\), \(s\) and \(u\) denote molar volume, entropy and energy for a single homogeneous phase. At a fixed composition the fundamental relation is

\[du=T\,ds-p\,dv.\]

Introduce the Helmholtz energy \(f=u-Ts\). Then \(df=-s\,dT-p\,dv\). Equality of the two mixed partial derivatives of the smooth function \(f(T,v)\) gives the Maxwell relation \((\partial s/\partial v)_T=(\partial p/\partial T)_v\).

Now expand \(ds\) in \(T,v\). The term \(T(\partial s/\partial T)_v\) equals \(c_v\), obtained directly by holding \(v\) fixed in the fundamental relation. Substituting both coefficients gives

\[du=c_v\,dT+\left[T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_v-p\right]\,dv.\]

The bracket has units of pressure. For \(p=RT/v\) it vanishes. For a van der Waals model \(p=RT/(v-b)-a/v^2\), the repulsive volume correction cancels between the two terms and the attraction term leaves \(a/v^2\). Assuming constant \(c_v\), integration yields

\[\Delta u=c_v(T_2-T_1)+a(1/v_1-1/v_2).\]

An insulated, rigid container contains a gas on one side of a removable internal partition and vacuum on the other. Removing the partition causes no external boundary work and exchanges no heat. States before and after settling therefore have equal internal energy. Let \(T_1=300\,\mathrm K\), \(v_1=0.00200\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}\), \(v_2=0.00400\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}\), \(a=0.150\,\mathrm{Pa\,m^6\,mol^{-2}}\), \(b=4.0\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}\) and \(c_v=24.0\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}K^{-1}}\). The volume contribution is

\[0.150(500-250)=37.5\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}.\]

To keep energy constant the temperature falls by \(37.5/24.0=1.56\,\mathrm K\), to \(298.44\,\mathrm K\). The differential identity links the equilibrium endpoints even though the actual expansion was irreversible. The value of \(b\) matters for their pressures and cancels from this energy change.

Practise on sheet 4 · Compare free and isothermal expansion of the same model gas. Further reading · University of Cincinnati · C. Vaz, Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics · §4.2 Maxwell Relations.

Chapter 8 · Gas mixtures, humidity and condensation

Translate relative humidity into a water mass balance and determine how much condensate a cooling coil removes.

From first principles

Obtain humidity ratio from partial pressures and dry-air mass

Treat moist air as an ideal mixture of dry air and water vapour occupying the same volume \(V\) at temperature \(T\). Dalton's law gives total pressure \(p=p_{da}+p_v\). For each component \(m_i=p_iV/(R_iT)\), so dividing vapour mass by dry-air mass cancels the shared volume and temperature:

\[\omega=\frac{m_v}{m_{da}}=\frac{R_{da}}{R_v}\,\frac{p_v}{p-p_v}\simeq0.622\frac{p_v}{p-p_v}.\]

Relative humidity is a different observable. It compares actual vapour partial pressure with the saturation pressure at the same temperature. Cooling without water removal keeps \(\omega\) constant, and approximately constant total pressure then keeps \(p_v\) constant until the saturation curve reaches it:

\[\varphi=p_v/p_{\rm sat}(T),\]
\[p_{\rm sat}(T_{\rm dew})=p_v .\]

Below the dew point some water can leave the gas as liquid, while dry-air mass is conserved. A steady coil receiving humidity ratio \(\omega_i\) and delivering \(\omega_o\) therefore removes condensate at rate \(\dot m_{da}(\omega_i-\omega_o)\). Inferring the same removal from relative humidity alone would ignore its dependence on temperature.

Derivation and applications

Measure the bypass fraction of a cooling and dehumidifying coil

Humid air crossing a coil may leave warmer and wetter than air in equilibrium with the coldest effective surface. A common reduced model treats a fraction \(b\) of the dry-air stream as bypassing the surface while the remainder reaches a saturated state at the apparatus dew-point temperature. Consider a steady coil at nearly constant total pressure, neglect fan work and heat gains, and use dry-air mass as the conserved basis. Denote inlet, saturated-surface and mixed outlet states by \(i,s,o\), with humidity ratio \(\omega\) and moist-air enthalpy \(h\) per kilogram of dry air.

Water-vapour and mixed-stream enthalpy balances give two independent ways to estimate the same bypass fraction:

\[\omega_o=b\omega_i+(1-b)\omega_s,\]
\[h_o=bh_i+(1-b)h_s.\]

Solving either for \(b\) exposes a consistency check on measured data and the assumed single surface state:

\[b=\frac{\omega_o-\omega_s}{\omega_i-\omega_s}=\frac{h_o-h_s}{h_i-h_s}.\]

The condensed water remains a separate outlet from the coil. With condensate leaving at liquid enthalpy \(h_\ell\) in the same reference convention, an overall balance gives positive heat removal

\[\dot m_\ell=\dot m_{da}(\omega_i-\omega_o),\]
\[\dot Q_{\rm out}=\dot m_{da}(h_i-h_o)-\dot m_\ell h_\ell .\]

The mixed-state model requires

\[0\le b\le1.\]

It also requires incoming air wet enough for condensation on the assumed surface. A dry surface with solely sensible cooling cannot be represented by forcing a saturated leaving branch.

Worked example

Evaluate air and water leaving a coil

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Use \(\omega_i=0.0160\), \(\omega_s=0.00760\) kg/kg dry air, \(h_i=71.0\), \(h_s=29.3\) kJ/kg dry air and \(b=0.250\). Then \(\omega_o=0.00970\) and \(h_o=39.7\) kJ/kg dry air. With \(1.20\) kg/s dry air, the condensate rate is \(0.00756\) kg/s. For \(h_\ell=42.0\) kJ/kg, heat removal is

\[1.20(71.0-39.725)-0.00756(42.0)=37.2\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

Second application

Test whether three measurements share one apparatus dew point

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

On another run with the same inlet and assumed surface, measured outlet values are \(\omega_o=0.01096\) and \(h_o=41.8\) kJ/kg dry air. The humidity ratio implies \(b=0.400\), while enthalpy gives \(b=0.300\). At \(b=0.400\) the model would predict \(h_o=46.0\) kJ/kg. The \(4.2\) kJ/kg discrepancy indicates that measurement uncertainty, a more distributed surface temperature or another heat transfer must be considered before inferring a unique bypass fraction.

Try it · Coil bypass

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Choose a quantity that survives condensation

A ventilation duct can carry an unchanged mass flow of dry air while water evaporates into it or condenses out. It is therefore convenient to express water content and mixture energy per kilogram of dry air. Model the dry air and water vapour as ideal gases sharing a common temperature and total volume. The partial pressure of each gas is the pressure it would exert if it occupied this volume alone. Dalton’s law gives

\[p=p_a+p_v.\]

Relative humidity \(\phi=p_v/p_{\rm sat}(T)\) describes proximity to saturation at the current temperature. It is not the mass fraction of water. For humidity ratio \(w=m_v/m_a\), divide the two ideal-gas mass equations. The common temperature and volume cancel, leaving a ratio of molar masses multiplied by the partial-pressure ratio.

\[w=\frac{M_v}{M_a}\frac{p_v}{p-p_v}\simeq0.622\frac{p_v}{p-p_v},\]
\[w_{\rm sat}(T)=0.622\frac{p_{\rm sat}(T)}{p-p_{\rm sat}(T)} .\]

Before any water condenses during cooling at constant total pressure, the humidity ratio and vapour partial pressure remain fixed. Relative humidity rises because saturation pressure falls. The dew point is the temperature at which saturation pressure equals that fixed vapour pressure. Once that point is crossed, further cooling can remove water.

Find water removal and the associated cooling duty

Air enters a coil at 30 °C, 100 kPa and 60% relative humidity, and leaves saturated at 12 °C. Use \(p_{\rm sat}(30)=4.24\) kPa and \(p_{\rm sat}(12)=1.40\) kPa. The inlet vapour partial pressure is 2.544 kPa, giving \(w_1=0.01624\) kg water per kg dry air. The outlet ratio is \(w_2=0.00883\). For dry-air flow 0.50 kg/s the condensate flow is \(0.50(0.01624-0.00883)=0.00371\,\mathrm{kg/s}\), about 13.3 kg/h.

Using a 0 °C liquid-water reference, approximate moist-air enthalpy per kilogram dry air by \(h=1.005T+w(2501+1.86T)\) kJ/kg dry air with \(T\) in °C. The duct streams carry about 71.7 and 34.3 kJ/kg dry air. Condensate leaving at 12 °C carries \(h_l\simeq4.18(12)=50.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Energy conservation gives heat removed per kilogram dry air as \(h_1-h_2-(w_1-w_2)h_l\), approximately 37.0 kJ/kg dry air, or 18.5 kW.

Separate humidity ratio from relative humidity

Suppose the outlet stream is reheated to 20 °C without adding water. Its vapour pressure remains 1.40 kPa at the same total pressure. Since \(p_{\rm sat}(20)=2.34\,\mathrm{kPa}\), the new relative humidity is 59.8%. Neither evaporating water nor drying the air was required during this reheat. A sensor reporting 60% at both the inlet and reheated outlet therefore describes two air streams containing rather different masses of water. The coil water balance, written on the dry-air basis, explains the apparent coincidence.

Bracket an unknown dew point

At the original inlet the vapour pressure was 2.544 kPa. Suppose a saturation table gives 2.487 kPa at 21 °C and 2.644 kPa at 22 °C. Linear interpolation over this short interval puts the dew point at \(21+(2.544-2.487)/(2.644-2.487)=21.36^\circ\mathrm C\). A coil surface warmer than this value cannot start condensing the entering air locally under the stated conditions.

Balance an adiabatic spray chamber

A direct evaporative cooler behaves differently from a refrigerated cooling coil. Water is added to an airstream, the dry-bulb temperature falls, and the humidity ratio increases. Assume steady operation, negligible shaft work and heat leakage, unchanged total pressure, and no liquid droplets carried out. Express enthalpy per kilogram dry air. The dry-air flow is conserved; added liquid flow per kilogram dry air equals \(w_2-w_1\). The resulting energy balance is

\[h_1+(w_2-w_1)h_{\ell,\rm in}=h_2.\]

Use the same enthalpy reference for liquid and moist air. If the added water has low liquid enthalpy on that reference, the moist-air enthalpy is approximately constant. Often the recirculated liquid is near the leaving-air temperature; the more accurate energy equation above can be used without that approximation. An ideal infinitely long saturated contactor has \(\phi_2=1\), but a finite cooler generally leaves unsaturated air. An outlet temperature or a contact effectiveness is needed in addition to the balances unless saturation is specified.

With constant heat capacities one may write \(h_2=c_{pa}T_2+w_2(h_{g0}+c_{pv}T_2)\). Substituting this form into the balance and grouping the unknown \(w_2\) shows explicitly which measured quantities are needed. The process cannot cool below the adiabatic saturation limit for the given inlet state and water supply merely by increasing the spray rate.

Check outlet humidity and water consumption

A cooler receives air at 35 °C and 100 kPa with inlet humidity ratio 0.0080 kg/kg dry air and dry-air flow 0.60 kg/s. Its measured outlet temperature is 23 °C and liquid makeup enters at 20 °C. Use \(h=1.005T+w(2501+1.86T)\) kJ/kg dry air and \(h_\ell=4.18T\) kJ/kg. The inlet carries \(1.005(35)+0.008[2501+1.86(35)]=55.70\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) dry air; liquid enters with \(h_\ell=83.6\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\).

The outlet equation is \(55.70+(w_2-0.0080)83.6=1.005(23)+w_2[2501+1.86(23)]\), giving \(w_2=0.01297\,\mathrm{kg/kg}\) dry air. Thus liquid consumption is \(0.60(0.01297-0.0080)=0.00298\,\mathrm{kg/s}\), or about 10.7 kg/h. The outlet vapour pressure is

\[100(0.01297)/(0.622+0.01297)=2.04\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

At a supplied saturation pressure of 2.81 kPa for 23 °C, this corresponds to about 73% relative humidity, consistent with a finite contact process.

Treating air as a dry sensible stream would suggest a cooling duty of roughly \(0.60(1.005)(35-23)=7.24\,\mathrm{kW}\) removed from the cabinet. In this adiabatic device most of the sensible decrease supplies evaporation inside the boundary; no corresponding cooling-coil duty exists. A downstream process sensitive to water content may therefore react very differently to evaporative cooling and cooling plus dehumidification, even when both report the same outlet dry-bulb temperature.

An independent check can be made by weighing the makeup reservoir over a stable ten-minute test. At the predicted consumption it should lose about \(10.7/6=1.78\) kg, provided no liquid is stored in the spray circuit during that interval. A greater measured loss together with the same outlet humidity suggests entrained droplets, drainage or leakage rather than necessarily more evaporation. A humidity probe downstream of an effective droplet separator and a separate liquid-drain measurement distinguish those paths and make the water balance close.

Identify condensation from a humidity-ratio chart

At a total pressure of 101.325 kPa,

\[w=0.62198\,p_v/(p-p_v),\]
\[p_v=\phi\,p_{\rm sat}(T).\]

The plot uses the Buck saturation-pressure correlation over liquid water. Follow a sealed parcel cooled at constant pressure with no condensation until it reaches saturation.

Problem sheet 4 · Saturation and moist air

Infer phase mass fractions from measured properties and use dry-air balances through cooling and mixing processes.

A vessel containing liquid and vapour

At a specified saturation pressure use \(v_f=0.0011,\ v_g=0.900\,\mathrm{m^3/kg}\),

\[h_f=505,\ h_{fg}=2200\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

A 0.180 m³ vessel contains 1.0 kg in equilibrium. Determine quality, vapour volume fraction and specific enthalpy. Does its liquid mass or vapour mass dominate?

Reveal the worked solution

Quality is

\[(0.180-0.0011)/(0.900-0.0011)=0.1990.\]

The vapour occupies \(0.1990(0.900)=0.1791\,\mathrm{m^3}\), approximately 99.5% of the volume. Enthalpy is

\[505+0.1990(2200)=942.8\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

The liquid still supplies about 80.1% of the mass.

Establish a condensate flow

An air stream at 100 kPa total pressure has vapour partial pressure 2.80 kPa. Cooling reduces this to 1.20 kPa through condensation. For a dry-air flow of 0.80 kg/s determine both humidity ratios, hourly condensate flow and relative humidity after reheating to a state with saturation pressure 3.00 kPa.

Reveal the worked solution

The ratios are \(0.622(2.8)/(100-2.8)=0.01792\) and \(0.622(1.2)/(100-1.2)=0.00755\) kg/kg dry air. Condensate flow is

\[0.80(0.01792-0.00755)3600=29.9\,\mathrm{kg/h}.\]

Reheating at fixed composition and total pressure preserves vapour partial pressure, giving a final relative humidity of \(1.20/3.00=40\%\).

Predict an outlet humidity

Two streams at the same pressure carry 0.30 and 0.70 kg/s of dry air with humidity ratios 0.006 and 0.016 kg/kg dry air. Mixed air leaves at 100 kPa and a temperature with saturation pressure 2.60 kPa. Find outlet humidity ratio, vapour pressure and relative humidity. Verify that the assumption of no condensation is consistent.

Reveal the worked solution

Dry-air flow is 1.00 kg/s and water flow \(0.30(0.006)+0.70(0.016)=0.0130\,\mathrm{kg/s}\). The humidity ratio is 0.0130. Solving \(w=0.622p_v/(p-p_v)\) gives

\[p_v=100(0.0130)/(0.622+0.0130)=2.047\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

Relative humidity is 78.7%, below saturation at this outlet temperature.

Compare free and isothermal expansion of the same model gas

One mole of gas obeys the van der Waals law with \(a=0.180\,\mathrm{Pa\,m^6\,mol^{-2}}\),

\[b=5.0\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}},\]
\[c_v=25.0\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}K^{-1}}.\]

It expands from 0.0030 to \(0.0060\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}\), initially at 320 K. Find its final temperature for insulated expansion into vacuum. Then calculate the heat needed for reversible isothermal expansion between the same volumes. Use \(R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}K^{-1}}\).

Show the worked solution

The potential-energy contribution is \(0.180(1/0.0030-1/0.0060)=30.0\,\mathrm J\) for the mole. Free expansion has \(\Delta U=0\), hence

\[T_2=320-30/25=318.8\,\mathrm K.\]

Along the isotherm,

\[\Delta U=30.0\,\mathrm J.\]

The reversible work done by the gas is \(\int p\,dv=RT\ln[(v_2-b)/(v_1-b)]-a(1/v_1-1/v_2)\). The first law gives

\[Q=\Delta U+W=8.314(320)\ln(0.00595/0.00295)\simeq1.87\,\mathrm{kJ}.\]

Here the reservoir supplies heat while the gas performs boundary work. Equal volume endpoints alone do not determine the heat transfer.

Chapter 9 · Useful work and exergy destruction

Evaluate the useful-work potential of a steady stream and distinguish energy conservation from the destruction of useful-work potential.

From first principles

Derive maximum useful work relative to an environment

For a closed body changing toward equilibrium with surroundings at \(T_0,p_0\), consider the reversible path that gives the largest possible useful work. Neglect bulk kinetic and gravitational energy. With heat \(Q_0\) positive into the body and total boundary plus shaft work \(W\) positive out, the first law gives

\[W=U_i-U_f+Q_0.\]

Reversible heat exchange with this environment requires

\[Q_0=T_0(S_f-S_i).\]

Part of total work simply displaces ambient pressure, so

\[W_{\rm useful,rev}=U_i-U_f+T_0(S_f-S_i)-p_0(V_f-V_i).\]

Choose the final state as the dead state, denoted by subscript zero. Grouping the available initial differences defines nonflow physical exergy:

\[B=(U-U_0)+p_0(V-V_0)-T_0(S-S_0).\]

If the process generates entropy, the same first and second law combination lowers the useful work by \(T_0S_{\rm gen}\). The expression depends on the specified surrounding state and assumes no other heat reservoirs or chemical changes. A stream crossing a control volume carries its pressure flow work as well, giving a distinct enthalpy-based expression for flow exergy.

Background · Exergy destruction
\[\dot X_{\rm dest}=T_0\dot S_{\rm gen}\]

The environmental reference temperature enters in kelvin. Irreversibility destroys useful-work potential even when the total energy balance closes.

Derivation and applications

Derive the work potential of a compressed flowing gas

A stream can deliver useful work by cooling or by expanding, even when no fuel or chemical reaction is involved. Choose a steady reference environment at \(T_0,p_0\) with the same gas composition. Consider one kilogram taken reversibly from its inlet state to the environmental state. Neglect kinetic and potential energy. Let \(q_0\) be heat supplied from the environment, positive into the device, and \(w\) shaft work delivered. The steady energy balance includes flow work in enthalpy:

\[w=h-h_0+q_0 .\]

The only external heat exchange occurs with the environment. Reversibility then gives \(s_0-s=q_0/T_0\), eliminating the unmeasured heat transfer. The maximum work is the specific physical flow exergy

\[b=(h-h_0)-T_0(s-s_0).\]

For an ideal gas with constant \(c_p\) and \(R\), insert \(dh=c_p\,dT\) and \(ds=c_p\,dT/T-R\,dp/p\). Integrate relative to \(T_0,p_0\):

\[b=c_p[(T-T_0)-T_0\ln(T/T_0)]+RT_0\ln(p/p_0).\]

The pressure term survives at ambient temperature. Near \(T=T_0\) the thermal term is quadratic in the temperature difference because energy and the ambient heat contribution cancel to first order. The formula describes work relative to a specified reference and assumes the inlet and outlet composition is the same. An actual dissipative path destroys part of the reversible work potential.

Worked example

Estimate the work available from warm compressed air

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Take air at \(T=360\) K, \(p=300\) kPa entering an environment at \(T_0=300\) K and \(p_0=100\) kPa. With \(c_p=1.005\) and \(R=0.287\) kJ/(kg K), the thermal contribution is \(1.005[60-300\ln1.2]=5.33\) kJ/kg. The pressure contribution is \(0.287(300)\ln3=94.6\) kJ/kg. Total available work is approximately \(99.9\) kJ/kg, mostly associated with pressure.

Second application

Account for loss in an ambient-temperature throttle

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Let \(0.100\) kg/s of ideal air throttle adiabatically from \(300\) to \(100\) kPa, both at \(300\) K. Enthalpy stays constant, no shaft work is extracted, and the exergy destruction is \(0.100(94.6)=9.46\) kW. Independent entropy accounting gives \(\dot S_{\rm gen}=\dot mR\ln3=0.0315\) kW/K. Its product with the ambient \(300\) K recovers the same \(9.46\) kW. A device balancing energy perfectly can therefore expend substantial work potential.

Try it · Flow exergy

How much work is lost when two finite bodies touch?

Two insulated metal blocks start at different temperatures. A small cyclic heat engine could transfer heat from one to the other and deliver shaft work until the temperatures equalise. Simply bringing the blocks into thermal contact also gives a common final temperature, but it conserves a different quantity. Calculate the two end states before estimating how much work the initial temperature difference made available.

Assume constant positive heat capacities \(C_h,C_c\), no phase change, no external heat exchange and no mechanical work by either block. In direct passive contact no outside work is produced. The total energy change is therefore zero, giving \(T_{\rm mix}=(C_hT_h+C_cT_c)/(C_h+C_c)\). If the intervening engine instead operates reversibly, each small entropy transfer leaving the hot block equals the entropy entering the cold one. At the final common temperature \(T_f\),

\[C_h\ln(T_f/T_h)+C_c\ln(T_f/T_c)=0,\]
\[T_f=\exp\left[\frac{C_h\ln T_h+C_c\ln T_c}{C_h+C_c}\right].\]

Here all temperatures are absolute. More formally the logarithms can be taken of ratios to the same reference temperature; that reference cancels. The weighted geometric mean lies below the weighted arithmetic mean. The reversible final state thus contains less internal energy than the passive final state. The difference has emerged as work,

\[W_{\max}=(C_h+C_c)(T_{\rm mix}-T_f).\]

An irreversible engine generates entropy and ends at a higher common temperature with smaller available work.

Take two blocks with capacities of 1.2 kJ/K, initially at 360 K and 280 K. Passive contact ends at 320.0 K; the reversible engine ends at \(\sqrt{360\times280}=317.49\) K and delivers 6.02 kJ. Passive contact generates 0.01890 kJ/K of entropy. To visualise the reversible path, let \(s\) be the entropy transferred from one block into the other. The instantaneous temperatures are \(T_h(s)=T_h(0)e^{-s/C_h}\) and \(T_c(s)=T_c(0)e^{s/C_c}\). The next increment of work is \((T_h-T_c)ds\), so the signed area between the two temperature curves reproduces the total work.

The scaling becomes particularly clear for equal capacities \(C\) when the starting temperatures are \(T_m+\Delta\) and \(T_m-\Delta\). The mixed temperature is \(T_m\), while the reversible final temperature is \(T_m\sqrt{1-(\Delta/T_m)^2}\). Expanding the square root for small temperature gaps gives

\[W_{\max}\simeq C\Delta^2/T_m.\]

For \(T_m=320\) K and \(\Delta=40\) K this is 6.0 kJ, close to the exact 6.02 kJ. Halving the temperature gap at the same mean temperature gives roughly a quarter of the work. The energy initially contained in the warmer block above the mean temperature scales only linearly with the gap, so that energy alone is a poor measure of usable work.

If the surroundings never exchange heat with either block, should their temperature appear in this maximum-work calculation?

Work through the solution

The reversible engine can use only the two specified bodies. Their initial states and conservation of their total entropy determine how far the work extraction goes. A different problem allows ultimate equilibration with an external reservoir at \(T_0\). In that enlarged problem the standard exergy destruction associated with the irreversible contact is \(T_0 S_{\rm gen}\). These work comparisons have different permitted end states. The notebook keeps the enclosure insulated and checks the work using the energy balance on both bodies throughout the exchange.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Specify the surroundings before rating a resource

A warm stream contains energy relative to a chosen reference. The amount that could become useful work depends on the surroundings available to receive heat. If stream and environment reach mutual thermal and mechanical equilibrium, there is no further physical work potential associated with their difference. This equilibrium is the physical dead state at \(T_0,p_0\). Exclude chemical reactions here and consider a steady flow with constant composition.

Imagine a reversible device that takes a stream from its current state to the dead state while interacting thermally only with the environment. The steady first law relates delivered work to changes in stream enthalpy, kinetic and potential energy, and heat. The reversible entropy balance gives heat received from the environment \(q_0=T_0(s_0-s)\). Eliminating that heat gives the specific flow exergy

\[\psi=(h-h_0)-T_0(s-s_0)+V^2/2+g(z-z_0).\]

Use consistent units for the kinetic and potential terms. The entropy contribution reduces the fraction of enthalpy that a reversible device can turn into useful work. At the dead state, with the same velocity and elevation as the reference, that work potential vanishes.

Cool a liquid to the environment

Take a stream of liquid water at approximately ambient pressure, 360 K, with negligible motion and elevation change. It flows at 0.20 kg/s towards a large environment at 300 K. Assume \(c_p=4.18\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\) constant. Integration gives

\[h-h_0=c_p(T-T_0),\]
\[s-s_0=c_p\ln(T/T_0).\]

The stream’s specific physical exergy is therefore

\[4.18[60-300\ln(360/300)]=22.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Its enthalpy difference is much larger, 250.8 kJ/kg.

Cooling all the way to 300 K makes at most \(0.20(22.2)=4.44\,\mathrm{kW}\) of useful work available, even though the stream releases 50.16 kW of heat. Direct heat exchange with the environment delivers no shaft work. The environment entropy gain is \(50.16/300=0.1672\,\mathrm{kW/K}\), while the stream loses \(0.20(4.18)\ln(360/300)=0.1524\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). The difference, about 0.0148 kW/K, is the entropy production rate. Multiplication by \(T_0\) gives the lost 4.44 kW of work potential.

Include heat crossing another temperature boundary

Heat \(Q\) supplied reversibly by a reservoir at a uniform boundary temperature \(T_b\) carries exergy \(Q(1-T_0/T_b)\). This follows by comparing its energy with the environmental heat required to discharge the associated entropy \(Q/T_b\). A heat input of 10 kW from a 600 K reservoir in a 300 K environment carries 5 kW of exergy. A transfer from a finite cooling stream has changing source temperature and requires integration, as in the water example. Irreversibility in the device destroys exergy at rate \(T_0\dot S_{\rm gen}\); directing heat to a useful secondary process can retain part of the exergy that direct cooling would destroy.

Include the work potential of pressure

For an ideal gas already at \(T_0\), an elevated pressure still gives physical flow exergy \(RT_0\ln(p/p_0)\). Air at 300 K and twice environmental pressure has about \(0.287(300)\ln2=59.7\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) of pressure-related work potential although its ideal-gas enthalpy equals that at the dead state.

Derive the least work needed to heat a finite stream

Heat pumps are commonly compared by heat delivered per unit of electrical work. A second-law comparison additionally accounts for the temperature at which heat is delivered. Consider a steady stream heated from \(T_i\) to \(T_o\) at nearly environmental pressure, with constant heat capacity rate \(C=\dot m c_p\). A reversible heat pump extracts heat from surroundings at \(T_0\) and delivers it through a temperature-matched sequence of infinitesimal condensers. For one increment \(dQ=C\,dT\) at stream temperature \(T\), the reversed Carnot relation gives

\[dW_{\min}=dQ(1-T_0/T).\]

Integrating over the stream yields

\[W_{\min}=C[(T_o-T_i)-T_0\ln(T_o/T_i)].\]

This is the increase of its physical flow exergy, and is smaller than evaluating the Carnot work factor at the final high temperature for the whole heat duty. A device that condenses at a single temperature above the warmest outlet generates entropy during delivery to the colder portions. The result assumes that the source of heat is the environment at \(T_0\); a colder source would introduce an additional resource requirement.

For steady equipment receiving only electricity and environmental heat, with negligible parasitic flows, a meaningful second-law efficiency is the stream’s exergy increase divided by actual electrical input. Electric power is entirely work potential, whereas a kilowatt of heat near room temperature carries only a small fraction of a kilowatt of exergy.

Compare a heat pump with a resistance heater

Water flows at 0.10 kg/s from 293 K to 333 K in a 293 K environment. With \(c_p=4.18\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), heat duty is \(0.418(40)=16.72\,\mathrm{kW}\). The exergy increase is \(0.418[40-293\ln(333/293)]=1.05\,\mathrm{kW}\) approximately. This relatively small figure reflects the modest average temperature lift. A well-insulated heat pump drawing 4.20 kW from the electrical supply has heating COP \(16.72/4.20=3.98\) and exergy efficiency about 25%. Its surroundings provide 12.52 kW of heat under the stated energy balance.

A resistance heater supplying the identical water service draws 16.72 kW when its losses to the room are negligible. It is nearly 100% efficient on the simple electrical-energy-to-heat basis, yet its exergy efficiency is only about 6.3%. For the heat pump the implied destruction of work potential is \(4.20-1.05=3.15\,\mathrm{kW}\); for resistance heating it is about 15.67 kW. Electricity used by a circulating pump, external heat leaks or a source below environmental temperature belongs in the appropriate device boundary before drawing a practical comparison. The heat-duty equality ensures that the machines are judged against the same delivered temperature service.

For the resistance case the entropy balance can be checked without using the exergy formula. Electrical work carries no entropy across the control surface, and assume no heat leakage. The stream increases its entropy at \(0.418\ln(333/293)=0.0535\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). Multiplying by the 293 K environment gives 15.7 kW destroyed, consistent with the difference between input work and stream exergy gain. This agreement depends on keeping the heater and heated stream in the same chosen boundary.

Chapter 10 · Steam power cycles and turbine efficiency

Close a Rankine cycle energy balance and predict how turbine irreversibility changes power, condenser duty and exhaust quality.

From first principles

Obtain Rankine-cycle efficiency by following one kilogram

Consider a simple steady steam loop with a pump, boiler, turbine and condenser. Number states \(1\) at pump inlet, \(2\) at pump outlet, \(3\) at turbine inlet and \(4\) at turbine exhaust. Neglect kinetic and potential changes and external heat leaks. The steady-flow energy balance for one kilogram through the adiabatic pump and turbine converts their enthalpy changes directly into shaft work; in the boiler and condenser there is no shaft work:

\[w_p=h_2-h_1,\]
\[w_t=h_3-h_4,\]
\[q_{\rm in}=h_3-h_2,\]
\[q_{\rm out}=h_4-h_1 .\]

Subtract the pump input from the turbine output and divide by heat supplied. Rearrangement gives a second expression that checks cycle energy closure:

\[\eta_{\rm th}=\frac{(h_3-h_4)-(h_2-h_1)}{h_3-h_2}=1-\frac{h_4-h_1}{h_3-h_2}.\]

These identities use actual enthalpies. An idealized isentropic turbine supplies \(h_{4s}\) from its outlet pressure and inlet entropy; isentropic efficiency then estimates actual \(h_4\). Liquid pump work often admits the incompressible estimate \(v(p_2-p_1)\), explaining why its enthalpy rise can be much smaller than the turbine enthalpy drop.

Derivation and applications

Close a steam-cycle balance using the condenser cooling-water measurement

The condenser often transfers more heat than the plant delivers in shaft work. Its cooling-water measurement supplies an independent check on assumed steam flow and moisture fraction. Choose a steady control volume enclosing both sides of a surface condenser, with no mixing, negligible heat leaks or pressure losses, and negligible kinetic and potential energy changes. Steam leaves as saturated liquid at the condenser pressure. Cooling water remains a single-phase liquid with constant \(c_w\). Denote steam and cooling-water mass rates by \(\dot m_s,\dot m_w\).

Separate mass balances keep both flow rates constant across their respective sides. The steady energy balance couples the enthalpy lost by steam to the energy gained by water:

\[\dot m_s(h_{\rm ex}-h_f)=\dot m_w c_w(T_{w,o}-T_{w,i}).\]

If the turbine exhaust is a saturated mixture of quality \(x\), then \(h_{\rm ex}=h_f+xh_{fg}\). Solving for quality gives a quantity inferred independently of a turbine efficiency assumption:

\[x=\frac{\dot m_w c_w(T_{w,o}-T_{w,i})}{\dot m_s h_{fg}} .\]

To connect this measurement to the complete cycle, write the boiler heat per kilogram as \(q_{\rm in}=h_3-h_2\) and the condenser rejection as \(q_{\rm out}=xh_{fg}\). Over a full ordinary Rankine cycle the working fluid stores no net energy:

\[w_{\rm net}=q_{\rm in}-q_{\rm out},\]
\[\eta_{\rm th}=1-\frac{xh_{fg}}{h_3-h_2}.\]

These relations assume no extraction, reheating or additional heat exchange elsewhere. If the exhaust is superheated or the condensate is significantly subcooled, the first energy balance remains valid but the latent-heat-only expression must be replaced by actual endpoint enthalpies.

Worked example

Infer exhaust quality and plant output

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Suppose \(\dot m_s=10.0\) kg/s, \(\dot m_w=500\) kg/s, \(c_w=4.18\) kJ/(kg K), water temperature rise \(10.0\) K and \(h_{fg}=2393\) kJ/kg. The water carries off \(20.9\) MW, giving \(x=20\,900/(10.0\times2393)=0.873\). If boiler heat is \(3000\) kJ/kg, the steam receives \(30.0\) MW and the cycle delivers \(9.10\) MW net, corresponding to efficiency \(30.3\%\).

Second application

Detect a cooling-water instrument inconsistency

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

With unchanged steam flow and latent heat, a reported rise of \(12.0\) K at the same water rate would imply \(25.08\) MW and \(x=1.048\). For a saturated exhaust the greatest possible rise in this setup is

\[10.0(2393)/(500\times4.18)=11.45\,\mathrm{K}.\]

The larger value suggests checking water flow, sensor bias or an assumption such as superheated inlet steam before accepting the inferred shaft output.

Try it · Condenser calorimetry

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Open this live calculation

Follow a kilogram through the loop

A simple steam plant pumps condensed water to boiler pressure, heats it to steam, expands it through a turbine and condenses it at low pressure. Water is nearly incompressible in the pump, while vapour occupies a much greater specific volume during expansion. A reversible steady adiabatic device satisfies \(dh=v\,dp\) because \(Tds=dh-v\,dp\) and \(ds=0\). Pressure increase therefore costs little work per kilogram when the fluid is liquid. This is one reason for condensing the working fluid before pumping it.

Number the saturated liquid leaving the condenser as state 1, the pump outlet as 2, the turbine inlet as 3 and the exhaust as 4. Neglect changes in kinetic and potential energy. Using positive magnitudes for pump work input and turbine work output gives

\[w_p\simeq v_1(p_2-p_1),\]
\[w_t=h_3-h_4,\]
\[q_{\rm in}=h_3-h_2,\]
\[\eta_{\rm th}=(w_t-w_p)/q_{\rm in}.\]

The exhaust pressure is set by the low-pressure side. An ideal turbine exit at that pressure has \(s_{4s}=s_3\); a real adiabatic turbine delivers a smaller enthalpy drop. Its isentropic efficiency is \(\eta_t=(h_3-h_4)/(h_3-h_{4s})\).

Calculate an ideal and an actual expansion

Use illustrative rounded state data. Condenser pressure is 10 kPa, with \(h_1=191.8\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), \(v_1=0.00101\,\mathrm{m^3/kg}\), \(s_f=0.649,\ s_{fg}=7.50\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\) and \(h_{fg}=2393\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Boiler pressure is 4000 kPa and superheated inlet properties are

\[h_3=3214\,\mathrm{kJ/kg},s_3=6.77\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}.\]

The ideal pump requires \(0.00101(4000-10)=4.03\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), so \(h_2=195.8\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\).

At the low pressure, the ideal exhaust quality is \(x_{4s}=(6.77-0.649)/7.50=0.816\), giving \(h_{4s}=191.8+0.816(2393)\simeq2145\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). With turbine efficiency 0.85 the actual exit enthalpy is

\[3214-0.85(3214-2145)=2305\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

The corresponding quality is

\[(2305-191.8)/2393=0.883.\]

At fixed inlet and exhaust pressure this less efficient turbine leaves more enthalpy in the exhaust, and therefore a higher quality inside the two-phase region.

Interpret the condenser and generator duties

The actual turbine delivers about 909 kJ/kg. Subtracting pump work leaves approximately 905 kJ/kg net shaft work. Boiler heat input is \(3214-195.8=3018\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), giving thermal efficiency about 30.0%. The condenser rejects \(2305-191.8=2113\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), which closes the cycle energy balance within the rounding of the property data. At 2.0 kg/s, the idealised net mechanical output is about 1.81 MW before generator and auxiliary losses.

A lower condenser pressure generally increases the available expansion work for a fixed turbine inlet. It can also lower exhaust quality in the ideal expansion, increasing liquid droplet loading on the later blades. Evaluating such a change requires new saturation properties and a fresh state calculation. Treating the exhaust as an ideal gas would omit the phase fraction precisely where it affects both the energy balance and turbine operating requirements.

Estimate the consequence of a pump loss

If the pump’s isentropic efficiency falls to 0.70, actual input rises from 4.03 to \(4.03/0.70=5.76\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). The turbine work remains the same under the specified inlet and outlet assumptions. Boiler duty falls by 1.73 kJ/kg because its feed arrives with more enthalpy. Both effects should enter the revised efficiency, even though pump work is small compared with the turbine output.

Derive the extraction fraction of an open feedwater heater

In a regenerative steam cycle, part of the flow is withdrawn from the turbine to preheat the boiler feedwater. This raises the average temperature of external heat input but reduces the mass continuing through the low-pressure turbine. Draw the control volumes per kilogram entering the boiler. Let fraction \(y\) be extracted at intermediate pressure and enthalpy \(h_b\), and let the remaining \(1-y\) pass to the condenser. A first pump returns the condensed fraction to the extraction pressure at enthalpy \(h_c\). In an insulated open heater the streams mix at the same pressure to a single liquid outlet of enthalpy \(h_f\).

Mass and energy balances reduce to \(y h_b+(1-y)h_c=h_f\), hence \(y=(h_f-h_c)/(h_b-h_c)\). This fraction must lie between zero and one for passive mixing at the stated states. A second pump then raises the full boiler flow to boiler pressure. If turbine inlet and final exhaust enthalpies are \(h_3,h_4\), work per kilogram admitted to the boiler is

\[w_t=(h_3-h_b)+(1-y)(h_b-h_4),\]
\[w_p=(1-y)w_{p1}+w_{p2}.\]

The first stage always carries the whole flow, as does the second pump. Only the low-pressure turbine, condenser and first pump carry the reduced mass. Applying the factor \(1-y\) to the entire turbine drop is a common bookkeeping mistake. Actual component efficiencies can be included through the enthalpies used in the same balances.

Quantify the exchange between specific work and thermal efficiency

Use an illustrative cycle with \(h_3=3300,\ h_b=2780,\ h_4=2300\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Condensate leaves at 192.0 kJ/kg and requires \(w_{p1}=0.5\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) to reach the heater pressure, so \(h_c=192.5\). Mixed feedwater leaves at \(h_f=640\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) and the second pump requires 5.0 kJ/kg. The extraction fraction is

\[(640-192.5)/(2780-192.5)=0.1730.\]

About 83% of the boiler flow therefore reaches the low-pressure exhaust.

Turbine work is

\[520+0.8270(480)=917.0\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Pump input is \(0.8270(0.5)+5=5.41\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), giving net 911.5 kJ/kg. Boiler input is \(3300-(640+5)=2655\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), or 34.3% thermal efficiency. Condenser duty, calculated independently from the remaining stream, is \(0.8270(2300-192)=1743.3\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), matching the difference between heat input and net work after rounding.

As a simplified comparison, let all steam expand to the same exhaust state and let a single pump require 5.5 kJ/kg with no regeneration. Net work rises to 994.5 kJ/kg but heat input rises to \(3300-197.5=3102.5\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\); efficiency falls to 32.1%. A boiler with a fixed heat supply favours the more efficient cycle, while a turbine limited by inlet mass flow may deliver more shaft power without extraction. The appropriate selection depends on which physical resource sets the constraint.

For a boiler limited to 10 MW of heat supply, the regenerative cycle can process \(10000/2655=3.77\,\mathrm{kg/s}\) of boiler flow and supply about 3.43 MW net. Without extraction the same heat limit permits only \(10000/3102.5=3.22\,\mathrm{kg/s}\), yielding approximately 3.21 MW net. These power outputs reverse the ranking of work per kilogram because the limiting quantity has changed from mass flow to heat input.

Problem sheet 5 · Exergy and steam-cycle performance

Compare useful-work bounds with actual work and estimate separate turbine and pump contributions to a power cycle.

A finite source cools partway

A liquid with heat capacity 2.0 kJ/(kg K) flows at 0.50 kg/s from 400 to 340 K at ambient pressure. The environment is at 300 K. Find released heat rate, maximum useful work rate from this cooling interval and entropy generated if heat goes directly into the environment.

Reveal the worked solution

Released heat is

\[0.50(2)(400-340)=60\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

The drop in flow exergy is

\[0.50(2)[60-300\ln(400/340)]=11.24\,\mathrm{kW}.\]

With direct exchange, the reservoirs and stream generate \(60/300-0.50(2)\ln(400/340)=0.03748\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). Multiplication by 300 K recovers the unused 11.24 kW of work potential.

Compare two exhaust states

At a fixed exhaust pressure \(h_f=190,\ h_{fg}=2400\) kJ/kg. A turbine has \(h_3=3200\) kJ/kg, ideal outlet enthalpy 2200 kJ/kg and isentropic efficiency 0.80. Find shaft work per kilogram, actual exhaust quality and output power at 1.5 kg/s. Which outlet contains the larger liquid fraction?

Reveal the worked solution

The actual work is \(0.8(3200-2200)=800\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), so \(h_4=2400\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Actual quality is \((2400-190)/2400=0.921\), and shaft output 1.20 MW. Ideal quality is \((2200-190)/2400=0.838\); the ideal exit has the larger liquid fraction because it has lost more enthalpy in the expansion.

Include pump efficiency

A cycle takes saturated liquid with \(v=0.0010\,\mathrm{m^3/kg}\) and \(h_1=200\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) through a 3.5 MPa pressure rise. Pump efficiency is 0.70; turbine inlet and outlet enthalpies are 3000 and 2250 kJ/kg. Find pump input, net work, heat input and thermal efficiency.

Reveal the worked solution

The ideal pump needs \(0.0010(3500)=3.50\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\); actual input is

\[3.50/0.70=5.00.\]

Thus \(h_2=205\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\), net work \(750-5=745\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) and boiler heat \(3000-205=2795\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\). Efficiency is 26.65%. Rejection \(2250-200=2050\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) closes the cycle energy balance.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Useful work and exergy destruction; Steam power cycles and turbine efficiency. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Notebook practice · How much work is lost when two finite bodies touch?

Chapter 11 · Heat-exchanger effectiveness and finite capacity rates

Derive the counterflow effectiveness relation and determine outlet temperatures when neither stream temperature is fixed.

From first principles

Derive exchanger effectiveness from the limiting heat balance

Consider a steady two-stream heat exchanger with no external heat gain, constant specific heats and no phase change. Each stream has a capacity rate \(C=\dot m c_p\), the power required for one kelvin of temperature change. Applying the first law independently to each stream gives a common transferred heat rate

\[\dot Q=C_h(T_{h,i}-T_{h,o})=C_c(T_{c,o}-T_{c,i}).\]

In passive exchange neither exit can cross the opposite stream’s inlet temperature. Therefore the maximum available temperature change on either stream is bounded by the same inlet difference \(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i}\). The smaller capacity rate limits the largest conceivable common duty. Define effectiveness from this physically accessible upper scale:

\[\dot Q_{\max}=C_{\min}(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i}),\]
\[\varepsilon=\dot Q/\dot Q_{\max},\]
\[\mathrm{NTU}=UA/C_{\min}.\]

The bound need not be reached by a finite exchanger or by every flow arrangement. Local conductance \(U\,dA\) and the changing local temperature difference determine the actual effectiveness. Knowing the two capacity rates and \(\varepsilon\) suffices to reconstruct both outlet temperatures using the separate energy balances.

Background · Heat-exchanger effectiveness
\[\epsilon=\dot Q/[C_{\min}(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i})]\]

Heat-capacity rate is mass flow times specific heat for a single-phase stream. The smaller rate limits the maximum possible sensible heat transfer.

Derivation and applications

Where the logarithmic mean temperature difference comes from

A local heat-transfer coefficient multiplies the temperature difference at that location. Integrating one exchanger therefore requires the changing driving difference across its whole area. Take a steady counterflow exchanger with no phase change, constant positive capacity rates \(C_h=\dot m_h c_h\), \(C_c=\dot m_c c_c\), constant overall coefficient \(U\) and no external heat loss. Measure area coordinate \(A'\) along the hot flow. Let \(d\dot Q\) be the positive heat transfer from hot to cold over the increment. Both fluid temperatures decrease along this chosen coordinate because the cold stream flows the other way.

\[d\dot Q=U(T_h-T_c)\,dA',\]
\[dT_h=-d\dot Q/C_h,\]
\[dT_c=-d\dot Q/C_c .\]

Thus \(\Delta=T_h-T_c\) changes linearly with cumulative heat transfer. Write its two physical terminal values as \(\Delta_1=T_{h,i}-T_{c,o}\) and \(\Delta_2=T_{h,o}-T_{c,i}\):

\[d\Delta=(1/C_c-1/C_h)d\dot Q,\]
\[\Delta_2-\Delta_1=(1/C_c-1/C_h)\dot Q .\]

To recover total area, integrate \(dA'=d\dot Q/(U\Delta)\) using the preceding linear relation. Eliminating the reciprocal capacity difference leaves

\[\dot Q=UA\,\frac{\Delta_1-\Delta_2}{\ln(\Delta_1/\Delta_2)} .\]

For equal terminal differences the apparent zero divided by zero has limit \(\Delta_1\). When one positive approach tends toward zero at a fixed heat duty, the necessary area diverges. Terminal differences must pair the temperatures at the same physical end, not two inlet temperatures chosen from opposite ends.

Worked example

Size an exchanger from measured terminal temperatures

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

A hot stream cools from \(90\) to \(55^\circ\)C while the counterflow cold stream warms from \(20\) to \(60^\circ\)C. The terminal differences are \(30\) and \(35\) K, so their logarithmic mean is

\[5/\ln(35/30)=32.4\,\mathrm{K}.\]

For a duty of \(70.0\) kW and \(U=500\) W/(m² K), the required area is \(4.32\) m². The capacities \(2.00\) and \(1.75\) kW/K independently deliver the same \(70.0\) kW.

Second application

Compare equal duty at a tighter terminal approach

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

A redesign has measured terminal differences of \(5.00\) and \(35.0\) K at the same duty and coefficient. The logarithmic mean drops to \(30/\ln7=15.4\) K and the required area rises to \(9.08\) m². Using the arithmetic mean \(20.0\) K would give only \(7.00\) m². The region with low driving difference consumes disproportionately more area per kilowatt transferred.
