Start from this chapter. Predict outlet temperatures using the counterflow effectiveness relation. At fixed conductance, increasing NTU here reduces the smaller heat-capacity rate. Determine whether effectiveness and total power move together.

OneSimulation laboratory · Explain why greater exchanger effectiveness can give less power

Set the maximum possible heat rate

In a two-stream heat exchanger, the hot temperature falls as the cold temperature rises. Treat each stream as single phase with constant heat capacity rate \(C=\dot m c_p\) in watts per kelvin. Neglect heat leakage, axial conduction and changes in kinetic energy. A steady overall balance gives

\[Q=C_h(T_{hi}-T_{ho})=C_c(T_{co}-T_{ci}).\]

Performing this balance alone leaves both outlet temperatures and the transferred heat rate unknown.

The stream with smaller capacity rate would undergo the larger temperature change for a given heat rate. Even in an ideal counterflow exchanger it cannot pass the inlet temperature of the other stream at its nearest end. Thus \(Q_{\max}=C_{\min}(T_{hi}-T_{ci})\). Effectiveness is \(\varepsilon=Q/Q_{\max}\), which immediately gives both outlet temperatures once the heat transfer law fixes \(\varepsilon\).

Apply a balance to an area element

Let \(A\) increase along the hot stream and write the local temperature difference \(\Delta=T_h-T_c\). An element transfers \(dQ=U\Delta\,dA\). In counterflow both temperature profiles decrease when followed in the hot-stream direction, giving \(dT_h=-dQ/C_h\), \(dT_c=-dQ/C_c\). Hence \(d\ln\Delta/dA=U(1/C_c-1/C_h)\). Integrate over area and express each end difference in terms of inlet temperatures and total heat rate using the stream balances.

\[NTU=UA/C_{\min},\]
\[C_r=C_{\min}/C_{\max},\]
\[\varepsilon=\frac{1-\exp[-NTU(1-C_r)]}{1-C_r\exp[-NTU(1-C_r)]}.\]

The sign in the differential balance follows from the chosen spatial direction. In parallel flow the cold stream warms along that coordinate and the local temperature difference decays more rapidly. For equal capacity rates the counterflow formula has a removable zero over zero. Its limit is \(NTU/(1+NTU)\), which can also be obtained from the constant temperature difference in that special case.

OneSimulation · Can the cold outlet leave hotter than the hot outlet?

The hot stream enters at 80 °C with a heat-capacity rate of 2 kW/K. The cold stream enters at 20 °C with a capacity rate of 1 kW/K. Compare the same heat exchanger conductance in parallel flow and counterflow. Obtain the two discharges from the same transferred heat rate \(\dot Q\), using \(\dot C=\dot m c_p\).

\[T_{h,o}=T_{h,i}-\dot Q/\dot C_h,\]
\[T_{c,o}=T_{c,i}+\dot Q/\dot C_c.\]

Equal outlets occur at 60 °C and require 40 kW, or effectiveness \(2/3\). Parallel flow approaches this common temperature asymptotically. Counterflow reaches it at \(\mathrm{NTU}=2\ln2\). At NTU 2 the two OneSimulation models predict cold and hot outlets of 66.5 °C and 56.8 °C in counterflow, against 58.0 °C and 61.0 °C in parallel flow. The counterflow outlets sit at opposite ends; heat still travels locally from the hotter fluid to the colder one.

"""Check for an outlet temperature cross in a two-stream heat exchanger."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.physics.systems.process import counterflow_effectiveness, parallel_flow_effectiveness

hot_in, cold_in = 80., 20.       # °C
hot_rate, cold_rate = 2., 1.     # kW/K
capacity_min = min(hot_rate, cold_rate)
ratio = capacity_min/max(hot_rate, cold_rate)
ntu = np.linspace(0, 4, 201)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4), constrained_layout=True)
for name, model, style in [("Counterflow", counterflow_effectiveness, "-"),
                            ("Parallel flow", parallel_flow_effectiveness, "--")]:
    heat = model(ntu, ratio)*capacity_min*(hot_in-cold_in)  # kW
    hot_out = hot_in - heat/hot_rate
    cold_out = cold_in + heat/cold_rate
    assert np.allclose(hot_rate*(hot_in-hot_out), cold_rate*(cold_out-cold_in))
    ax.plot(ntu, hot_out, style, color="#a5442e", label=f"{name}, hot outlet")
    ax.plot(ntu, cold_out, style, color="#19596a", label=f"{name}, cold outlet")

ax.axvline(2*np.log(2), color="0.5", linestyle=":", label="Counterflow outlets equal")
ax.set(xlabel="Transfer units UA / minimum capacity rate", ylabel="Outlet temperature (°C)")
ax.legend(fontsize=8)
fig.savefig("engineering-thermodynamics.png", dpi=170)

Predict both outlet temperatures

A counterflow cooler receives a hot stream at 90 °C with \(C_h=2.0\,\mathrm{kW/K}\) and a cold stream at 20 °C with \(C_c=1.0\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). Its conductance \(UA\) is 1.50 kW/K. Thus \(C_r=0.5\), \(NTU=1.5\), and the exponential is

\[e^{-0.75}=0.4724.\]

Effectiveness is 0.6908. Consequently \(Q=0.6908(1.0)(90-20)=48.4\,\mathrm{kW}\),

\[T_{ho}=90-48.4/2=65.8^\circ\mathrm C,\]
\[T_{co}=20+48.4=68.4^\circ\mathrm C.\]

The cold outlet is warmer than the hot outlet. They are at opposite physical ends, so this does not imply a local reversal of heat flow. The end differences are \(90-68.4=21.6\) K and \(65.8-20=45.8\) K, both positive. Their logarithmic mean is approximately \((45.8-21.6)/\ln(45.8/21.6)=32.2\,\mathrm K\), independently recovering \(Q=UA\Delta T_{\rm lm}\simeq48.3\,\mathrm{kW}\). This local check catches errors caused by swapping the terminal temperatures of a counterflow exchanger.

Predict the counterflow experiment

A smaller stream capacity rate raises NTU when the exchanger conductance is held fixed, yet it can reduce heat transfer in watts because less thermal capacity passes each second. For inlet temperatures 350 and 290 K and \(C_r=0.60\), use the counterflow expression to predict the effectiveness at NTU 1.5 and 3.0. Then halve both capacity rates, as the model does to obtain the second NTU, and predict the ratio of heat rates. Check both outlet temperatures and the hot-stream heat rate, paying attention to which stream has the smaller capacity. Open the heat-exchanger experiment.

Identify which side controls an exchanger’s conductance

The effectiveness relation uses a single conductance \(UA\), but a designer may need to determine whether the hot film, cold film or fouling dominates it. Write the total thermal resistance in K/W as \(R_{\rm tot}=1/(h_hA_h)+R_w+R_f+1/(h_cA_c)\). Areas must be kept explicit if the two sides use different reference areas. Suppose a test can vary hot-side flow while holding cold-side flow, wall resistance and deposit condition fixed. Let a supported correlation in the tested region imply \(h_h\propto\dot m_h^{\,n}\). With \(m_r=\dot m_h/\dot m_{\rm ref}\), the relation takes the linear form

\[(UA)^{-1}=R_{\rm fixed}+R_{h,\rm ref}\,m_r^{-n}.\]

This is the basis of a Wilson-plot style separation. Each measured \(UA\) can be obtained from balanced heat duty and the correct logarithmic mean temperature difference. The intercept estimates the combined fixed resistance; the slope estimates the hot-film resistance at reference flow. One set of measurements alone does not separate cold film from wall and deposits individually. Temperature-dependent material properties and changes in the opposite stream can spoil the assumed linear relation.

Treating \(UA\) as proportional to flow whenever convection rises would ignore the series resistances left in its path. As \(m_r\) becomes large the hot-film term tends to zero, leaving an asymptote \(1/R_{\rm fixed}\) rather than unbounded conductance. Pumping power typically rises over this same change of flow.

Use a flow sweep to distinguish fouling from film resistance

A clean counterflow rig at reference hot-side mass flow has \(UA=500\,\mathrm{W/K}\), giving total resistance \(0.00200\,\mathrm{K/W}\). At twice the flow it has \(UA=600\,\mathrm{W/K}\), or \(0.001667\,\mathrm{K/W}\). Over this interval use measured exponent \(n=0.80\), so

\[2^{-0.8}=0.5743.\]

Subtracting the two equations gives

\[R_{h,\rm ref}=(0.00200-0.001667)/(1-0.5743)=0.000783\,\mathrm{K/W}.\]

The combined fixed resistance is \(0.001217\,\mathrm{K/W}\). Even an ideal hot film could raise conductance only to about 822 W/K under the unchanged fixed-side assumptions.

A later test at reference flow gives \(UA=400\,\mathrm{W/K}\), so total resistance has grown to 0.00250 K/W. If a second flow sweep confirms the same hot-film slope, the added fixed resistance is 0.00050 K/W. For a heat-transfer area of 2.0 m² on the chosen basis this corresponds to fouling resistance \(R_f''=0.0010\,\mathrm{m^2K/W}\). If instead the slope has changed, assigning the entire difference to deposits is unwarranted; changed flow distribution or fluid properties may contribute. Temperature measurement also matters strongly when the two terminal differences are close to sensor uncertainty. Compare the hot and cold enthalpy duties before fitting the conductance and retain the residual across all measured flow settings.

A third clean test can challenge the fitted exponent. At four times reference hot flow, the predicted resistance is \(0.001217+0.000783(4^{-0.8})=0.001475\,\mathrm{K/W}\), or \(UA\simeq678\,\mathrm{W/K}\). A systematic departure that grows at high flow could reflect changing cold-side properties, thermocouple bias or failure of the hot-film correlation. Two calibration points always fit a two-parameter straight line; the independent third point actually tests the assumed dependence.

Determine whether a specified exchanger effectiveness is attainable

Fix both inlet temperatures and \(C_{\min}\). Changing area changes NTU. For constant properties and no losses the effectiveness divides actual heat rate by \(C_{\min}(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i})\). Compare two flow arrangements and the isothermal-stream limit.

How a gravity-drained condensate film limits heat transfer · Thermal processes and transport in engineering

Saturated vapour condenses on a cool vertical plate. A liquid film thickens as it drains, adding thermal resistance toward the bottom. Derive the local heat-transfer coefficient from coupled momentum and energy balances, then determine which observations would invalidate the smooth-film model.

Chapter 12 · Thermal radiation and enclosure exchange

Use surface emission and reflected irradiation to derive the net heat exchange between parallel grey surfaces.

From first principles

Derive enclosure heat exchange from emission, reflection and absorption

Consider an opaque diffuse grey surface at uniform absolute temperature \(T\). Let \(G\) denote incoming radiant power per area and \(J\) total outgoing radiant power per area. The Stefan–Boltzmann law gives blackbody emission \(E_b=\sigma T^4\). Emissivity \(\epsilon\) scales actual emission; under the grey thermal model absorptivity equals \(\epsilon\), so the reflected fraction is \(1-\epsilon\). Add emitted and reflected contributions to obtain

\[J=\epsilon E_b+(1-\epsilon)G,\]
\[q''_{\rm out}=J-G=\epsilon(E_b-G).\]

In an enclosure each surface receives irradiation from the radiosities it sees. For two large parallel facing plates, \(G_1=J_2\) and \(G_2=J_1\). Solving the two linear radiosity equations for their net outward flux difference yields

\[q''_{1\to2}=\frac{\sigma(T_1^4-T_2^4)}{1/\epsilon_1+1/\epsilon_2-1}.\]

The denominator incorporates repeated reflections. Equal temperatures give zero net exchange even though each surface radiates. Black plates reduce to the difference of their blackbody emissions. A small surface that sees itself or other walls requires the corresponding geometric view factors in its irradiation balance.

Derivation and applications

Determine a small surface temperature in a large radiating enclosure

A small opaque specimen suspended in a large enclosure receives radiation over its entire visible hemisphere. Under diffuse grey conditions Kirchhoff’s relation gives equal absorptivity and emissivity \(\epsilon\); a large isothermal black enclosure at \(T_w\) supplies irradiation \(G=\sigma T_w^4\). Assume the specimen is isothermal at \(T\), sees only the enclosure, and also exchanges heat with a gas at \(T_g\) through a known constant film coefficient \(h\). Neglect conduction through supports. Let \(p''\) be a uniform internal heat supply per unit radiating area.

Emission and absorption occur separately, so the net outward radiative heat flux is the emitted flux minus the absorbed irradiation. The steady balance becomes

\[q''_{\rm rad}=\epsilon\sigma(T^4-T_w^4),\]
\[p''=h(T-T_g)+\epsilon\sigma(T^4-T_w^4).\]

To compare film and radiation effects without approximating a fourth power, factor the difference. Define a secant radiation coefficient between the two actual temperatures:

\[h_r(T,T_w)=\epsilon\sigma(T+T_w)(T^2+T_w^2),\]
\[q''_{\rm rad}=h_r(T-T_w).\]

For perturbations about an operating point, hold \(T_w,T_g\) fixed and differentiate the balance. The slope governing a small additional input is different from the secant coefficient:

\[\frac{dT}{dp''}=\frac{1}{h+4\epsilon\sigma T^3}.\]

Both coefficients have units of W/(m² K); their numerical values coincide as \(T\to T_w\). Fourth powers require absolute temperatures. The balance is monotone for positive \(T\), giving one physically relevant operating temperature for a compatible specified input.

Worked example

Size a heater for a test coupon

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

Let \(T=350\) K, \(T_w=300\) K, \(T_g=310\) K, \(h=8.00\) W/(m² K) and \(\epsilon=0.800\). With \(\sigma=5.6704\times10^{-8}\) W/(m² K⁴), radiation loss is \(313\) W/m² and convection loss \(320\) W/m². A coupon with \(0.0100\) m² total exposed area requires \(6.33\) W. The corresponding secant radiation coefficient is \(313/50=6.26\) W/(m² K).

Second application

Predict a small calibration shift

Change the inputs and test this calculation.

At the same operating point the tangent radiation coefficient is \(4(0.800)\sigma(350)^3=7.78\) W/(m² K). Adding \(0.100\) W over the same area increases \(p''\) by \(10.0\) W/m². Linearization predicts a temperature rise of \(10/(8.00+7.78)=0.634\) K. The differential coefficient applies to this incremental measurement; the secant coefficient gave the original heat exchanged with the enclosure.

Try it · Radiation heater

Set the inputs to the values in the worked example, then vary one quantity while holding the others fixed. Compare the curve and numerical balance with the second application. The calculation uses the assumptions derived above; its readout identifies bounds such as choking, phase-region changes and inconsistent measurements.

Separate what a surface emits from what leaves it

A hot surface transfers energy across a vacuum through electromagnetic radiation. A black surface emits total hemispherical power \(E_b=\sigma T^4\) per unit area, using absolute temperature. A diffuse grey opaque surface emits a fraction \(\varepsilon E_b\) and reflects a fraction of incident irradiation \(G\). Under the grey equilibrium-property assumptions, absorptivity equals emissivity and reflectivity is \(1-\varepsilon\). The total radiation leaving the surface, called radiosity, is then

\[J=\varepsilon E_b+(1-\varepsilon)G.\]

Net radiative heat flux is

\[q''=J-G.\]

Eliminate the unknown irradiation using the radiosity relation. For a surface of area \(A\) and \(0<\varepsilon<1\), this gives

\[Q=(E_b-J)/[(1-\varepsilon)/(A\varepsilon)].\]

The denominator behaves like a resistance between the blackbody emission potential and the surface radiosity. It vanishes for an ideal black surface, whose outgoing radiation is set solely by its temperature.

Couple two large facing plates

Consider two large parallel diffuse grey plates of equal area with negligible edge losses. Each plate sees only the other, so \(G_1=J_2\) and \(G_2=J_1\). The net rate from 1 to 2 is \(A(J_1-J_2)\). Combine that space resistance \(1/A\) with the surface resistances of the two plates in series. After cancellation the net flux is

\[q''=\frac{\sigma(T_1^4-T_2^4)}{1/\varepsilon_1+1/\varepsilon_2-1}.\]

The fourth powers cannot generally be evaluated using Celsius temperatures, even though a simple temperature difference has the same magnitude in kelvins and degrees Celsius. For finite or angled surfaces, a view factor describes how much of one surface’s outgoing radiation reaches the other. Equal area alone does not imply a view factor of one.

Add a freely floating radiation shield

Take \(T_1=500\,\mathrm K\), \(T_2=300\,\mathrm K\), both plate emissivities 0.80 and \(\sigma=5.670\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{W/(m^2 K^4)}\). The blackbody difference is \(3084\,\mathrm{W/m^2}\). Division by \(1/0.8+1/0.8-1=1.5\) gives \(2056\,\mathrm{W/m^2}\). Insert an opaque thin shield with emissivity 0.10 on both faces and negligible temperature drop through its thickness. There are now two radiation gaps, each with denominator \(1/0.8+1/0.1-1=10.25\).

At steady state the shield exchanges equal heat flux with both plates and stores no energy. With equal gap denominators it follows that \(T_s^4=(T_1^4+T_2^4)/2\), so \(T_s\simeq433\,\mathrm K\). The total resistance denominator is 20.5 and heat flux drops to about 150 W/m². The floating shield temperature is higher than the arithmetic average of the plate temperatures. Solid supports connecting the shield to either plate would add conductive paths and change its temperature and the total heat leak. This explains why a low measured radiative load alone may not determine the performance of an insulated assembly.

Compare radiation with a convection coefficient

For black exchange, factor \(T_1^4-T_2^4=(T_1-T_2)(T_1+T_2)(T_1^2+T_2^2)\). For the original grey plates the corresponding effective coefficient is

\[2056/(500-300)=10.28\,\mathrm{W/(m^2K)}.\]

It can be compared directly with a convective coefficient for the same temperature difference, but its value changes when either absolute temperature changes.

Combine view-factor reciprocity with grey-surface resistances

Two large parallel plates have equal area and see one another completely. For opposite-sized diffuse grey surfaces in a two-surface enclosure, exchange through the intervening space depends on view factor \(F_{12}\), the fraction of energy leaving surface 1 that directly reaches surface 2. Reciprocity requires

\[A_1F_{12}=A_2F_{21}.\]

A convex inner body entirely surrounded by a shell has \(F_{12}=1\); radiation leaving the larger concave shell need not all reach the inner body because some hits the shell again.

Net transfer through the space is \(Q=A_1F_{12}(J_1-J_2)\), with radiosities \(J_1,J_2\). Combine this relation with both surface resistance expressions and eliminate the radiosities. For uniform isothermal surfaces that can each be represented by one radiosity, the result is

\[Q=\frac{\sigma(T_1^4-T_2^4)}{(1-\varepsilon_1)/(A_1\varepsilon_1)+1/(A_1F_{12})+(1-\varepsilon_2)/(A_2\varepsilon_2)} .\]

For concentric long cylinders with negligible ends \(F_{12}=1\) and \(A_1/A_2=r_1/r_2\). The rate per inner area simplifies to \(\sigma(T_1^4-T_2^4)/[1/\varepsilon_1+(A_1/A_2)(1/\varepsilon_2-1)]\). As the outer area becomes very large its surface resistance has little effect on transfer per unit inner area. When the two areas approach equality the familiar parallel-surface denominator is recovered. This area weighting matters when a shiny outer enclosure surrounds a relatively small part.

Separate radiation and support conduction in a vacuum vessel

A long heated cylinder has outer radius 10 mm and active length 0.50 m, concentric with a cylindrical vacuum jacket of inner radius 40 mm. Take \(T_1=400\,\mathrm K,\ T_2=300\,\mathrm K,\ \varepsilon_1=0.70,\ \varepsilon_2=0.20\), and ignore end exchange. Areas satisfy \(A_1/A_2=0.25\), and \(A_1=2\pi(0.010)(0.50)=0.03142\,\mathrm{m^2}\). The effective denominator is

\[1/0.70+0.25(1/0.20-1)=2.429.\]

The blackbody flux difference is \(5.670\times10^{-8}(400^4-300^4)=992.3\,\mathrm{W/m^2}\), giving radiative loss \(992.3(0.03142)/2.429=12.8\,\mathrm W\).

Two solid supports each have area 4.0 mm², length 20 mm and mean conductivity 15 W/(m K). Conduction through both is \(2(15)(4.0\times10^{-6})(100)/0.020=0.60\,\mathrm W\), so holding the inner cylinder at its stated temperature requires approximately 13.4 W. A measured heater power of 15 W would leave about 1.6 W unaccounted for, potentially from end radiation, electrical leads, residual gas or emissivity uncertainty. If the jacket is blackened to \(\varepsilon_2=1\), the radiation rises to \(992.3(0.03142)(0.70)=21.8\,\mathrm W\). Improving absorption of the outer wall increases the heat loss from the inner component even though neither temperature changes.

An intercept test can distinguish temperature-dependent pathways more effectively than a single operating point. At a small excess \(\Delta T\) around 300 K, radiative loss varies approximately as \(4\sigma T^3 A_1\Delta T/[1/\varepsilon_1+(A_1/A_2)(1/\varepsilon_2-1)]\). The original cylinder has linearised radiative conductance about \(4(5.67\times10^{-8})(300)^3(0.03142)/2.429=0.0792\,\mathrm{W/K}\), compared with 0.0060 W/K for its two supports. At large temperature differences the effective radiation conductance rises with absolute temperature while ideal support conduction stays linear. Recording heater power at several balanced temperatures lets those trends be tested, provided the surface emissivities and support conductivity do not themselves vary significantly over the range.

A measurement with electrical power off also establishes any background sensor offset before estimating these comparatively small near-ambient conductances from a temperature difference.

Which surface treatment changes net radiation most?

Two large diffuse-grey opaque plates at 400 K and 300 K face each other across a nonparticipating gap. For unit view factor the net radiation resistance is proportional to \(1/\epsilon_h+1/\epsilon_c-1\). Vary the hot coating and keep the cold one fixed.

Radiative exchange in a grey enclosure · Thermal processes and transport in engineering

A thin radiation shield does not need to absorb all incoming radiation to reduce heat transfer. Multiple reflections connect every surface to every other visible surface. Build the radiosity equations, recover the two-plate result and infer what can be learned from a measured shield temperature.

Problem sheet 6 · Heat exchangers and radiation shields

Relate thermal conductance and surface properties to local temperature differences and net energy transport.

A balanced counterflow exchanger

Both stream capacity rates are 1.5 kW/K and conductance \(UA=3.0\) kW/K. Inlets are at 80 and 20 °C. Determine effectiveness, heat rate, both outlets and the local temperature difference. Explain why both end differences agree.

Reveal the worked solution

At \(C_r=1\), \(NTU=2\) and \(\varepsilon=2/3\). The exchanger transfers \((2/3)(1.5)(60)=60\,\mathrm{kW}\). Hot outlet is 40 °C and cold outlet 60 °C. At each end the hot stream exceeds the adjacent cold stream by 20 K. Equal capacity rates produce equal profile slopes when followed along the same physical direction.

Infer a required exchanger size

A hot stream of capacity rate 4 kW/K cools from 100 to 75 °C. A counterflow cold stream enters at 25 °C with capacity rate 2 kW/K. Calculate its outlet, the terminal differences and required \(UA\). What is the effectiveness?

Reveal the worked solution

The duty is \(4(100-75)=100\,\mathrm{kW}\), giving cold outlet \(25+100/2=75^\circ\mathrm C\). Terminal differences are 25 and 50 K. Their logarithmic mean is \((50-25)/\ln2=36.07\,\mathrm K\), so \(UA=2.773\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). The maximum duty is \(2(100-25)=150\,\mathrm{kW}\); effectiveness is 0.667.

A shield in a black enclosure gap

Large black parallel walls face one another at 400 and 300 K. A thin black shield is inserted midway using negligible conducting supports. Find unshielded flux, shielded flux and equilibrium shield temperature. Does equal spacing enter the result?

Reveal the worked solution

The unshielded flux is

\[\sigma(400^4-300^4)=992\,\mathrm{W/m^2}.\]

Each black gap contributes the same unit denominator, halving the flux to 496 W/m². The shield satisfies \(T_s^4=(400^4+300^4)/2\), giving \(T_s=360.3\,\mathrm K\). Equal spacing is unnecessary for two effectively infinite parallel plates whose view factors are unity.

Close the energy and exergy balances of a heat exchanger

An insulated counterflow exchanger cools water from 90°C to 60°C at 1.0 kg/s. Another water stream enters at 20°C and leaves at 50°C. Both have cₚ = 4.18 kJ/(kg K). Find the cold mass flow, thermal conductance UA, effectiveness and rate of exergy destruction at an ambient temperature of 293.15 K. Ignore pressure losses.

Worked solution

The hot side releases 125.4 kW. Conservation of energy gives a cold mass flow of 1.0 kg/s because the two temperature changes have equal magnitude. For counterflow, the end temperature differences are 90−50 = 40 K and 60−20 = 40 K. Their logarithmic mean is 40 K by continuity, giving UA = 3.135 kW/K.

The minimum heat-capacity rate is 4.18 kW/K, and the maximum possible duty is 4.18 × 70 = 292.6 kW. The effectiveness is 0.4286. As a check, NTU = UA/Cmin = 0.75 and for balanced counterflow ε = NTU/(1+NTU) = 0.4286.

With absolute temperatures, the entropy creation rate is 4.18[ln(333.15/363.15)+ln(323.15/293.15)] kW/K, about 0.0469 kW/K. Multiplying by ambient temperature gives about 13.7 kW of exergy destruction. Insulation eliminates the external heat flux, but finite temperature differences inside the exchanger still produce entropy.

Explore the governing equations after solving the sheet: Heat-exchanger effectiveness and finite capacity rates; Thermal radiation and enclosure exchange. Compare the assumptions and units before transferring your chosen parameters; the controls may cover a different numerical range from the written exercise.

Recover an exchanger capacity rate from outlet data

For a steady counterflow exchanger, the baseline hot stream cools from 350 to 309.84 K and the cold stream warms from 290 to 314.20 K. Hot-side power loss is 0.2168 W. Infer the hot heat-capacity rate, estimate the cold rate assuming equal powers, and infer \(UA\) from the stated NTU of 1.5. Why is the inferred cold rate approximate?

Work through the solution

The hot balance gives \(C_h=\dot Q/(350-309.84)=0.00540\) W/K. Setting the cold gain equal gives \(C_c=0.2168/(314.20-290)=0.00896\) W/K. Their ratio is 0.603, close to the input 0.60. The smaller capacity rate is the hot one, so \(UA={\rm NTU}\,C_{\min}=0.00810\) W/K. The finite mesh yields a small mismatch between the two streamwise estimates of heat rate. Using the measured cold-side power instead would directly test that balance.

Chapter 13 · Choosing a pressure ratio for a gas-turbine cycle

Why does a bigger pressure ratio eventually reduce the work from a gas turbine? Follow one kilogram of gas round four devices, keep separate accounts for heat and work, and see what changes when the compressor and turbine are imperfect.

Label four states before comparing designs

Use an air-standard Brayton cycle. A compressor takes gas from state 1 to 2. A constant-pressure heater raises it to the maximum allowed temperature at 3. A turbine expands the gas to the original pressure at 4. A cooler returns it to 1. Neglect kinetic and potential energy changes and pressure losses in the heat exchangers. The heater and cooler replace combustion and exhaust in this closed model. Use a calorically perfect gas with constant \(c_p\) and \(\gamma=c_p/c_v\). For an ideal adiabatic reversible device the entropy change vanishes. Integrating the ideal-gas entropy differential gives

\[0=c_p\ln(T_{2s}/T_1)-R\ln r,\]
\[x=r^{(\gamma-1)/\gamma},\]
\[T_{2s}=xT_1,\]
\[T_{4s}=T_3/x .\]

The pressure ratio \(r=p_2/p_1=p_3/p_4\) is dimensionless. The subscript s identifies a state at the specified exit pressure reached along an isentrope. The turbine and compressor compare different inlet states. A common bookkeeping mistake is to use the compressor inlet temperature to calculate turbine work.

Subtract the compressor demand before optimizing output

Write positive turbine output and positive compressor consumption separately. The first law then gives the net delivered work \(w\) and the heat input \(q\) per kilogram. Maximizing \(w\) at fixed \(T_1,T_3\) amounts to differentiating with respect to x.

\[{w\over c_p}=T_3(1-1/x)-T_1(x-1),\]
\[{q\over c_p}=T_3-xT_1,\]
\[{1\over c_p}{dw\over dx}=T_3/x^2-T_1 .\]

The maximum occurs at \(x=\sqrt{T_3/T_1}\). The second derivative is negative. There the compressor exit and turbine exit have the same temperature. Dividing work by heat also gives the ideal efficiency \(1-1/x\), which increases throughout the range that admits positive heat input. A design chosen for maximum shaft work and a design chosen for maximum thermal efficiency can therefore have different pressure ratios. At the extreme \(x=T_3/T_1\), both heat input and net work vanish.

A design point and its sensitivity to losses

At 300 K and 1200 K with \(\gamma=1.4,\ c_p=1.005\) kJ/(kg K), maximum ideal work occurs at \(x=2\), hence

\[r=2^{3.5}=11.31.\]

Both intermediate temperatures are 600 K. The compressor consumes 301.5 kJ/kg and the turbine produces 603.0 kJ/kg. The delivered 301.5 kJ/kg for 603.0 kJ/kg heating gives 50% efficiency.

Now set the compressor isentropic efficiency to 0.80 and the turbine efficiency to 0.90. The actual compressor rise is divided by its efficiency, while the ideal turbine drop is multiplied by its efficiency. At the same pressure ratio the actual temperatures are 675 K and 660 K, the net work is 165.8 kJ/kg, and heat input is 527.6 kJ/kg. The corresponding work derivative vanishes at \(x=\sqrt{\eta_t\eta_c T_3/T_1}\). This moves the optimum to a smaller pressure ratio. Use the curves to locate both maxima before calculating their positions.

Start with both device efficiencies set to one, record the pressure ratio at maximum work, then reduce only compressor efficiency. Predict which side of the old optimum becomes more costly and compare your reasoning to the shifted peak.

Tutorial · can the exhaust preheat the compressed gas?

Take the same imperfect components at \(r=4\). Calculate temperatures 2 and 4. An insulated counterflow regenerator has equal capacity rates and effectiveness 0.70. Determine the saving in heater duty and explain why its benefit vanishes at certain pressure ratios.

Show the state and heat calculations

Here \(x=1.4860\), so \(T_2=482.3\) K and \(T_4=846.8\) K. The compressed stream gains \(0.70(846.8-482.3)=255.2\) K, saving about 256.5 kJ/kg of external heat. Both shaft devices retain the same inlet and exit states. The model predicts no change in their work. When the exhaust arrives colder than the compressed stream it cannot serve as the hot source for this duty. A practical design would also account for exchanger pressure loss.

Chapter 14 · Why a real gas cools or warms across a throttle

A gas passes through an insulated porous plug. Its pressure drops and its measured temperature changes. Start with the energy flowing through the plug and obtain a prediction from the equation of state.

Establish which property travels through the plug

A steady control volume with one inlet, one outlet and no shaft work has \(h_1+u_1^2/2+gz_1=h_2+u_2^2/2+gz_2\) when the casing is insulated. Choose the measurement stations in large, level pipes where changes in mean kinetic energy are negligible. Then \(h_1=h_2\). The pressure gradient is sustained by irreversible friction in the plug. Along the family of equilibrium outlet states, a small pressure change produces \(dT=\mu_{JT}dp\), with \(\mu_{JT}=(\partial T/\partial p)_h\). This family lets us compute endpoints without resolving the interior pore flow.

Recover the pressure dependence of enthalpy

Use molar properties. For a simple compressible pure substance, \(dh=T\,ds+v\,dp\). View entropy as a function of \(T,p\). At fixed pressure its temperature derivative is \(c_p/T\). To obtain its pressure derivative, differentiate \(g=h-Ts\). Its differential is \(dg=-s\,dT+v\,dp\). Equality of mixed derivatives gives

\[(\partial s/\partial p)_T=-(\partial v/\partial T)_p.\]

Substitution yields

\[dh=c_p\,dT+\left[v-T\left({\partial v\over\partial T}\right)_p\right]dp,\]
\[\mu_{JT}={T(\partial v/\partial T)_p-v\over c_p}.\]

For an ideal gas \(v=RT/p\) the two numerator terms cancel at all temperatures. Real gas attractions and finite molecular volume affect this near cancellation. Since \(dp<0\) for the pressure drop, positive \(\mu_{JT}\) indicates cooling. Check units by dividing cubic metres per mole by joules per mole per kelvin. The result is kelvins per pascal.

An instructive gas with an inversion temperature

Represent a dilute model gas by \(v=RT/p+B(T)\), with \(B=b-a/(RT)\). Here \(a,b\) are constants and the omitted equation-of-state terms become important at higher density. The ideal part cancels identically. Differentiating the correction gives

\[\mu_{JT}\simeq {T B'(T)-B(T)\over c_p}={2a/(RT)-b\over c_p},\]
\[T_{\rm inv}={2a\over Rb}.\]

With \(b=4.0\times10^{-5}\) m³/mol, \(a=0.080\) Pa m⁶/mol², \(R=8.314\) J/(mol K) and \(c_p=30\) J/(mol K), the inversion lies near 481 K. At 300 K, \(\mu_{JT}=0.0805\) K/bar. A modest drop from 3 bar to 1 bar cools this gas by about 0.161 K. At 600 K the derivative is about −0.0264 K/bar. The same pressure drop then warms the gas by 0.0528 K. Treat this as a local calculation. Over a large temperature range one should integrate the property derivative using a validated equation of state.

Alter the pressure drop while holding the attractive constant fixed. Locate the zero crossing at two pressure drops. Explain why it remains at the same temperature in this first-order calculation and why the cooling magnitudes change.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · infer the attractive correction

A low-density gas at 320 K gives a positive slope of 0.060 K/bar in a throttle experiment. Assume the same \(b,c_p\) as above. Estimate a and the inversion temperature. How would an unaccounted heat leak from a warm room bias the inference if the gas cools?

Show the inference and sign check

Convert the slope to \(6.0\times10^{-7}\) K/Pa. Rearranging gives \(a=(RT/2)(c_p\mu_{JT}+b)=0.0772\) Pa m⁶/mol², whence \(T_{\rm inv}=464\) K. A warm leak partially cancels cooling, making the observed outlet temperature drop too small. Because the pressure drop is also negative, the apparent positive slope is smaller. In this parameter fit the estimated a is therefore too low.

Further reading · MIT 2.43 · property relations and the Joule–Thomson coefficient

Chapter 15 · When another centimetre of cooling fin stops helping

A fin adds surface area, but heat must travel down its length before it can reach that area. Derive the temperature profile from an energy balance and quantify how much of the nominal area contributes to cooling.

Balance a short slice with distributed convection

Consider a straight prismatic fin of conductivity k, cross-section A, exposed perimeter P and length L. Hold the root at \(T_b\), let a fluid at \(T_\infty\) surround the fin and use a uniform convection coefficient h. Temperature is assumed nearly uniform across each cross-section. A small transverse Biot number \(h(A/P)/k\) supports that assumption. Ignore radiation and heat loss from the tip. Define \(\theta=T-T_\infty\). The axial heat entering a slice, less the heat leaving its far face, must equal \(hP\theta\,dx\).

\[q_x=-kA\,\theta',\]
\[-{dq_x\over dx}=hP\theta,\]
\[\theta''-m^2\theta=0,\]
\[m=\sqrt{hP/(kA)}.\]

A positive temperature excess makes the heat flow diminish downstream. For an insulated tip \(q_x(L)=0\), so a hyperbolic cosine centered at the tip satisfies the zero-slope condition. Its coefficient follows from the root temperature.

\[{\theta(x)\over\theta_b}={\cosh[m(L-x)]\over\cosh(mL)},\]
\[Q_b=kAm\theta_b\tanh(mL).\]

Efficiency follows by comparing with an isothermal fin

If every part of the lateral area stayed at \(T_b\), the heat transfer would be \(hPL\theta_b\). The actual-to-ideal ratio is therefore

\[\eta_f=\tanh(mL)/(mL).\]

For small mL the temperature falls little along the wall and doubling the area nearly doubles the heat flow. For large mL the root heat flow approaches \(kAm\theta_b\). Near the long-fin limit increasing conductivity or the root cross-section can buy more cooling than adding length. Efficiency alone does not measure total heat removal because it contains the nominal area in its denominator.

Two fins made from the same stock

Use a rectangular aluminium strip, 20 mm wide, 2 mm thick, \(k=200\) W/(m K), \(h=25\) W/(m² K) and \(\theta_b=50\) K. All four long faces are exposed. With \(A=4.0\times10^{-5}\) m² and \(P=0.044\) m, \(m=11.73\) m⁻¹. At 50 mm length, \(mL=0.586\), \(Q_b=2.47\) W and \(\eta_f=0.899\). The tip remains about 42.5 K above ambient.

At 150 mm, the heat rate is 4.43 W and the efficiency drops to 0.536. The material and exposed area tripled, while the heat transfer increased by about 79%. A fin root bonded through a finite contact resistance \(R_c\) would run cooler than the heated base. Replacing the temperature excess by \(T_{\rm base}-T_\infty-Q_b R_c\) in the root heat equation gives a direct way to include the bond in a thermal circuit.

Choose a short fin and inspect how far the infinite-fin approximation is from the insulated-tip temperature. Increase length until the root heat rate is almost saturated. Estimate the added cooling from another centimetre at that point.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · select a length from a marginal-benefit criterion

For the same strip, require 95% of the infinitely long fin's heat rate. Find the required length. Then estimate the heat rate if the base is held 50 K above ambient through a contact resistance of 1 K/W.

Show the inverse calculation

Set \(\tanh(mL)=0.95\), giving \(mL=\tfrac12\ln(1.95/0.05)=1.832\) and \(L=0.156\) m. The root conductance there is \(G=kAm(0.95)=0.0891\) W/K. Adding the contact resistance gives

\[Q=50/(1/G+1)=4.09\,\mathrm{W}.\]

The temperature drop across the contact is about 4.09 K. This check distinguishes the fin-root temperature from the base temperature reported by a sensor under the bond.

Further reading · Lienhard and Lienhard · A Heat Transfer Textbook, chapter 4 on conduction and fins

Chapter 16 · A moving boundary in a freezing layer

A flat cold surface grows a layer of ice from water initially at its melting temperature. Estimate when it will reach a specified thickness, then discover how a finite heat-transfer resistance changes its early growth.

Write a balance at a boundary whose position is unknown

Let the solid occupy \(0<x<s(t)\). The boundary at x=0 is held at \(T_w<T_m\), the liquid remains at \(T_m\), and the solid-liquid interface is at its equilibrium melting temperature. Treat density, latent heat of fusion \(L_f\) and solid conductivity k as constants. Ignore volume change and fluid convection. When the front moves by ds, each unit area releases latent energy \(\rho L_f\,ds\). In this one-phase problem conduction into the cold side removes that energy. Taking the magnitude of heat extracted, the interface condition reads

\[\rho L_f\,{ds\over dt}=k\,{\partial T\over\partial x}\bigg|_{s^-}.\]

The derivative is positive because temperature increases towards the liquid. Solving the full problem requires time-dependent conduction in a domain with a moving outer end. A useful approximation emerges when the sensible heat needed to cool the new solid is small beside its latent heat. Measure that ratio with the Stefan number \({\rm Ste}=c_p(T_m-T_w)/L_f\).

Derive the square-root law and check the approximation

For small Ste the temperature profile in the solid adjusts quickly compared with front growth. Approximate the gradient by \((T_m-T_w)/s\). Multiplying the interface balance by s separates the variables. Integrating from an initial layer \(s_0\) gives

\[s\,ds={k(T_m-T_w)\over\rho L_f}\,dt,\]
\[s^2=s_0^2+{2k(T_m-T_w)\over\rho L_f}t .\]

With zero initial thickness the predicted speed starts unbounded because the model inserts a finite temperature difference across zero resistance. A finite surface heat-transfer coefficient removes this singularity. If coolant at \(T_c\) reaches the wall through a film \(1/h\), the heat flux is \((T_m-T_c)/(1/h+s/k)\). For a layer growing from zero, integration gives

\[t={\rho L_f\over T_m-T_c}\left({s\over h}+{s^2\over2k}\right).\]

The linear term dominates while the film presents the larger resistance. Once the solid is thick, the quadratic term recovers diffusion-controlled growth. These two regimes can be distinguished by recording thickness at equal time intervals.

An ideal wall and a finite film

For water freezing at 0 °C take \(k=2.2\) W/(m K), \(\rho=917\) kg/m³, \(L_f=334\) kJ/kg and \(c_p=2.1\) kJ/(kg K). With a wall at −5 °C the Stefan number is 0.0314. The ideal-wall time to form 10 mm is \(917(334000)(0.01)^2/[2(2.2)(5)]=1392\) s, about 23 minutes. Reaching 20 mm requires four times that duration. The increasing conduction distance accounts for the slowing front.

With coolant at −5 °C and \(h=200\) W/(m² K), the added time for 10 mm is 3063 s. The resulting total is about 74.2 minutes. At that point the film resistance is 0.005 m² K/W and the ice contributes 0.00455 m² K/W. Both terms are of the same order. Vary h and find when a second thickness measurement would provide more information about ice conductivity than a first one.

At a fixed coolant temperature compare the early and late parts of the thickness histories. Increase the film coefficient strongly. Predict where the finite-film curve should approach the fixed-wall result first in relative terms, then read the completion time.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · recover the coolant coefficient from one observation

A 15 mm layer is observed after 8000 s with the same properties and coolant temperature. Estimate h from the integrated equation. Would a time below the ideal-wall value be compatible with a positive h in this model?

Show the resistance fit

The ideal-wall contribution is about 3132 s. The remaining 4868 s equals \(\rho L_f s/[h(T_m-T_c)]\), giving \(h\simeq189\) W/(m² K). Both resistances are positive. A shorter time than the ideal-wall prediction would require checking the temperature, initial ice thickness, geometry or material data before assigning a heat-transfer coefficient.

Chapter 17 · Diffusion with a stationary carrier gas

A solvent evaporates into a gas layer. Its molecules diffuse relative to the mixture, while evaporation also induces a bulk molar flow. Work through the species balances to see why the flux ceases to scale linearly with surface concentration.

Choose the reference frame for a flux

Let A be the evaporating species and B an insoluble carrier. A planar film of thickness L lies between the evaporating surface, at mole fraction \(y_s\), and a well-mixed reservoir at \(y_\infty<y_s\). Keep total pressure, temperature, total molar concentration \(c=p/(RT)\) and binary diffusivity D uniform. In a stationary laboratory frame the total species flux equals diffusion relative to the molar-average velocity plus transport by that velocity. If positive z points towards the reservoir, write

\[N_A=-cD{dy_A\over dz}+y_A(N_A+N_B).\]

Steady conditions in one dimension make \(N_A,N_B\) independent of z. Carrier B cannot enter the liquid and has zero net flux there. This fixes \(N_B=0\) throughout the film. B still diffuses towards the interface relative to the moving mixture. Its diffusive flux balances its outward convective flux. The constraint \(N_A+N_B=0\) would instead describe equimolar counterdiffusion between two exchanging species.

Separate variables and enforce both boundary concentrations

Collect the terms in \(N_A\), divide by \(1-y_A\) and integrate from the surface to the reservoir. The remaining integral is a logarithm of the carrier mole fraction.

\[N_A(1-y_A)=-cD{dy_A\over dz},\]
\[N_A={cD\over L}\ln{1-y_\infty\over 1-y_s}.\]
\[1-y_A(z)=(1-y_s)\left({1-y_\infty\over1-y_s}\right)^{z/L}.\]

The concentration profile curves because a given net flux requires a different concentration gradient at different carrier fractions. A dilute expansion of the logarithm gives

\[N_A\simeq(cD/L)(y_s-y_\infty).\]

The exact-to-dilute flux ratio exceeds unity when the surface emits A. The discrepancy becomes measurable long before the carrier disappears. At fractions very close to unity, the assumptions of a stagnant, isothermal film also deserve new scrutiny.

Small and large amounts of vapour at the surface

At 300 K and 1 bar, \(c=40.09\) mol/m³. Take \(D=2.0\times10^{-5}\) m²/s and \(L=5\) mm. The coefficient \(cD/L=0.1604\) mol/(m² s). With a vapour-free reservoir and \(y_s=0.05\), the logarithm is 0.05129 and the predicted flux is 0.00823 mol/(m² s). A linear gradient would give 0.00802, about 2.5% lower.

For \(y_s=0.50\), the flux rises to 0.111 mol/(m² s), versus 0.0802 for the dilute estimate. At mid-depth the exact fraction is \(1-\sqrt{0.5}=0.293\), while linear interpolation gives 0.25. An experimental check can therefore use either a mass-loss measurement or a local concentration measurement. Evaporative cooling changes the saturation mole fraction at the surface, so a measured surface temperature matters even if room temperature seems stable.

Use a vapour-free reservoir and increase the surface fraction from 0.10 to 0.80. Estimate which midpoint concentration lies farther from linear interpolation. Compare the resulting flux errors and identify the physical transport term responsible.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · recover the effective film thickness

At the same temperature and diffusivity the surface mole fraction is 0.30, the reservoir fraction is 0.05 and a balance reports \(N_A=0.050\) mol/(m² s). Estimate L. What film thickness would be inferred if the outward molar flow were ignored?

Show the two fits

Solving the exact expression gives

\[L=cD\ln(0.95/0.70)/N_A=4.90\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The linear fit yields

\[L=cD(0.25)/N_A=4.01\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The latter fit makes the film thinner to compensate for omitted advection. In an experiment that effective thickness would predict the wrong flux when the surface composition changes appreciably.

Further reading · Lienhard and Lienhard · A Heat Transfer Textbook, chapter 11 on mass transfer by diffusion

Chapter 18 · Reading a vapour-compression refrigerator from its four enthalpies

A cooling machine circulates refrigerant through a compressor, condenser, valve and evaporator. Follow one kilogram around the circuit and see why compressor efficiency and liquid subcooling change the coefficient of performance by different mechanisms.

Write separate steady-flow balances on each component

State 1 is low-pressure vapour at compressor inlet. State 2 is high-pressure vapour at the discharge. At 3 the condenser delivers high-pressure liquid, which throttles to the evaporator pressure at 4. Neglect velocity and height changes and assume insulated compressor and valve. For the valve the flow energy balance gives

\[h_4=h_3.\]

For the other devices write positive magnitudes for useful cooling, input work and rejected heat:

\[q_L=h_1-h_4,\]
\[w_c=h_2-h_1,\]
\[q_H=h_2-h_3,\]
\[{\rm COP}_R={q_L\over w_c}.\]

Summing the component balances gives

\[q_H=q_L+w_c.\]

The COP may exceed one because a refrigerator transports heat using work. The maximum for two specified external reservoir temperatures is \(T_L/(T_H-T_L)\) in kelvins. No particular refrigerant's enthalpy table is implied in the following illustrative property data. In a design calculation determine the properties at consistent pressure, temperature or quality from one validated refrigerant source.

Get the compressor exit and valve exit by different paths

At specified discharge pressure define \(h_{2s}\) by the same entropy as state 1. The isentropic efficiency of the compressor is \(\eta_c=(h_{2s}-h_1)/(h_2-h_1)\). Rearranging gives

\[h_2=h_1+(h_{2s}-h_1)/\eta_c.\]

At the valve exit, if \(h_f<h_4<h_g\) at evaporator pressure, the vapour mass fraction follows from \(h_4=(1-x_4)h_f+x_4h_g\). Entropy increases through the valve even though enthalpy is constant. Assigning a constant entropy there would alter both flash quality and refrigeration effect.

\[x_4={h_3-h_f\over h_g-h_f},\]
\[\dot m={\dot Q_L\over h_1-h_3},\]
\[\dot W_c=\dot m\,{h_{2s}-h_1\over\eta_c}.\]

Keeping evaporation and condensation pressures fixed isolates the role of each state change. In an installed system the pressures, compressor map, flow and heat exchangers interact. A subcooler with a separate electrical load must also be included when evaluating a whole-system COP.

Evaluate a baseline and a subcooled variant

Take \(h_f=200,\ h_g=h_1=400,\ h_{2s}=430,\ h_3=250\) kJ/kg with \(\eta_c=0.75\). The actual outlet has \(h_2=440\) kJ/kg, cooling per kilogram is 150 kJ/kg and compressor input is 40 kJ/kg. The evaporator inlet is 25% vapour by mass, and COP is 3.75. At 6.0 kW cooling demand the flow is 0.0400 kg/s, compressor input 1.60 kW and rejected heat 7.60 kW.

At the same pressure levels and compressor inlet state, lower \(h_3\) by 10 kJ/kg before throttling. Valve-exit quality becomes \(40/200=0.20\), and 160 kJ/kg can be absorbed in the evaporator. COP becomes 4.00. For the specified cooling duty the flow falls to 0.0375 kg/s and compressor power to 1.50 kW. By comparison raising compressor efficiency from 0.75 to 0.85 with the original condenser exit reduces the work per kilogram to 35.29 kJ/kg and gives COP 4.25 at the original mass flow. One change acts on refrigeration effect and the other on compression work.

At unchanged compressor efficiency reduce the liquid enthalpy by 20 kJ/kg. Predict how much flash vapour is avoided for each kilogram passing the valve. Then reduce compressor efficiency and determine which of the two changes affects inlet quality.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · diagnose a measured power excess

A rig with the original inlet and condenser enthalpies delivers 5.4 kW cooling while the measured refrigerant-side shaft input is 1.8 kW. Infer mass flow, actual outlet enthalpy and compressor isentropic efficiency. An electrical measurement gives 2.0 kW. What additional efficiency does that measurement quantify?

Close the component boundaries

The evaporator gives \(\dot m=5.4/150=0.0360\) kg/s. The shaft input gives \(w_c=1.8/0.0360=50.0\) kJ/kg and \(h_2=450\) kJ/kg. Hence

\[\eta_c=30/50=0.60.\]

With 2.0 kW electrical input, the upstream motor/drive delivers \(1.8/2.0=0.90\) of that power to the shaft. Shaft-based COP is 3.0, electrical COP is 2.7. Comparing measured COP values requires identifying which boundary each power meter encloses.

Chapter 19 · Finding the vapour fraction of an equilibrium flash

A mixture enters a separator at given overall composition, pressure and temperature. Some components prefer the vapour more strongly than others. Derive a scalar equation for the fraction that evaporates and test for a two-phase solution before iterating.

Connect composition to the equilibrium ratios

Let \(F=L+V\) be the total molar feed and liquid and vapour flow rates. Overall component fractions are \(z_i\), liquid fractions \(x_i\) and vapour fractions \(y_i\). At equilibrium the chemical potential of each species agrees across phases. In an ideal gas over an ideal liquid this reduces to Raoult's law \(y_i p=x_i p_i^{\rm sat}(T)\), so \(K_i=y_i/x_i=p_i^{\rm sat}(T)/p\). A larger K enriches that species in the vapour. For a real mixture K can depend on phase composition and require an outer property iteration. Here use fixed positive K values to isolate the material balance.

Eliminate the component flows

Define \(\beta=V/F\). Component conservation gives

\[z_i=(1-\beta)x_i+\beta y_i=x_i[1+\beta(K_i-1)].\]

Thus \(x_i=z_i/[1+\beta(K_i-1)]\). For both compositions to sum to one, enforce \(\sum_i(y_i-x_i)=0\), giving the Rachford–Rice equation:

\[R(\beta)=\sum_i{z_i(K_i-1)\over1+\beta(K_i-1)}=0,\]
\[R'(\beta)=-\sum_i{z_i(K_i-1)^2\over[1+\beta(K_i-1)]^2}.\]

The denominators are positive for \(0\le\beta\le1\). With at least one non-unity K of positive feed fraction, R decreases strictly, so at most one physical root exists. A positive R(0) and a negative R(1) bracket two phases. If \(R(0)\le0\) the stable fixed-K split is all liquid; if \(R(1)\ge0\) it is all vapour, with equality corresponding to incipient phase appearance. When every K is one this balance cannot specify \(\beta\). A bracketed numerical method preserves the meaningful interval even near a phase boundary.

Perform a binary flash and change the feed composition

Choose \(K_A=2,\ K_B=0.5,\ z_A=z_B=0.5\). Then

\[R(0)=0.25,\ R(1)=-0.25.\]

Multiplying by the two denominators gives \(0.25-0.50\beta=0\), hence \(\beta=0.50\). Liquid fractions are \(x_A=1/3,\ x_B=2/3\), and vapour fractions are

\[y_A=2/3,\ y_B=1/3.\]

For 10 mol/s feed each outlet carries 5 mol/s. The A balance reads \(5(1/3)+5(2/3)=5\) mol/s.

Increase \(z_A\) to 0.60 at the same T, p and K. The physical root becomes \(\beta=0.80\). The phase compositions remain at the same two endpoints; their proportions change to accommodate the new feed. Indeed the overall A fraction is

\[0.20(1/3)+0.80(2/3)=0.60.\]

At \(z_A=0.75\) the feed lies beyond the vapour endpoint. Then R(1) is positive, and searching outside the interval would produce a vapour fraction above unity. Report a single vapour phase instead. An adiabatic flash at unknown temperature would require a further enthalpy balance.

Start from equal feed fractions and increase the fraction of A until the root leaves the physical interval. Predict which pure-phase endpoint is reached. Compare residual signs at both endpoints before interpreting the reported phase.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · determine whether an iteration is warranted

At an operating point take \(K_A=3,\ K_B=0.6\). Compute the liquid and vapour A fractions when both phases coexist. For \(z_A=0.30\), calculate \(\beta\). Repeat the phase test for \(z_A=0.10\) and explain why normalized hypothetical phase compositions can hide a failed balance.

Solve phase closure and the lever rule

Vapour closure gives \(3x_A+0.6(1-x_A)=1\), hence \(x_A=1/6\), \(y_A=1/2\). For overall fraction 0.30,

\[\beta=(0.30-1/6)/(1/2-1/6)=0.40.\]

At \(z_A=0.10\),

\[R(0)=0.10(2)+0.90(-0.4)=-0.16.\]

The feed remains liquid. Renormalizing an unphysical trial vapour composition could make it sum to unity while violating the original component and equilibrium equations. Check the physical endpoint signs first.

Chapter 20 · Where a thermoelectric cooler rejects its heat

A detector has to operate ten kelvin below the laboratory air temperature. A thermoelectric module beneath it can transport heat against that temperature difference when an electric current flows. Increasing the current also generates heat inside the module and warms its heat sink. Build an energy balance that predicts when additional current helps, when it costs more than it delivers and why a nominal cooling-capacity figure alone cannot specify an operating point.

Identify the two faces and the power supply

Treat the detector plate as an isothermal cold face at \(T_c\) and the face in contact with the heat sink as an isothermal hot face at \(T_h\). The laboratory air is at temperature \(T_a\). All three temperatures enter the calculation in kelvin. Temperature differences in kelvin and degrees Celsius have the same numerical value, but the Peltier term contains an absolute temperature. A calculation using a cold-side value of 15 instead of 288 would suppress its principal heat-transport mechanism.

Define \(Q_c\) as the steady heat absorbed from the cold plate and \(Q_h\) as the steady heat given to the sink, both in watts. The electrical power entering the module is \(IV\). The complete module has no net energy storage in steady state, hence \(Q_h=Q_c+IV\). For a detector dissipating 15 W, its refrigeration requirement may be larger than this dissipation if wiring or insulation admits additional environmental heat. The load balance belongs to the detector, while the module characteristic supplies the deterministic prediction of what it can remove at specified temperatures.

Use effective parameters for the assembled device: Seebeck coefficient \(\alpha\) in V/K, electrical resistance \(R\) in ohms and thermal conductance \(K\) in W/K. Current flows through the series electrical elements, while the thermoelectric legs supply largely parallel paths for heat conduction. An effective module coefficient cannot be silently replaced by the coefficient of a single leg. Manufacturer data can vary with temperature, mechanical mounting and electrical contact. The constant-property approximation used here permits an exact algebraic check before introducing fitted temperature-dependent coefficients.

Separate transported heat, back conduction and ohmic heating

A reversible thermoelectric contact transports heat \(\Pi I\) where its Peltier coefficient is

\[\Pi=\alpha T.\]

This relation follows from the Kelvin connection between the reversible Peltier and Seebeck effects. With the sign of current chosen for cooling, the cold junction removes \(\alpha T_c I\) from the plate. Heat conducts down the temperature gradient from the hot face toward the cold one at a rate \(K(T_h-T_c)\), reducing the net amount the module can absorb from the detector.

Resistance generates \(I^2R\) of heat throughout the legs. The familiar one-half allocation can be derived rather than treated as bookkeeping by convention. In a homogeneous leg of constant thermal conductivity and equal cross section, a uniform volumetric heat source produces a parabolic correction to the imposed linear temperature profile. The source-driven correction vanishes at both isothermal ends and its slopes there have equal and opposite signs. Each face therefore receives half of the internally generated heat. A different material arrangement or strongly temperature-dependent coefficients can change that split.

\[Q_c=\alpha T_c I-\tfrac12 I^2R-K(T_h-T_c),\]
\[Q_h=\alpha T_h I+\tfrac12 I^2R-K(T_h-T_c).\]

Subtracting the two equations gives

\[Q_h-Q_c=I[\alpha(T_h-T_c)+IR].\]

Thus the electrical terminal voltage compatible with these heat flows is \(V=IR+\alpha(T_h-T_c)\). The second term is the back electromotive force associated with the imposed temperature difference. Omitting it underestimates the input power and inflates the reported coefficient of performance. As a sign check, set \(I=0\). Heat still leaks through a module with \(T_h>T_c\); \(Q_c\) is then negative.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Close the hot-side balance and solve for temperature

A heat sink with thermal resistance \(R_s\) must satisfy

\[T_h=T_a+R_sQ_h.\]

Insert the hot-face expression above, collect every occurrence of \(T_h\) on the left, and solve before evaluating the cold-face capacity. The result is

\[T_h=\frac{T_a+R_s(KT_c+I^2R/2)}{1+R_s(K-\alpha I)}.\]

When \(R_s=0\), the hot junction stays at ambient temperature. For finite \(R_s\) it responds to the rejected heat, which includes the electrical input. A steady-state candidate also requires a positive stabilising slope in the hot-face balance. With a finite hot-side heat capacity the linear coefficient of a temperature perturbation is proportional to \(\alpha I-K-1/R_s\). The denominator in the formula is positive precisely when this perturbation decays, for \(R_s>0\). Extremely large currents can invalidate constant material properties well before a formal singularity is reached.

Change the physical and numerical inputs of the notebook model. Compare the updated curves and balance checks with the recorded notebook figures. Each plot uses the same equations and assumptions documented in that notebook; its readout names any analytical or reduced-model reference.
