In the example its value stays 2.50 square micrometres per second for every sample orientation, while the determinant stays one square micrometre to the fourth per second squared. A comparison of two camera orientations therefore provides numerical checks independent of the fitted angle. A drift mistakenly left in the displacement covariance can violate the expected linear dependence on lag, making the inferred effective eigenvalues grow with the selected fitting window. Subtract the ensemble mean displacement at each lag and record that mean separately as a flow measurement. Conversely do not subtract an individual best-fit line from every short trajectory without modelling the resulting loss of long-time fluctuations; that procedure can bias both diffusion coefficients downward.

Choose an integration window for a transport coefficient

Integrate an oscillatory velocity correlation over a finite record. Compare a short apparent plateau with the independently computed infinite-time result.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · Invert diffusion measurements at two lags

In a one-dimensional diffusive window a measured displacement variance is 0.024 square micrometres at 10 ms and 0.064 at 50 ms. Assume long-time Brownian displacements plus independent frame-localisation variance per position measurement. Estimate \(D\) and localisation precision.

Fit slope and intercept separately

The variance slope is \((0.064-0.024)/(0.050-0.010)=1.00\) square micrometre per second, so \(D=0.500\) square micrometres per second. The intercept is 0.014 square micrometres, equal to twice the single-frame localisation variance. The implied rms localisation error is \(\sqrt{0.007}=0.0837\) micrometres. Forcing the fit through the origin at the shortest lag would give \(D=1.20\), more than twice the inferred value.

Acquire more lags to test the linear model. A static drift contributes a term growing as \(t^2\) if its mean displacement is not removed, while finite exposure can lower the physically measured short-lag variance. Two points always define a line and cannot independently validate either noise model.

Tutorial · Estimate a hidden slow tail in viscosity

A stress correlation is modelled as \(A[e^{-t/\tau}+0.02e^{-t/(40\tau)}]\). What fraction of its true area comes from the low-amplitude tail? How much does a cutoff at \(5\tau\) capture?

Weight a correlation by its duration

The fast and slow areas are \(A\tau\) and \(0.80A\tau\). The slow contribution is 44.4 percent of total. Integration to five fast times captures \(A\tau[(1-e^{-5})+0.80(1-e^{-5/40})]\simeq1.087A\tau\), or about 60.4 percent. Visual disappearance of the dominant peak therefore does not establish convergence of the transport coefficient.

The slow contribution must be supported by independent long blocks or a physically motivated model. A fitted tiny constant baseline can generate arbitrarily large apparent area if extrapolated indefinitely. Subtracting a sample mean estimated from the same short record can conversely suppress a genuine slow mode. Compare records much longer than the proposed tail time before trusting the extrapolation.

Tutorial · Is an algebraic tail compatible with ordinary diffusion?

Suppose a measured long-time velocity correlation remains positive and scales as \(t^{-\alpha}\). Determine when its Green–Kubo area exists and infer the displacement growth for \(0<\alpha<1\) and for \(\alpha=1\). Why would a finite periodic simulation require care?

Integrate the time-dependent slope before naming a diffusion coefficient

For \(\alpha>1\) the tail integral converges. For \(0<\alpha<1\), the running area grows as \(t^{1-\alpha}\), so displacement variance grows as \(t^{2-\alpha}\) under the stated persistent positive tail. At \(\alpha=1\), the running area grows logarithmically and the displacement variance is of order \(t\log t\). Sign oscillations, finite-size termination or another late-time crossover can alter these conclusions.

A periodic box admits only discrete long-wavelength modes and may cut off a hydrodynamic tail. Repeating at larger sizes and comparing the range before signals traverse the box helps identify a bulk regime. Unwrap particle coordinates crossing periodic boundaries before calculating a displacement. A wrapped coordinate can falsely saturate and imitate a confined particle even in a freely diffusing periodic fluid.

Cambridge Kinetic Theory · correlations, response and transport

Work fluctuations and equilibrium free-energy differences

Statistical mechanics from microscopic counting to material response

Repeated rapid changes of a laboratory control can give a broad distribution of work even when every run follows the same prescribed protocol. Derive why a particular exponential average retrieves an equilibrium free-energy difference, why the average work is generally larger, and why a finite experiment can miss the rare trajectories that make the equality true.

Specify the system, preparation and measured work

Let a classical system have Hamiltonian \(H(z,\lambda)\), with microstate \(z\) and an externally set parameter \(\lambda\). Initially it is sampled from the canonical equilibrium distribution at \(\lambda_A\) and temperature \(T\). A prescribed protocol \(\lambda(t)\) carries it to \(\lambda_B\). Work done on the system along one trajectory is the inclusive parameter work

\[W=\int_0^{t_f}\dot\lambda\,\partial_\lambda H(z(t),\lambda(t))\,dt,\]
\[\Delta F=F(\lambda_B,T)-F(\lambda_A,T).\]

If \(\lambda\) is a trap centre, the generalised force in this convention is the derivative of energy with respect to that centre. A transducer reporting the opposite force exerted by the system on the actuator needs a sign change. For a stochastic subsystem in contact with a heat bath, a change of subsystem energy also includes heat. It is not generally equal to the measured protocol work. Specify the tip, trap, sample and bath boundary before turning a force trace into a work distribution.

Every run must begin from the same equilibrium preparation, not simply from whichever end state the previous protocol produced. The final state immediately after a fast drive need not be equilibrium. The free energy being inferred refers to equilibrium endpoint Hamiltonians at the same temperature. Equilibrating after the drive exchanges heat but involves no additional protocol work when the control is held fixed. Changing control during that relaxation would define a different protocol.

A Hamiltonian proof using phase-space volume

First treat an isolated driven Hamiltonian system after canonical preparation. Work along a trajectory equals \(H_B(z_f)-H_A(z_i)\). Multiply \(e^{-\beta W}\) by the initial canonical weight and integrate over initial phase space. The dependence on initial energy cancels. Hamiltonian dynamics preserves phase-space volume and is invertible, so change variables from initial to final point to obtain

\[\left\langle e^{-\beta W}\right\rangle_A=\frac1{Z_A}\int dz_i\,e^{-\beta H_B(z_f)}=\frac{Z_B}{Z_A}=e^{-\beta\Delta F}.\]

Thus the Jarzynski equality follows without assuming the driving is slow. This proof requires the mapping to cover the relevant final phase space with the stated measure; idealised instantaneous removal of infinitely high constraints can create subtleties of support. For a weakly coupled thermal environment include bath variables in the Hamiltonian derivation or use a stochastic dynamics satisfying appropriate local detailed balance. Arbitrary feedback, nonequilibrium initial preparation or an unmodelled strong bath coupling does not automatically obey this simple subsystem formula.

Jensen's inequality gives

\[\langle W\rangle\ge\Delta F,\]

with equality for a deterministically reversible work distribution in the relevant limit. An individual run may give less work than \(\Delta F\), sometimes even work of the opposite sign. This does not violate the ensemble inequality. The exponential average weights such small-work events more strongly than their raw frequency indicates. The larger the typical dissipated work, the more difficult a direct estimate often becomes.

Solve a complete finite-time example analytically

Take one overdamped particle equilibrated in a one-dimensional harmonic trap \(H(x,\lambda)=k(x-\lambda)^2/2\) at \(\lambda=0\). Suddenly shift the centre to \(d\) while the particle position cannot respond. The endpoint free energy is unchanged by translation in an unbounded uniform bath. Initial \(x\) is Gaussian with mean zero and variance \(k_BT/k\). During the instantaneous shift

\[W=\frac k2[(x-d)^2-x^2]=\frac{kd^2}{2}-kdx .\]

Work is Gaussian with mean \(kd^2/2\), variance \(kd^2k_BT\), and \(\Delta F=0\). Evaluating the Gaussian moment generates \(e^{-\beta\langle W\rangle+\beta^2\operatorname{var}W/2}=1\), verifying the equality exactly at arbitrary shift distance. The mechanism is visible in the trajectories: particles already on the downstream side can lose potential energy in the sudden control shift, contributing negative work and disproportionately large exponential weights.

For \(d=2\sqrt{k_BT/k}\), the mean work is two thermal energies and the standard deviation is also two thermal energies. The probability of negative work is about 0.159. For \(d=5\sqrt{k_BT/k}\), mean work is \(12.5k_BT\), standard deviation \(5k_BT\), and negative work occurs only about 0.0062 of the time. Typical runs can all show substantial positive work even though the correct free-energy change for the whole translation is zero.

The most important work values for the exponential average are not simply all values below zero. Multiplying a Gaussian \(P(W)\) by \(e^{-\beta W}\) shifts its peak to \(\langle W\rangle-\beta\operatorname{var}W\), which for this trap shift is \(-\langle W\rangle\). In the five-width shift example that important region is five standard deviations below the ordinary work mean. Observing a few negative work values can still leave the estimate badly biased if the most heavily weighted part remains unsampled.

Compare forward and reverse trajectory probabilities

Define the reverse protocol by reversing the order of control values, and reverse momenta and any time-odd external parameters as required by microscopic time reversal. Prepare its initial system independently in equilibrium at \(B\). Under microscopic reversibility the probability ratio of a forward path and its time-reversed counterpart has a boundary weight from the initial ensembles and a conditional path weight tied to heat exchange. These combine to give \(e^{\beta(W-\Delta F)}\). Grouping paths by work produces the Crooks relation

\[\frac{P_F(W)}{P_R(-W)}=e^{\beta(W-\Delta F)} .\]

Where the two work densities coincide, \(W=\Delta F\). Integrating the relation against \(e^{-\beta W}\) recovers Jarzynski. The reverse work axis must be reflected: a crossing of two histograms plotted with the same unflipped work sign generally has no such interpretation. A finite bin estimates an integral over a work interval, so discretisation and sparse counts modify a naive pointwise log-ratio fit.

For a more general measured Gaussian forward work distribution with mean \(5.0k_BT\) and variance \(4.0(k_BT)^2\), the equality predicts \(\Delta F=3.0k_BT\) if the Gaussian law including tails is justified. With equal-variance reverse statistics, the corresponding mean of \(-W_R\) is \(1.0k_BT\). The two densities cross at \(3.0k_BT\). Plotting \(\log[P_F(W)/P_R(-W)]\) against \(W\) should give slope \(\beta\), providing a check on independent force and temperature calibration. Good agreement over a tiny overlap range does not constrain remote tails strongly.

Use the finite estimator honestly

From independent forward works \(W_i\), estimate \(\Delta F\) as minus \(k_BT\) times the logarithm of their mean exponential weight. The sample mean of weights is unbiased under correct sampling, but its logarithm is nonlinear. Jensen implies the finite-sample free-energy estimate is biased upward on average under the ideal assumptions. A single dataset can fluctuate either way. An ordinary mean-work error bar does not describe uncertainty in an exponential moment.

For Gaussian work the squared coefficient of variation of one exponential weight is \(e^{\beta^2\operatorname{var}W}-1\). With \(d=2\) thermal widths it equals

\[e^4-1=53.6.\]

To estimate the mean weight to ten percent relative standard error by independent averaging would require roughly 5360 runs under that distribution. At five thermal widths the required count is dramatically higher. The nominal number of runs is not a sufficient convergence diagnostic: inspect the fraction of weight carried by the largest events and compare independent batches.

For numerical stability shift all dimensionless logarithmic weights by their largest value before exponentiating, then add it back after taking the logarithm. This prevents overflow without repairing missing rare events. Bootstrapping a dataset with no observations in the relevant tail cannot generate them. More gradual driving, intermediate equilibrium windows or a bidirectional estimator using forward and reverse overlap can reduce the difficulty. Each approach requires its own equilibrium preparations and sufficient overlap, not just a different final formula applied to the same deficient sample.

Discretise a real force trace without changing the work definition

For a measured optical trap centre \(\lambda_j\) and positions \(x_j\), actual control updates and acquisition clocks may be staggered. If the control moves instantaneously from \(\lambda_j\) to \(\lambda_{j+1}\) at fixed \(x_j\), its exact contribution is \(H(x_j,\lambda_{j+1})-H(x_j,\lambda_j)\). A smoothly moving control instead uses an integral of the measured generalised force, with a quadrature matched to timestamps and sampling bandwidth. A midpoint convention applied to an unresolved abrupt jump can miss a finite quadratic term.

For the harmonic trap the instantaneous work above is exactly \(k[(\lambda_j+\lambda_{j+1})/2-x_j](\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_j)\). Taking only the initial force would give a difference \(k(\lambda_{j+1}-\lambda_j)^2/2\) per jump. Over many short smooth increments this bias may vanish under refinement, but for a deliberate quench it is the entire mean work if the initial mean displacement is zero. The distinction can be checked by reversing the jump and independently measuring the equilibrium spatial variance.

Compare irreversible work and relaxation with the course's independently defined entropy-balance experiment

Derive the dissipated work for a constant-speed pull

Between infinitely slow displacements and a sudden quench lies an exactly soluble finite-duration example. For a harmonic trap translated at constant velocity \(u\) in an overdamped bath, the mean coordinate obeys \(\zeta\,d\langle x\rangle/dt=-k(\langle x\rangle-\lambda)\). Begin equilibrated at zero and set \(\lambda(t)=ut\) for \(0<t<t_f\). The mean lag \(\lambda-\langle x\rangle\) obeys a first-order equation and grows from zero toward \(u\tau\), with \(\tau=\zeta/k\). The mean force exerted by the actuator in this sign convention is \(k(\lambda-\langle x\rangle)\). Integrating the product of this force with control speed gives

\[\langle W\rangle=\zeta u^2[t_f-\tau(1-e^{-t_f/\tau})].\]

The free-energy change for translating this uniform trap is zero. At a fixed displacement \(d=ut_f\), taking \(t_f\ll\tau\) gives \(kd^2/2\), precisely the sudden-quench mean. As \(t_f\gg\tau\) the main term is \(\zeta d^2/t_f\), tending to zero for a quasistatic pull. Simply keeping speed fixed while taking a longer time is a different limit because the endpoint then moves farther.

The fluctuating part of \(x(t)\) around its deterministic mean remains an equilibrium Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process in this additive linear model. Work is a linear functional of that Gaussian process and is therefore Gaussian. Its variance follows by integrating its covariance twice, giving \(\operatorname{var}W=2k_BT\langle W\rangle\). This recovers the exponential equality at every pull duration and supplies a nontrivial benchmark for a stochastic integrator. The probability of extracting work can be computed from the resulting Gaussian tail without assuming a second ensemble.

For a pull of two thermal widths completed in one relaxation time, use \(\zeta d^2/t_f=kd^2=4k_BT\). The bracketed dimensionless fraction is \(1-(1-e^{-1})=e^{-1}\), so mean work is \(1.47k_BT\) and standard deviation about \(1.72k_BT\). A sudden pull of the same distance has higher mean \(2k_BT\). A pull lasting five relaxation times reduces the mean to \(4k_BT[5-(1-e^{-5})]/25=0.641k_BT\). These changes predict which protocol will yield a usable work-overlap region for a finite number of trials.

A measurement can test this crossover without fitting free energy at all. Calibrate stiffness from an equilibrium position distribution, obtain the relaxation time from a correlation or driven response and predict the entire mean-work curve versus duration. Then assess Jarzynski estimates independently. Failure of the predicted variance-to-mean relation can reveal nonharmonic confinement, non-Gaussian bath forces, actuator timing offsets or insufficient equilibration. It need not invalidate the fluctuation identity itself.

A two-outcome work distribution shows how rare events restore equality

A particularly transparent work distribution comes from a sudden change in a two-level system. Initially its ground energy is zero and upper energy \(2k_BT\). In a sudden protocol move the upper level to zero while leaving the initial ground energy unchanged. The initial upper probability is

\[p=e^{-2}/(1+e^{-2})=0.1192.\]

Work can take only two values: zero for an initially ground system and minus two thermal energies for a particle initially upper. Both new energy levels are degenerate, so the exact equilibrium partition ratio is

\[2/(1+e^{-2})=1.762.\]

The exponential average equals \((1-p)+pe^2=1.762\), giving \(\Delta F=-0.566k_BT\). Mean work is only \(-0.238k_BT\), greater than this equilibrium free-energy change as required. With five independent realisations the probability of observing no upper preparation is

\[(1-p)^5=0.530.\]

More than half such tiny samples would report zero work every time and infer a free-energy change of zero. A bootstrap resampling those five identical observations would report no empirical uncertainty at all, spectacularly missing the known sampling limitation.

Increase the initial gap to \(6k_BT\) before the same quench to degeneracy. The initial upper probability falls to 0.00247, but its exponential weight rises to \(e^6\). Its mean contribution to the exponential estimator remains near one. Even one hundred runs then miss this branch with probability about 0.78. Equality relies on the true preparation probability of a rare event combined with its enormous statistical weight. A finite dataset cannot certify absence of the branch by having no observations of it.

The reverse experiment begins with two equally populated degenerate states and raises one level. It samples the formerly rare branch far more easily. Reflect its raw works onto the forward work axis and compare point masses, not unrelated smooth histogram densities. A forward and reverse data combination then constrains the free-energy difference much more effectively. A fitted reverse sample must still begin at equilibrium in the degenerate endpoint; carrying the low excited-state fraction over from the forward final state would destroy its intended initial ensemble.

Instrumental work noise is amplified by the exponential

Suppose each genuine trajectory work is recorded with an independent Gaussian error of mean zero and variance \(s_W^2\). The arithmetic mean work stays unbiased. The mean exponential weight is multiplied by \(\exp(\beta^2s_W^2/2)\), so a naive Jarzynski estimate of free energy shifts downward by \(\beta s_W^2/2\) in energy units in the infinite-record limit. This systematic shift is distinct from the finite-record logarithm bias, which tends upward on average for ideal correctly measured weights.

For rms measurement error \(0.5k_BT\), this Gaussian-noise effect is \(-0.125k_BT\). A small positive finite-sampling bias can accidentally cancel it, producing an apparently correct number with both the work measurement and ensemble estimate wrong. Vary the number of independent trajectories and the instrument noise separately. Calibrate work reconstruction on repeated known control or force signals to estimate the error distribution rather than assuming it is Gaussian from its rms alone.

If measurement error correlates with the trajectory—for example a saturated force transducer clips the largest restoring forces—the factorisation above fails. Errors in low-work tails can matter most even if a force trace looks accurate over the bulk of ordinary values. Compare independent sensors or forward and reverse force calibrations across the full visited range. A threshold discarding negative work as unphysical would systematically delete exactly the values the equality needs.

A related problem occurs when temperature itself is inferred from the same finite fluctuation record. Temperature uncertainty affects both the exponent and conversion of the final logarithm to energy. A Gaussian work model gives an analytic sensitivity, but outside that model propagate uncertainty by re-evaluating the full weighted estimator over independently plausible temperatures and work calibrations. Treat correlations between calibration and work estimates appropriately; merely quoting the precision of the bath set point does not establish the temperature of a fast, weakly equilibrated preparation.

Verify a stochastic work protocol on a two-state network

A small simulated system with discrete energies and heat-bath jumps supplies an independent test of a coupled dynamics and work implementation. At each step change a control value instantaneously while preserving current state; record the corresponding energy difference as parameter work. Then allow a period of fixed-control stochastic relaxation whose transition probabilities preserve the new canonical distribution. Energy change during that relaxation is heat, not additional protocol work. Define the reverse process by precisely reversing the sequence of controlled changes and using an independent equilibrium start at its endpoint.

An exact proof for such a process does not require a continuum limit. After one energy change, multiplying the previous equilibrium weights by the exponential work converts them into unnormalised canonical weights of the new levels. A stochastic transition preserving these new weights leaves that weighted distribution unchanged. Apply the same argument successively and sum the final weighted probabilities to retrieve the ratio of endpoint partition functions. Each trajectory can jump many times, but the weighted identity follows from a short sequence of matrix operations.

This proof is a practical verification tool. Enumerate all paths of a two-state system over a few control steps and compare the exact weighted sum with a Monte Carlo estimate. Test a deliberately coarse but precisely specified sequence before refining the time mesh. If direct enumeration fails, inspect signs, equilibrium preparation, order of control and relaxation, and whether the transition kernel truly preserves the prescribed endpoint weights. If enumeration succeeds while sampled averages disagree, inspect rare weights and independent batch variability before modifying the underlying dynamics.

Why postselection changes a work experiment

The standard work equality assumes a specified forward protocol and its initial equilibrium ensemble. Suppose an operator begins with an equilibrium distribution in the harmonic trap but keeps only trajectories whose initial displacement already points toward the intended final centre. Those retained starts are not a canonical ensemble for the original unrestricted trap. The conditional work distribution is systematically shifted toward smaller work, so inserting it into the ordinary exponential average generally produces a biased apparent free-energy change. This selection problem arises even if no macroscopic control action visibly changes between the retained runs.

For a sudden positive shift \(d\), work decreases linearly with initial \(x\). Conditioning on \(x>0\) deletes precisely the high-work half of a symmetric Gaussian preparation. More generally, groups selected with probabilities depending on the measured initial state should be recombined using the true group frequencies if the aim is to estimate the unconditional work moment. An equal-weight average of two conditionally estimated free energies is not equivalent to averaging their exponential weights, because the logarithm is nonlinear.

If the feedback actually chooses the direction of a subsequent shift after measuring the initial position, the protocol itself is now correlated with the state. For example, shifting the trap toward the initial displacement on each run reduces average mechanical work relative to a fixed-direction protocol. The additional information and the measurement/control apparatus are part of the thermodynamic accounting. A direct substitution into the no-feedback equalities derived above would address the wrong experiment. A full information-thermodynamic treatment needs specified measurement probabilities and a well-defined corresponding reverse procedure.

A simple experimental guard is to record every attempted run, including rejected starts, along with selection and trigger conditions. Verify the initial position and energy distributions both before and after data-quality cuts. A cut based on electronic saturation can remove a physically important work tail even if it has no deliberate feedback purpose. Conversely, discarding runs for an independent external electronics failure can be statistically benign when independence is demonstrated. The fact that a cut is operationally convenient is not evidence for such independence.

The same reasoning applies to chained protocols. A sequence of small equilibrium windows can improve overlap, but each initial distribution must be re-equilibrated or appropriately reweighted. Starting each window immediately after the previous rapid drive changes its preparation. A complete path-level method may still treat the sequence consistently; multiplying independent endpoint estimates derived under a false equilibrium assumption generally does not. An explicit record of state preparation, control timing and retained trajectories is as central to a fluctuation-relation measurement as an accurate mechanical force calibration.

A useful stationary control keeps the trap centre fixed but processes position and force data through the same analysis pipeline as a moving-trap run. Correct inclusive parameter work is then zero on every trajectory because the control never changes, even though the particle exchanges random heat with the bath and its potential energy fluctuates. A processed nonzero work distribution in this test reveals that the analysis integrated a different quantity, confused particle displacement with actuator displacement or introduced artificial control noise. Conversely a real translating-trap experiment can finish with the same mean potential energy it had initially after equilibration, while having delivered positive net work and dissipated heat. Tracking both control displacement and particle displacement makes these statements testable trajectory by trajectory where the specified stochastic energy convention permits.

Determine which rare work values dominate a free-energy estimate

Change the preparation gap and the number of repetitions. Compare the average work, the exact free-energy change and the chance of missing the rare branch entirely.

Tutorial · A stiffness quench changes the equilibrium free energy

An equilibrated classical harmonic trap has stiffness \(k\). It is suddenly changed to \(2k\) at the same centre. Derive mean work, exact free-energy difference and verify the exponential equality without assuming Gaussian work.

Integrate the Gaussian position rather than a guessed Gaussian work law

Work is \(W=kx^2/2\), so mean work is \(k_BT/2\). The configurational partition function scales as \(k^{-1/2}\), giving \(\Delta F=(k_BT/2)\log2=0.3466k_BT\). The exponential-weight average is the integral of \(e^{-\beta kx^2/2}\) against the original position density proportional to \(e^{-\beta kx^2/2}\). The combined Gaussian integral yields

\[1/\sqrt2=e^{-\beta\Delta F}.\]

The work is nonnegative and chi-square distributed, not Gaussian. Its mean excess above \(\Delta F\) is about \(0.1534k_BT\).

Tutorial · Estimate a crossing with uncertainty

Measured forward and sign-reflected reverse densities have equal-variance Gaussian shapes with means 6.0 and 2.0 thermal energies. Their standard deviations are both 2.0 thermal energies. Find the crossing, verify the expected logarithmic slope and explain how an unmodelled force offset would affect a displacement protocol.

Complete the square and track the offset through both protocols

The ratio of the two Gaussian densities has logarithmic slope \((6-2)/4=1\) per thermal energy and vanishes at their midpoint \(4.0k_BT\). This is consistent with a free-energy change of four thermal energies. If fitted means have independent standard errors 0.2 and 0.3 thermal energies, their midpoint has standard error \(\sqrt{0.2^2+0.3^2}/2=0.18k_BT\) under the equal-variance model. Unknown unequal variances or poor histogram overlap require a more general fit.

An additive force calibration offset integrated over a known displacement adds a constant work shift proportional to that displacement. The reverse protocol has opposite displacement and hence opposite offset in its raw work, which becomes the same shift after reflecting its work axis. The crossing moves with both histograms and their difference need not reveal the calibration error. Independent force-zero checks matter.

Tutorial · Why an unchanged average dissipated work is insufficient

Two protocols with the same endpoints have the same mean work above equilibrium free-energy change. One has a narrow distribution near that mean and a very rare low-work tail; the other has a broader nearly Gaussian shape. Can equal mean dissipation guarantee comparable convergence of a unidirectional free-energy estimate?

Compare the quantities that govern the estimator

The mean work determines only the first moment. The exponential estimator depends on the entire distribution and its variance depends on the second exponential moment \(\langle e^{-2\beta W}\rangle\), which can differ greatly at fixed \(\langle W\rangle\) and fixed Jarzynski average. A rare low-work tail can dominate both the true mean weight and its sampling error. Identical mean dissipation does not determine its scale or frequency.

Compare forward and reverse overlap over the actual important work interval, analyse independent batches and vary protocol speed. In a proposed experiment verify initial equilibrium at each endpoint, acquisition of the full work history and calibration of temperature and control force. Convergence of a mean mechanical work with repeated trials answers a different question from convergence of an exponential estimate of equilibrium free energy.

Jarzynski · original nonequilibrium equality for free-energy differences

Crooks · entropy production and the forward/reverse work relation

Measure the heat capacity of a reservoir from the width of an energy histogram

Counting, correlations and thermal measurements

Two small Einstein solids share a fixed number of excitation quanta. A calorimeter records how many are in the first solid. Count the allowed microstates, compare the exact distribution with a reservoir approximation and see how energy conservation correlates the readings.

Count the ways to store an integer number of quanta

Let solid A contain \(a\) independent harmonic modes and solid B contain \(b\), all with energy spacing \(\varepsilon\). Omit their fixed zero-point energy. The total excitation count \(Q\) is fixed. In one solid a configuration is an ordered list of nonnegative mode occupations. An arrangement of \(q\) identical excitation symbols and \(a-1\) separators specifies such a list. The multiplicity is \(\binom{q+a-1}{a-1}\). The count for distinguishable packets would be different, because exchanging two excitations between identical occupations introduces no new configuration here.

\[P(q)=\frac{\binom{q+a-1}{a-1}\binom{Q-q+b-1}{b-1}}{\binom{Q+a+b-1}{a+b-1}},\]
\[q=0,\dots,Q.\]

The denominator counts all occupations of the \(a+b\) modes; summing the numerator over every partition must reproduce it. As a simple check, set \(a=2,b=1,Q=2\). The unnormalised weights for \(q=0,1,2\) are 1, 2 and 3. The most probable count is 2, but the mean is \(4/3\). “Thermal equilibrium” does not require every observed energy to coincide with the most likely energy.

Separate the mean from the fluctuations

By permutation symmetry each individual mode holds mean \(Q/(a+b)\). Therefore \(\langle q\rangle=Qa/(a+b)\). For the second moment, introduce an auxiliary variable \(x\) marking quanta in solid A. The coefficient of \(z^Q\) in \(G(x,z)=(1-xz)^{-a}(1-z)^{-b}\) is the unnormalised probability generating function. Differentiating once in \(x\) weights by \(q\), twice by \(q(q-1)\). At \(x=1\) the second derivative is \(a(a+1)z^2(1-z)^{-(a+b+2)}\). Divide its coefficient by the total multiplicity and combine first and second moments. Writing \(s=a+b\) gives

\[\operatorname{var}(q)=\frac{Qab(s+Q)}{s^2(s+1)}.\]

The second solid contains exactly \(Q-q\). Its variance is identical, and the covariance of the two measured energies is minus their common variance in energy units. A pair of independently fitted canonical distributions cannot reproduce this anticorrelation. At \(Q=24,\ a=4,\ b=8\) the mean number in A is 8 and the variance is about 14.77. These are fluctuations of a perfectly isolated combined system, not a leak in the calorimeter.

Background · Can two noisy calorimeters expose the true energy variance?

The two solids exchange energy while their sum is fixed. If the first energy fluctuates upward by \(\delta E\), the other fluctuates downward by exactly the same amount. Let the two recorded energies be \(X=E_1+n_1\) and \(Y=E_2+n_2\). Suppose each readout noise is independent of the physical energies and of the other noise. The same-channel variance and the cross covariance then measure different combinations.

\[\mathrm{var}(X)=\mathrm{var}(E_1)+\mathrm{var}(n_1),\]
\[\mathrm{cov}(X,Y)=-\mathrm{var}(E_1).\]

A single histogram that is too broad can mimic a reservoir with different heat capacity. Simultaneous measurements in the two channels allow the exchanged-energy variance to be recovered from the negative covariance. A drifting bath or common electronic pickup violates the assumptions. The measured variance of \(X+Y\) is a direct diagnostic for such extra fluctuations when the readout noise levels are known.

Compare with a canonical ensemble at the same mean

In a canonical description each mode has a geometric distribution. Match its mean to \(\bar n=Q/s\). A single mode then has variance \(\bar n(1+\bar n)\), and \(a\) independent modes have variance \(a\bar n(1+\bar n)\). Dividing the exact microcanonical variance by this canonical variance leaves \(b/(s+1)\). At the example point this is \(8/13\). Increasing \(a\) along with \(b\) at fixed fraction does not make a macroscopic subsystem independent of the reservoir; the ratio stays below one. Holding \(a\) fixed, taking \(b\) large and keeping \(Q/s\) fixed does recover the canonical width.

The negative-binomial canonical distribution has support on every nonnegative integer, even those above \(Q\). This is another sign that it describes another experiment. Merely deleting the tail without changing its other weights does not recover the true finite-reservoir conditional probability.

Calculate the distribution without enormous factorials

The ratio of successive unnormalised weights is \((q+a)(Q-q)/[(q+1)(Q-q+b-1)]\). Start with a positive arbitrary initial weight, march through the allowed \(q\) and normalise once. The graph compares this exact probability to a canonical law at the same mean. Adjust reservoir size and total energy; use the displayed first two moments to distinguish a shift in the average from a change in the width. The plotted canonical values cover the physically accessible count interval, so their displayed partial sum can be less than one.

Tutorial

If A and B contain identical mode counts, is \(Q/2\) necessarily the most likely observed value?

Work through the solution

The distribution is symmetric. For odd \(Q\) there is no integer value at exactly \(Q/2\); two equally likely central counts are possible. With one mode in each solid, every admissible value is equally likely. Symmetry fixes the mean exactly even when it does not identify a unique most probable observation.

For \(a=4,b=8,Q=24\) what variance would an unconditioned canonical fit predict?

Work through the solution

Here \(\bar n=2\). The canonical count variance is \(4(2)(3)=24\). Multiplying by \(8/13\) returns \(14.769\) for the closed pair. Count-to-energy conversion multiplies variances by \(\varepsilon^2\).

An experiment finds a noisy sum of the two reported energies. What can one infer before relaxing the energy-conservation model?

Work through the solution

In the ideal model \(E_A+E_B\) is constant. Check both readouts' measurement noise and energy calibration. Independently fluctuating detector errors add variance in the sum even for a closed sample. Record a reference configuration or estimate detector noise from repeated measurements of the same state.

The finite-reservoir chapter connects this exact count to the curvature of the entropy.

Recover a free-energy difference from a distribution of work

Counting, correlations and thermal measurements

A two-state system starts in equilibrium, then a control jumps its energy gap. Individual work readings differ although every run uses the same control protocol. Use the elementary model to derive an exponential work identity, compare it with the average work and estimate how many runs it takes to see the events that dominate the estimate.

Specify how work is measured

Set the lower energy to zero and the upper energy initially to \(\epsilon_i\). Prepare a canonical ensemble at inverse temperature \(\beta\), isolate it and suddenly change the gap to \(\epsilon_f\) without changing the energy eigenvectors. A run that started low stays low and requires zero work. A run that started high stays high and requires work \(\epsilon_f-\epsilon_i\), positive if the external control gives energy to the sample. No heat flows during the jump. Returning the system to equilibrium afterwards can exchange heat, but it is a separate part of the protocol.

The initial high-state probability is

\[p_i=e^{-\beta\epsilon_i}/(1+e^{-\beta\epsilon_i}).\]

Therefore

\[\langle e^{-\beta W}\rangle=(1-p_i)+p_i e^{-\beta(\epsilon_f-\epsilon_i)}=\frac{1+e^{-\beta\epsilon_f}}{1+e^{-\beta\epsilon_i}}=\frac{Z_f}{Z_i}.\]

Taking a negative logarithm and multiplying by \(1/\beta\) gives the equilibrium free-energy difference of two ensembles at the same temperature. The actual final population after the jump is still \(p_i\), which generally differs from the final equilibrium occupation. This distinction explains why an equilibrium state function can be recovered from a nonequilibrium final distribution.

Compare the two averages

The mean work is \(p_i(\epsilon_f-\epsilon_i)\). Convexity of the exponential gives

\[\langle W\rangle\ge\Delta F.\]

In units of \(k_BT\), take an initial gap of 4 and a final gap of 0. The high state occurs with probability 0.01799 and gives work −4 when it occurs. Mean work is about −0.07194, yet \(\Delta F=-\ln[2/(1+e^{-4})]\simeq-0.6750\). Both numbers are negative. The greater number is the one closer to zero. Checking only the sign of average work would not reveal the large difference.

Each rare upper-state event contributes \(e^4\) to the exponential average. Its probability times its weight is nearly one, comparable to the entire contribution of the common lower state. This is why replacing an average of exponentials by an exponential of an average loses essential information. Repeating the same protocol more slowly with contact to a bath would describe a different path through state space.

Ask what a finite dataset can support

For \(N\) independent preparations, the probability of seeing no initial upper state is \((1-p_i)^N\). With 20 runs in the example this is about 0.696. In such a dataset every measured work is zero and the empirical free-energy difference is zero. This is fully compatible with the model. To have at least a 95 percent chance of seeing even one upper-state event requires \(N\ge\ln(0.05)/\ln(1-p_i)\), or about 166 runs. Seeing only one such event still gives a noisy estimate of its frequency.

The graph holds the final gap at zero. Change the initial gap and ensemble size. It shows the probability of missing the rare branch at each sample size, together with the five-percent level. The reported exact free energy and mean work use all initial states. These predictions can be checked by explicitly enumerating a two-outcome histogram, without drawing a random sample and silently treating its fluctuations as a new law.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial

For an initial gap zero and a final gap \(2k_BT\), calculate \(\langle W\rangle\) and \(\Delta F\).

Work through the solution

Initially \(p_i=1/2\). The average work is \(k_BT\). The free-energy difference is \(k_BT\ln[2/(1+e^{-2})]\simeq0.5662 k_BT\). Some energy supplied by the control is unavailable as recoverable equilibrium free energy at this temperature.

A preparation procedure always starts the sample in its lower state independent of the specified initial gap. Can the same equality be used by changing only the sample count?

Work through the solution

No. The canonical initial probabilities were used algebraically to turn the weighted sum into \(Z_f/Z_i\). With deterministic preparation the exponential average is one in this protocol. Repeated measurements approach that different expectation.

At fixed initial and final gaps, what does reducing temperature do to the number of runs required to see an initial excitation?

Work through the solution

The excitation probability is approximately \(e^{-\beta\epsilon_i}\) for a large positive reduced gap. Required sample count grows approximately as \(1/p_i\) for any fixed detection probability. Controlling preparation and understanding tails become more important as temperature falls.

Connect this model to the partition function and to the role of free energy in a reversible isothermal process.

Applied study

Size a heat exchanger from energy balances and determine the temperature limits of the design.

Follow energy through an open system

A heat exchanger recovers energy from a warm process stream to preheat an incoming cold stream. A turbine also cools a stream, yet delivers shaft work. A steady-flow control volume distinguishes the mechanisms. Neglect changes in kinetic and potential energy and let heat into the control volume and shaft work out be positive. For one inlet and one outlet the first law reads \(\dot Q-\dot W=\dot m(h_{\rm out}-h_{\rm in})\), where \(h\) is specific enthalpy. For an ideal gas with constant specific heat, or a liquid stream with constant specific heat and negligible pressure change,

\[h_{\rm out}-h_{\rm in}=c_p(T_{\rm out}-T_{\rm in}).\]

Draw a boundary around an insulated exchanger containing both streams. With no shaft work, any heat given up by the hot stream must appear in the cold stream. Define heat-capacity rates \(C_h=\dot m_hc_{p,h}\) and \(C_c=\dot m_cc_{p,c}\), measured in W/K. The positive rate of internal heat transfer is

\[\dot Q_{\rm ex}=C_h(T_{h,i}-T_{h,o})=C_c(T_{c,o}-T_{c,i}).\]

The smaller capacity rate limits the possible transfer to \(\dot Q_{\max}=C_{\min}(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i})\). Energy balance alone gives one equation for the two outlet temperatures. The missing information is how much surface and thermal conductance connect the streams.

Derive the counterflow temperature profile

Let \(x\) increase in the hot-stream direction. The cold stream enters at the other end. Per unit length, a strip of wall of width \(w\) transfers \(Uw(T_h-T_c)\). Assume steady operation, constant overall coefficient \(U\), no external losses and negligible axial conduction. A slice balance gives

\[dT_h/dx=-Uw(T_h-T_c)/C_h,\]
\[dT_c/dx=-Uw(T_h-T_c)/C_c.\]

Both slopes are negative in this coordinate. A cold parcel warms as it moves towards decreasing \(x\).

Take \(C_h=C_{\min}\), \(C_r=C_h/C_c\) and \(\mathrm{NTU}=UA/C_h\), with transfer area \(A=wL\). Subtract the slice balances. The local temperature difference at \(L\) divided by its value at zero is \(\exp[-\mathrm{NTU}(1-C_r)]\). Substitute the two stream balances above to eliminate the outlet temperatures. The resulting effectiveness is

\[\varepsilon=\frac{\dot Q_{\rm ex}}{\dot Q_{\max}}=\frac{1-e^{-\mathrm{NTU}(1-C_r)}}{1-C_r e^{-\mathrm{NTU}(1-C_r)}}.\]

With equal capacity rates the difference is constant along the wall, and the limiting effectiveness is \(\mathrm{NTU}/(1+\mathrm{NTU})\). With a vanishing area there is no exchange. These limits provide simple checks on the algebra.

Worked example · Two water channels

Use \(T_{h,i}=350\) K, \(T_{c,i}=290\) K, \(C_h=0.0054\) W/K and \(C_c=0.0090\) W/K. The wall and fluid films together give \(U=90\) W/(m² K) over \(A=0.045(0.002)=9.0\times10^{-5}\) m². Thus \(C_r=0.60\), \(\mathrm{NTU}=1.50\), and the exponential factor is

\[e^{-0.60}=0.5488.\]

The effectiveness is 0.6727 and \(\dot Q_{\rm ex}=0.6727(0.0054)(60)=0.2180\) W.

The hot outlet is \(350-0.2180/0.0054=309.64\) K, and the cold outlet is \(290+0.2180/0.0090=314.22\) K. These outlets lie at opposite ends. The local hot-to-cold differences at the ends are \(350-314.22=35.78\) K and \(309.64-290=19.64\) K, both positive. For a body cooling in time against a reservoir, the same conductance enters a different energy balance. Study transient cooling of a solid.

Transfer problem · A compressor and turbine

In an ideal gas-turbine cycle, a compressor raises the pressure by a factor of 8, heat is supplied at constant pressure and the turbine expands the stream back to its starting pressure. Let air enter the compressor at 300 K and the turbine at 1200 K. Assume ideal-gas air with constant \(\gamma=1.4\) and \(c_p=1.005\) kJ/(kg K), reversible adiabatic compression and expansion, and negligible pressure losses. For either isentropic machine \(T_{\rm out}/T_{\rm in}=(p_{\rm out}/p_{\rm in})^{(\gamma-1)/\gamma}\).

The pressure factor raised to the temperature exponent is

\[8^{0.4/1.4}=1.811.\]

The compressor therefore discharges at 543.4 K and the turbine at 662.5 K. By the steady-flow first law the turbine delivers \(c_p(1200-662.5)=540.2\) kJ/kg of work, while compression requires \(c_p(543.4-300)=244.6\) kJ/kg. The net output is 295.6 kJ/kg. Dividing by the heat input \(c_p(1200-543.4)=659.9\) kJ/kg gives an ideal thermal efficiency of 44.8%. The exhaust is hotter than the compressed air by about 119 K, so those two streams offer an opportunity for recuperation before further heat is supplied.

Exercises

1. Size the exchanger at fixed flow

Keep both original capacity rates and \(U\) fixed. What area gives \(\varepsilon=0.80\)? How long would the 2 mm wide wall need to be?

Show the sizing calculation

Inverting the effectiveness formula gives

\[\mathrm{NTU}=\ln[(1-\varepsilon C_r)/(1-\varepsilon)]/(1-C_r)=\ln(2.6)/0.4=2.389.\]

Then

\[A=\mathrm{NTU}\,C_h/U=1.43\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2},\]
\[L=A/w=71.7\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The extra area supplies diminishing gains as the end temperatures approach one another.

2. Check the second law

For the original insulated exchanger assume negligible pressure drops and incompressible streams. Use the logarithmic entropy change of each stream to find the entropy-generation rate.

Show the second-law balance

At steady state \(\dot S_{\rm gen}=C_h\ln(T_{h,o}/T_{h,i})+C_c\ln(T_{c,o}/T_{c,i})\). The hot stream loses entropy and the cold stream gains more. Substitution of 309.638 K and 314.217 K gives \(6.02\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{W/K}\). The positive result is consistent with transfer through a finite temperature difference.

Run the heat-exchanger example at ntu=1.5, capacity_ratio=0.6 and inlet temperatures 350 and 290 K. Record both cross-section-averaged outlet temperatures and the power lost by the hot stream. In this code the geometry and conductance are fixed. Changing ntu changes the computed capacity rates and inlet speeds.

  1. Set ntu=3.0 at the original capacity ratio. Both flows halve. The one-dimensional model predicts an effectiveness of 0.853 and a hot outlet near 298.8 K. The heat rate should fall, despite the greater effectiveness, since there is now less hot water to cool every second.
  2. Restore ntu=1.5 and set capacity_ratio=0.3. The hot capacity stays fixed and the cold capacity doubles. Expect a lower cold outlet (about 303.1 K) and an increased heat rate (about 0.235 W). Check both energy balances against the numerical solution.

Problem sheet 6 · Recover an exchanger capacity rate from outlet data

Notation and assumptions

Read each defining equation with its assumptions and units, then follow the link to the argument that gives the equation its meaning.

Biot number

\[\mathrm{Bi}=hL_c/k\]

Here h is a surface heat-transfer coefficient and k is conductivity. A small Biot number supports a nearly uniform specimen temperature for the specified boundary condition.

Derivation · Thermal diffusion and transient surface cooling

Entropy generation

\[\dot S_{\rm gen}=\dot S_{\rm system}-\sum\dot Q_j/T_j-\dot S_{\rm mass,in}+\dot S_{\rm mass,out}\]

Use boundary temperatures at which heat actually crosses. All flows, storage and units must refer to the same control volume.

Derivation · Entropy, irreversibility and useful-work loss

Exergy destruction

\[\dot X_{\rm dest}=T_0\dot S_{\rm gen}\]

The environmental reference temperature enters in kelvin. Irreversibility destroys useful-work potential even when the total energy balance closes.

Derivation · Useful work and exergy destruction

Heat-exchanger effectiveness

\[\epsilon=\dot Q/[C_{\min}(T_{h,i}-T_{c,i})]\]

Heat-capacity rate is mass flow times specific heat for a single-phase stream. The smaller rate limits the maximum possible sensible heat transfer.

Derivation · Heat-exchanger effectiveness and finite capacity rates

Specific enthalpy

\[h=u+pv\]

Specific quantities are per unit mass. Enthalpy accounts for internal energy and flow work when mass passes through a control surface.

Derivation · Open systems, enthalpy and steady-flow devices

Vapour quality

\[x=m_v/(m_v+m_l)\]

This mass fraction applies in a two-phase equilibrium region. Mole fraction, mass fraction and fraction of volume occupied by vapour can be very different.

Derivation · Phase equilibrium and steam quality

Further reading

  • Trace states around the Rankine and Brayton cycles. Reconstruct the work and heat transfers from the property data before comparing cycle efficiencies. MIT · Thermodynamics and Propulsion

  • Extend the heat-exchanger work by separating wall, film and contact resistances. Identify the assumption that allows a single overall heat-transfer coefficient. Lienhard and Lienhard · A Heat Transfer Textbook
